<margin>
Because its appearance is equal to that of the Moon, that [specific war] does not occur. *Apasavya* <note>counter-clockwise or southern motion</note> is indeed recognized when it is perceived as such.
+ 3
</margin>

The knowledge of that—the knowledge of this planetary war—is established by me in the *Pañcasiddhāntikā* <note>The "Five Astronomical Treatises" by Varahamihira, c. 505 CE</note> according to the established systems <term>Siddhanta</term>. Some read this as "self-proven from the Siddhantas" <gloss>that is</gloss> from the *Suryasiddhanta* composed by the Sun-god or the sages, it is produced as the rise of the Siddhanta.

Now, he states the cause of the war: In the sky, the cause for those moving <note>the planets</note> is their position in paths one above the other. To the observer, because they are at an extreme distance, they appear as if they have reached a state of equality <note>conjunction</note> while wandering in their respective orbits. In this sequence of conjunction, four types of war are described by the sages Parashara and others, named: *Bheda* <note>occultation/piercing</note>, *Ullekha* <note>grazing</note>, *Anshumardana* <note>clashing of rays</note>, and *Asavya* <note>passing from the south</note>.

Of the planets and others moving in the sky, due to their positions being higher or lower in their own orbits according to their respective epicycles, they come into the range of vision. Because they are very far and not close to the observer, they appear to have attained equality. From this immediate proximity—meaning the first contact of their approach—a planetary pair is formed. That war is described as being of four types by the sages Parashara, Garga, Kashyapa, Vashishtha, and others. <gloss>known</gloss><insert>known</insert><gloss>by these</gloss><insert>these</insert> names: *Bheda*, *Ullekha*, and *Anshumardana*.

<margin>
Not by the one standing above, but by the one standing below...
That is fifteen.
2
</margin>

By the names *Anshumardana* and others, even a fourfold war appears as one. It is "equality": it means the lower planet is covered by the upper one. Wherever there is a war, a "union" or contact is made. That is *Ullekha* <note>grazing</note>. The rays are the *Anshu*; when the rays of those two planets are joined together, it is perceived as what is called *Anshumardana* <note>crushing of rays</note>. *Apasavya* is said to be "not toward the right" <note>counter-clockwise/southern</note>.

Standing all around to the left/south is called *Apasavya*. The Teacher <note>Varahamihira</note> has defined the characteristics of *Apasavya* in the conjunction of the Moon. Whatever result is taught by the Guru for the Moon when it stands to the north, that same result is understood for *Apasavya*. This is what is meant: whatever result occurs when the Moon is in the northern direction, the exact opposite result occurs in *Apasavya* when it is in the southern direction.

The son of the Rishi <note>Rishiputra</note> says the same thing: "When the Moon is to the south of the planets or constellations, it should be known as *Apasavya*; when to the north, it is *Pradakshina* <note>clockwise/northern</note>." Regarding the *Pradakshina* of the Moon, Garga says: "In the victory or defeat of the planets and in their resort to the constellations, how is <insert>the *Apasavya* common to all</insert> *Pradakshina* to be known, and likewise *Apasavya*? When the Moon is to the north of the constellations or planets..."

*Ullekha*, *Anshumardana*, and the middle... standing in the states of *Bheda*, *Ullekha*, and *Anshumardana* with their rays—these are the specifics. The fourfold war of the planets was spoken of by Parashara and other sages. Parashara and others say that because of the appearance...

<margin>
3
Of the constellation...
Or of the planets...
This *Ullekha*...
</margin>

...recorded by Garga, Kashyapa, and Vashishtha. By saying "Parashara and others," he shows the difference in the teachings of the sages. For instance, Rishiputra and others have described fifteen types of war. <gloss>Know this as auspicious for my happiness</gloss> The opinion of Parashara and the others is also the Teacher's own, which he mentions in various places in his own treatise, saying: "This is the opinion of Garga, Vashishtha, and Parashara." And therefore Parashara says: <insert>Namely, *Bheda* and equality...</insert>

It cites ancient authorities like Parashara and Garga to validate these definitions, particularly regarding the Moon's influence.</summary>

# Page 83

<warning>The manuscript edges are frayed and darkened. There are several marginal notes and interlinear additions in a smaller hand.</warning>

For example: through piercing, through grazing, through clashing of rays, and so on. The experts declare this to be of four types; among these, each preceding one is more significant than the next.

As the sage **Garga** says:
"Crying out, weeping, the crushing of rays, and the counter-clockwise movement <note>original: *apasavya*</note> of the planets—this is called the fourfold warfare."

And as the sage **Kashyapa** says:
"Among all the planets, those that move quickly are like thieves or dancers in their motion. Venus, the Sun, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn—these move slowly in order. These fast-movers and slow-movers, traveling through the divisions of time, come into conjunction when they occupy a single path. They stand one above the other, or appear to stay together. Piercing <note>original: *bheda*</note>, grazing <note>original: *ullekha*</note>, ray-crushing <note>original: *amshumarda*</note>, and the other is counter-clockwise <note>original: *apasavya*</note>. This fourfold conjunction is the warfare of those who move in two ways."

Thus, the counter-clockwise motion is described in relation to the higher elevation of another planet. Now, he describes the fourfold results for each of the four types of warfare:

In **Piercing**, there is the destruction of rain and the split <note>original: *bheda*</note> of friends and great families. In **Grazing**, there is fear of weapons and conflict among ministers, and no dear ones remain. In the **Obstruction of Rays**, there are wars and the ruin of kings.

<margin>83</margin> <margin>Cruel</margin> <term>Weeping and Crushing: <note>Specific types of planetary conflict resulting in suffering.</note></term> In warfare and in counter-clockwise motion, these happen to kings and those without kingdoms. In the war known as **Piercing**, there is the destruction of rain (the loss of the monsoon), and a mutual split occurs among friends, allies, and the heads of great families. In the war known as **Grazing**, there is fear of weapons, conflict among ministers (discord between secretaries), and no dear ones—meaning there is fear of famine. In the war known as **Obstruction of Rays**, wars occur between kings, and there is a crushing of enemies. The word "crushing" <note>original: *marda*</note> is applied to each: there is crushing by weapons and crushing by anger. These people <margin>ya</margin> suffer the heat of the king; "crushing" means extreme pain.

Just as in the war of counter-clockwise motion, it is not only in the "Obstruction of Rays" that the ruin of kings occurs; rather, in the **Counter-clockwise** war, wars between kings certainly occur. There is nothing additional in the counter-clockwise war that is not in Piercing or Grazing. Since Piercing, Grazing, and Ray-crushing are understood as specific types of conjunction and motion, only three types of war should be spoken of.

To this, it is replied: When a planet is situated in the southern direction, its signs of defeat are: roughness, trembling, discoloration, being "uncovered," being "overtaken," being "crooked," or "turning back." When situated in the northern direction, the signs of victory are: glossiness and largeness. Even when situated in the south, if a planet possesses signs of victory such as largeness, <margin>82</margin> then it is considered the winner. When the southern one is the winner, what then of the northern one? <margin>cha</margin>

The result is not just based on direction. When the rays are destroyed, the result is the opposite. In the counter-clockwise motion, everything is contrary. This logic applies to the results of the planet that is <gloss>2. fallen, 3. abandoned, or conquered</gloss>. Thus, in the war known as Counter-clockwise, the result is calculated through this reversal. According to the teacher **Bhatta**, the cruel southern <margin>82</margin> <margin>+ and Teacher Bhatta says</margin> result is calculated by reversal <gloss>2. fallen, 3. abandoned, or conquered</gloss> in the order of the northern planets' orbits.

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of planetary "warfare" (yuddha) and the relative positioning of celestial bodies, specifically focusing on the distinction between clockwise and counter-clockwise movements.</meta>

# The Conflict: Concerning the Counter-Clockwise Position
---
<gloss>Veru Meru <note>Likely a reference to the sacred mountain Meru as the cosmic axis</note></gloss> ->In the 4th and 5th directions<-

The Gods, situated in the north, behold the northern half of the terrestrial globe <term>Bhugola</term> moving clockwise <gloss>indeed</gloss> from bottom to top; likewise, those at the southern pole, where the <term>Vadavamukha</term> <note>The "Mare's Mouth," a mythical submarine fire at the south pole</note> is located, see the other half. Due to these various perspectives of the ancient Teachers, the minds of dull-witted students became confused and their thoughts fragmented. Therefore, the Teacher, having conceptualized a fourth type of planetary conflict named "Counter-clockwise" <term>Apasavya</term>, has declared the results. This has been stated by many, such as Parashara and others. In your own wisdom, you might ask: if the result for a planet situated in the north is the same as the result for one situated in the south—which is called "Counter-clockwise"—then why is this stated differently here? The Teacher has defined the "Clockwise" <term>Savya</term> and "Counter-clockwise" <term>Apasavya</term> positions by a strict rule. As he said: When a <term>Gandharvanagara</term> <note>A "City of the Gandharvas," referring to an optical illusion or mirage in the sky</note> appears, white like heat-tempered silver, accompanied by lightning strikes and sudden winds, it portends danger from enemies for the King; however, victory is assured if it is on the clockwise side. If it stands on the left side, there is fear of the enemy; if on the right, there is victory—in common worldly usage, the left side is called "clockwise" <note>The text here explores the technical reversal of directions in celestial observation</note>. Thus, the Holy Vyasa said: "To one fighting while facing the East, the Sun is on the right; this Sushena of mine is in front; by moving counter-clockwise, you should go to the south, O Honorable One." And so says Badarayana: "There too, the right eye is the Sun and the left is the Moon; if on the left, there is fear of the enemy, if on the right, victory." This is said to be the half-rotation. Regarding the position on the earth, it is otherwise. Even if one has gone to the eastern region, a <term>Shakuna</term> <note>An omen, often a bird</note> situated in the northern direction that circles toward the south is called "Circumambulatory" <term>Pradakshina</term> or "Clockwise" <term>Savya</term>. The Teacher says there is no difference between clockwise and counter-clockwise when it is to the south. Situated in the three divisions between the directions of Indra <note>East</note> and Agni <note>Southeast</note>, those who are treasurers, or who live by fire, or are engaged in austerities, move clockwise, and so on.

The Teacher Aryabhata has spoken of the movements of clockwise and counter-clockwise; therefore, why is it also spoken of regarding the planets? Those who dwell at the base of the clouds <note>terrestrial inhabitants</note> see the omens, the Gemini constellation, and the Sun just as those who dwell at the <term>Vadavamukha</term> <note>the South Pole</note> do. Regarding this, the Teacher said: Because the Sun rises and travels toward the North, the Immortals <note>the Gods at the North Pole</note> see it moving upward, not downward. Witnessing this, the Teacher Aryabhata accepted the human perspective. In all omens, when something comes from the two left sides and crosses to the right side, that is "Circumambulatory" or "Clockwise." The opposite—moving from the right side to the left side—is "Counter-clockwise." On the left, there is fear of the enemy; on the right, victory. It is shown by the Teachers that victory comes through the clockwise movement. Similarly, for a journey, people desire the clockwise position. If the counter-clockwise is the opposite of the left, then how is it understood that "on the left there is fear of the enemy"? It is explained thus: by the distinction between direct <term>Anuloma</term> and retrograde <term>Pratiloma</term> motion, the different results of the planets are shown. There, the result is the same whether they are moving directly or retrogradely to the south...

Page 84

<note>The text begins by discussing the results of planetary friction or overlapping.</note>
...makes mention of the subsequent effect. In that case, the result is the same; thus, whether the friction <term>friction: "vimarda," referring to planets appearing to touch</term> occurs in the natural order or the reverse order, there is no difference in the result. These are the three types of planetary configurations. Whatever result is ascribed to "crushing" <term>crushing: "upamardana"</term> is the same result for the configuration known as "counter-clockwise" <term>counter-clockwise: "apasavya"</term>. This is what is meant: whatever result is declared for a planet situated to the north of another, that same result applies to a planet in a "counter-clockwise" state when it is victorious. Therefore, the designation of "counter-clockwise" is not considered distinct in its effects.

### Definitions of the Crier, the City-Dweller, and the Attacker
Now, he explains the technical names of the planets as "Crier" <term>Crier: "ākranda," a king who is an ally or one who calls for help</term>, "Attacker" <term>Attacker: "yāyi," a king on the march or an invader</term>, and "City-Dweller" <term>City-Dweller: "paura," a king defending his own city</term>:

->**The Sun is the Crier when in the middle; he is the City-Dweller when situated among City-Dwellers; he is the Attacker when among Attackers. Mercury, Jupiter, and Saturn are known as City-Dwellers. The Moon is the Crier. Ketu, Mars, Rahu, and Venus are the Attackers. These are the certainties regarding the planets. When the Crier, Attacker, or City-Dweller planets are victorious, they bring victory to the kings of their respective classes.**<-

The Sun, when in the middle of the sky at midday, receives the name "Crier." As long as the living planets are situated to the west, he is the Crier because he follows them. The very same Sun is the "City-Dweller" or "Citizen" <term>Citizen: "nāgara"</term> during the first third of the day (morning). In the final third of the day (afternoon), he becomes the "Attacker." An Attacker is one who goes forth; he moves with effort. Mercury, Jupiter, and Saturn are always "City-Dwellers" or "Urbanites" at all times. The Moon is always the "Crier." Ketu <note>the descending node/comet</note>, Mars, Rahu <note>the ascending node</note>, and Venus are the "Attackers." Ketu is "the crested one," Mars is "the glowing coal," Rahu is "the son of Simhika," and Venus is "the descendant of Bhrigu." These planets are the Attackers; this is what the sages declare. When the Crier, Attacker, or City-Dweller planets are struck or defeated, they destroy the kings of those categories. When they are victorious, they grant victory to their own class.

He further specifies:
**When a City-Dweller is defeated by a City-Dweller, the City-Dweller planets destroy the City-Dweller kings. The same applies to the Attackers and Criers. This is also true for the conflict between the Citizen and the Attacker.**
When City-Dwellers are conquered by other City-Dwellers, the City-Dweller kings destroy the City-Dweller kings. In this same manner, the Attackers and Criers destroy one another, and the Citizen and Attacker planets, if defeated, destroy one another. As **Parashara** <note>an ancient sage and astronomer</note> says: "From their victory comes victory; from their defeat, slaughter; from their division, division; and from their equality, stability."

### Characteristics of the Defeated Planet
Now he describes the signs of a defeated planet:
**The one situated in the southern direction, who is flickering, rough, retreating from the start, small, mounted upon, distorted, lusterless, and discolored—that one is the defeated.**
Whichever planet is situated in the southern direction (the left side in the sky) is considered "sick." It is flickering (trembling), rough, and "retreating from the start," meaning it has entered into a reverse or retrograde motion. It is small, "mounted upon" (overshadowed by another), has undergone a distortion of its natural appearance, is devoid of brilliance, and has lost its natural color. Such a planet is "defeated."

As **Parashara** says: "One should know the defeated planet by ten characteristics: discolored, rough, small, staying in the southern path, struck down, motionless, deformed, overtaken in speed, retreating from the start, and trembling." **Garga** <note>another foundational sage of Indian astronomy</note> says the same...

<summary>This page defines the three functional categories of planets (Crier, Attacker, and City-Dweller) based on their identity and the Sun's position, and lists the ten physical criteria used to determine which planet has lost a celestial "battle."</summary>
<keywords>planetary war, Yuddha, Akranda, Paura, Yayi, Parashara, Garga, planetary defeat, Indian astronomy, omens</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of planetary phenomena, specifically focusing on the criteria for determining victory and defeat in 'Planetary War' (Graha Yuddha).</meta>

Cloud-streaked, dark, harsh, and rough; moving in an anticlockwise direction <note>original: "apasavyagato"</note>, or appearing within the retrograde arc; fallen from its natural position, or turned back; lusterless, distorted, or struck by a high velocity; appearing in the west or appearing parched—with these seventeen characteristics, a planet is declared defeated in war.

## Now, the characteristics of the Victor are described:

The victor is to be identified by characteristics opposite to those mentioned above: expansive, glossy, and very bright. Even if located in the southern direction, it is endowed with victory. <margin>A planet in the southern direction is usually considered weak, but other strengths can override this.</margin> Therefore, one endowed with the opposite signs—such as being in the northern direction, without trembling, having a full disk, being expansive, situated above, undistorted, and radiant—is the victor. As **Garga** <note>An ancient sage and authority on Indian astronomy/astrology</note> says: "The one who is lustrous, endowed with rays, clear, shining like silver, and appearing large—that one becomes the victor upon meeting." One who is superior in brilliance and stands in his own power should be known as the victor in a planetary conjunction. Such a planet is to be declared the winner. Furthermore, a planet that is expansive in its orb, clear, and possessed of beauty and splendor, even if situated in the southern direction, is still the victor; this is frequently possible for **Venus** <note>original: "Shukra"</note>. Likewise, the teacher **Pulisha** states: "Among all victors, **Mercury** <note>original: "Budha"</note> is the victor even when in the southern direction."

## Now, regarding Conjunctions of Equal Strength:

When both planets are endowed with rays, are expansive, and glossy during a meeting, then there is mutual affection <note>a "friendly" conjunction</note>; if they are the opposite, it is a conflict. If both are bristling with a web of rays, expansive, and clear during their union, then in that meeting there is mutual love between them. <insert>According to the rule of 'the sun, the cry, and the middle,' their union is auspicious.</insert> If those same two are the opposite—devoid of rays, small, and harsh during the meeting—then they bring destruction to their own sides. Here, a specific detail is added: whether it is a "war" or a "conjunction," if the characteristics are mixed, the results on earth must be declared as middling. "War" refers to the interaction of the five planets starting with **Mars** <note>original: "Bhauma"</note>, while "Conjunction" refers to their meeting with the **Moon**. If the signs of war or conjunction are not clearly one or the other—meaning a state of war exists but without a clear victor or loser—such as when both are ray-clothed and expansive but situated in unfavorable positions, it is not known who is conquered and who is the victor. In a conjunction of planets with the Moon, if it moves rapidly or moves to the south, the results are auspicious. This is what is meant: if the Moon moves to the north or south, or moves through the middle, then the specific results of that "piercing" must be told, not just the general results of a conjunction. In the same way, the effects seen on earth for kings should be assigned according to these characteristics: in victory, victory is to be predicted; in defeat, defeat. Thus, by the Moon's northward movement, good fortune is to be declared; by the southern, misfortune; and by the middle, a middling result.

## Now, the results of Mars being defeated by all other planets:

When defeated by **Jupiter**, the women of the king... <note>The text breaks off here</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of planetary warfare (Graha-yuddha) and the resulting earthly omens, specifically detailing the consequences when Mars, Mercury, and Jupiter are defeated by other planets.</meta>

<margin>
45
85
</margin>

...the people of Salwa <note>original: "Sahlah"</note> are oppressed. When Mars is defeated by Saturn <note>original: "Saura"</note>, the citizens are victorious, but the common subjects suffer; the granaries are destroyed and those who live in penance <note>ascetics</note> are consumed by hardship. When Jupiter <note>original: "Guru/Brihaspati"</note> defeats Mars <note>original: "Avanisuta/Angaraka"</note>, the people of Bahlika <note>Bactria</note>, those traveling or wishing to travel, those distressed by fire, goldsmiths, and servants are oppressed and suffer great heat. When Mercury <note>original: "Shashija/Budha"</note> defeats Mars, the commanders of armies, the people of Kalinga, and the Salwas are oppressed. When Saturn <note>original: "Sanaishchara"</note> defeats Mars, the city-dwellers and kings achieve victory, but the subjects sink into despair. When Venus <note>original: "Shukra"</note> defeats Mars, there is distress and burning heat for the overseers of granaries, village leaders, and those performing great sacrifices.

Now he describes the results of Mercury being <term>defeated by all other planets</term>: When Mars strikes Mercury, the trees, rivers, ascetics, the Ashvaka people, kings, those living in the North, and those initiated into Vedic rituals suffer distress. When Jupiter defeats Mercury, the <gloss>barbarians</gloss> <note>original: "Mleccha" - often referring to non-Vedic or foreign tribes</note>, the wealthy, the city-dwellers, the people of Trigarta, and those living in the mountains are oppressed, and the earth trembles. When Saturn defeats Mercury, the trees, rivers, ascetics, the Ashvaka mountain-dwellers, kings, those living in the North, and those who have taken vows of truth suffer oppression. When Jupiter <note>original: "Brihaspati"</note> defeats Mercury, the <gloss>common folk</gloss>, the elderly, thieves, the wealthy, city-dwellers, the Trigartas, and mountain-dwellers are all oppressed and suffer. The earth <note>original: "Mahibhrut"</note> shakes and moves. When Saturn defeats Mercury, boatmen, fishermen, warriors, those bearing weapons, the people of Shrughna, those born of water, and pregnant women are all oppressed. When Venus <note>original: "Bhrigu"</note> defeats Mercury, there is an outbreak of fire, and the destruction of crops, clouds, and those traveling on the road.

Now he describes the results of Jupiter <note>original: "Jiva"</note> being defeated by all other planets: When Venus defeats Jupiter, the <gloss>Kuluta</gloss> people, the Gandharas, Kaikayas, Madras, Matsyas, Vatsas, and Vangas, as well as the cattle and crops, are destroyed. When Mars strikes Jupiter, the kings of the Middle Country <note>original: "Madhyadesha"</note> and the cows suffer; the Arjunayanas, Yaudheyas, Shibis, and Brahmins do not dwell in peace. When Mercury <note>original: "Shashitanaya"</note> defeats Jupiter, those bearing weapons and the Middle Country come to ruin. When Venus defeats Jupiter, the <gloss>Kuluta</gloss> people, Gandharas, Kaikayas, Madras, Matsyas, Vatsas, Vangas, the cows, and the grain crops are destroyed. When Mars <note>original: "Angaraka"</note> strikes Jupiter, the Arjunayanas, Yaudheyas, Shibis, and Brahmins are all oppressed. When Mercury defeats Jupiter—the word "also" implies—the <gloss>barbarians</gloss>, those who speak the truth, those who bear weapons, and the Middle Country all go to destruction. Furthermore, the power of Jupiter is lost, and all go to ruin.

Now, the results of Venus being defeated by all other planets...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of planetary warfare (Graha-yuddha) and the specific mundane effects (omens) resulting from the defeat of one planet by another. The focus here is on the consequences when Venus (Shukra) and Saturn (Sauri) are defeated.</meta>

The author now describes the results:

When **Venus is defeated by Jupiter**, the superior "marcher" <note>The 'Yayi' or the attacking king/leader in a conflict</note> meets his destruction. There is conflict between the Brahmins and the Kshatriyas <note>The priestly and warrior classes</note>, and Indra <note>The god of rain</note> withholds his waters. The peoples of Kosala, Kalinga, Vanga, Vatsa, and Matsya, along with those of the Middle Country (Madhyadesha), suffer distress, as do eunuchs and the people of Shurasena.

When **Venus is defeated by Mars**, the commander-in-chief of the army is killed, and great battles occur between kings.

When **Venus is defeated by Mercury**, the mountain-dwellers are destroyed, the milk of cows fails, and there is very little rain.

When **Venus is defeated by Saturn**, the leaders of guilds, those who live by the sword <note>soldiers or mercenaries</note>, the people of Kacha, and those born of the water <note>aquatic life or water-dependent trades</note> suffer. This is the specific result; other general effects regarding planetary devotion should be considered elsewhere.

->**Commentary on the above:**<-

<term>When Venus is defeated by Jupiter</term>: The superior leader or "marcher" meets his end or reaches destruction. Likewise, there is trouble and opposition between the Brahmins and the Kshatriyas. Indra (the lord of rain) abandons the water, meaning it does not rain. Furthermore, the people of Kosala, Kalinga, Vanga, Vatsa, and Matsya—these together with the Middle Country—attain suffering. Similarly, eunuchs and the Shurasena people suffer.

<term>When Venus is defeated by Mars</term>: There is the killing or death of the chief of the forces (the General). Battles and wars occur between sovereigns.

When **Venus is defeated by Mercury** (the Son of the Moon): Those living in the mountains go to their destruction, the milk of cows is destroyed, and there is certainly a scarcity of rain.

<term>When Venus is defeated by Saturn</term> (the Son of the Sun): The heads of corporations/guilds, those whose livelihood is weapons, the Kacha tribe, and water-born creatures are oppressed and undergo pain. This is the specific result; however, the general effects of planetary influence mentioned elsewhere are also suppressed or modified by this.

Now he describes the results for **Saturn** when it is defeated:

When **Saturn is defeated by Venus**, there is a lack of rain, and distress for sages, birds, and men of great pride.

When **Saturn is defeated by Mars**, the people of Tangana, Andhra, Odra, Dashaya, and Vahlika suffer in their respective countries.

When **Saturn is defeated by Mercury**, the people of Anga, merchants (those living by buying and selling), birds, animals (four-legged creatures), and elephants are tormented.

When **Saturn is defeated by Jupiter**, those countries where women are numerous, as well as the Marishaka and Shaka peoples, are tormented and suffer.

He states a specific rule here:

This specific result has been declared for the planets **Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, and Saturn** when they are defeated. However, the final prediction should be made by considering the "planetary devotion" <note>Bhakti: the specific regions, peoples, and objects assigned to each planet</note>; as the planets are assigned their respective devotions, so the results follow.

<term>Mars (Kuja), Mercury (Jna), Jupiter (Vagisha), Venus (Sita), and Saturn (Asita)</term>: This specific rule is stated for these planets when they are conquered. Any other results should be spoken of according to the planetary "bhakti" (correspondences)...

It emphasizes that while general rules exist, the specific impact depends on the regions and classes traditionally governed by each planet.</summary>

Those defeated in such a manner, or in that same way, clearly manifest in the middle; or, it is said, they destroy the strength of the devotee. And so says Parashara:
> When the planets are in the regions of the winds <note>original: "marut-syadeśāḥ"</note>, they create distress; but when they have attained their full strength, they are conducive to victory, especially from the fourth house onwards.

### // Now the chapter on "Conjunctions" is explained. //

At the very beginning of this, he describes the characteristics of the Moon's motion:
> If the Moon passes to the north of the stars, as far as possible, it is considered a "circumambulation" <note>A respectful clockwise passing</note> and is auspicious for kings; if the Moon passes to the south, it is not auspicious.

The Moon <note>original: "śaśāṅkaḥ"</note> goes to the north of the stars as far as possible. The phrase "as far as possible" indicates those stars that are near. For those stars whose latitude is equal to or less than the Moon's latitude, this is generally possible—such as Krittika, Rohini, Pushya, Magha, Chitra, Vishakha, Anuradha, Jyeshtha, Shatabhisha, and Revati. For others, it occurs only occasionally. For those far away, its passage between them is not possible, such as Svati, Shravana, and Dhanishtha. In these directions, Svati is at the twenty-seventh degree of northern latitude; the Moon's latitude is four and a half degrees. Therefore, the Moon always passes to the south of it. For those stars whose northern latitude is greater than the Moon's latitude, the Moon always passes to their south. For those whose southern latitude is greater than the Moon's latitude, the Moon always passes to their north. This should also be applied to Mars and the other planets as far as possible. Where it is possible for them, it should be applied. If the Moon passes to the north of the planets like Mars, having performed a "circumambulation," that clockwise motion is beneficial and brings welfare to kings. By passing to the left, or the south, it is not auspicious, meaning it does not grant well-being. This should also be applied to the planets as far as possible. And so say the sons of Vasishtha:
> Passing to the south is considered "counter-clockwise," while passing to the north is "circumambulation." This should be known for the Moon in relation to the planets and the stars alike.

And so says the Elder Garga:
> When the Moon is to the north of the stars or the planets, they call that "circumambulation," which leads to abundance and good rain. When it proceeds to the south of the stars or the planets, that is "counter-clockwise," which is a sign of lack of rain and fear.

<margin>86</margin> <insert>Extremely</insert> Likewise, he describes the result when the Moon moves to the north of Mars <note>original: "aṅgāraka"</note>:
> If the Moon passes to the north of Mars, the mountain-dwellers, the powerful, and the warrior class <note>Kshatriyas</note> become joyful and ready for expeditions, and the Earth becomes prosperous.

If the Moon passes <insert>extremely</insert> to the north of Mars <note>original: "kuja"</note>, then there is victory for the mountain-dwellers (those living in the mountains) and the powerful (those endowed with strength). Likewise, the warriors, along with those on expeditions, become delighted and happy. Furthermore, the Earth becomes delighted and happy with the growth of much grain.

Now he speaks of Mercury:
> If the Moon is to the north of the Son of the Sun <note>Mercury; original: "khasuta"</note>, it brings victory to the citizens and kings, creates abundance, causes the accumulation of crops, and brings joy to the people and a collection of wealth for the lords of men.

The Moon, if it goes to the north of its own son, Mercury, then it leads to victory for the citizens and the kings. It creates abundance (is an abundance-maker).

<summary>This page concludes the chapter on planetary warfare and begins the chapter on conjunctions (Samagama), specifically focusing on the Moon's passage relative to stars and planets. It establishes that a northern passage (circumambulation) is auspicious for kings and agriculture, while a southern passage is ill-omened.</summary>
<keywords>Bhattotpala, astronomy, Moon, Mars, Mercury, planetary conjunctions, circumambulation, auspicious signs, agriculture, kingship</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the astrological discourse on planetary conjunctions with the Moon, specifically focusing on the effects of the Moon passing to the north or south of various planets. It maintains the technical style of Sanskrit astronomical commentary (Jyotisha).</meta>

It happens, and thus there is an increase in crops, an increase in the population, and for the lords of men—the kings—it increases their treasuries and storehouses.

### On the Conjunction with Jupiter
When the Moon passes to the north of Jupiter <note>Sanskrit: *Bṛhaspati*</note>, there is prosperity for citizens, the twice-born <note>the priestly class or Brahmins</note>, warriors, and scholars. There is growth for religion and for the Middle Country <note>Madhyadesha, the central heartland of ancient India</note>, and the subjects are said to be delighted. When the Moon is situated in the northern direction relative to Jupiter: for the citizens, the Brahmins, the ruling classes, and the learned scholars, as well as for the Middle Country, there is growth and an abundance of food <term>Subhiksha: a state where food is easily obtained</term>. Likewise, all subjects become contented and satisfied.

### On the Conjunction with Venus
Now he speaks of Venus <note>Sanskrit: *Shukra* or *Bhargava*</term>. If the Moon passes to the north of Venus, there is an increase in treasuries, elephants, and horses; there is victory for those on the march and for archers; and an abundance of crops is also quickly foretold. When the Moon passes to the north of Venus, it bestows growth upon the ministers' storehouses, the elephants, and the horses. Likewise, victory comes to the archers and those seeking conquest <note>Sanskrit: *vijigīṣu*</note>. An abundance of crops and wealth from alms is also produced.

### On the Conjunction with Saturn
Now he speaks of the son of the Sun <note>Saturn, or *Shani*</note>. If the Moon should pass to the right <note>clockwise/north</note> of Saturn, there is victory for those such as the lords of cities; the Shakas, Vahlikas, Sindhus, and Pahlavas <note>various historical ethnic groups/tribes</note> become filled with joy. If the Moon performs a northern passage relative to Saturn, there is victory for the kings who dwell in cities. Furthermore, the Shaka people, the Vahlikas, the Saindhavas, and the Pahlavas—all these, along with the Greeks <term>Yavanas: originally referring to Ionians/Greeks, later to foreigners from the West</term>—attain great joy and become highly delighted.

### Specific Rules on Planetary Conjunctions
He states a specific rule here: For whichever stars or planets the Moon <note>literally "the ice-rayed one"</note> passes to the north without affliction, it brings prosperity to their respective devotees, cities, and regions. But if it passes to the south, it destroys them. For those stars and planets (Mars and others), if the Moon rises to the north and travels without causing distress, it brings nourishment to the cities and the traveling men associated with those planets, as well as to the specific countries said to be ruled by those planets. However, by a southern passage, it strikes and destroys those very same cities and regions.

He states another specific rule here: Whatever fruit is seen when the Moon is in the north, the exact opposite occurs when it is in the south. This conjunction of the Moon with the stars and planets has been proclaimed; however, there is no actual "war" <note>Sanskrit: *yuddha*</note> between the Moon and the planets. If the Moon travels to the south, all the results mentioned for the northern passage are reversed. These conjunctions of the Moon with the stars and planets are thus described. The word "certainly" is used for emphasis: there is no planetary combat between the Moon and Mars or the other planets <note>unlike the "war" that can occur between two star-like planets</note>.

<summary>This page details the auspicious effects of the Moon passing north of Jupiter, Venus, and Saturn, predicting prosperity for specific social classes and ethnic groups like the Greeks and Scythians. It establishes the general rule that northern passages bring growth while southern passages bring destruction, noting that the Moon does not engage in "planetary war."</summary>

<keywords>Astrology, Moon, Jupiter, Venus, Saturn, Shakas, Yavanas, Madhyadesha, planetary war, agriculture, kingship</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues Bhattotpala’s commentary on Varahamihira’s Brhat Samhita, transitioning from the chapter on planetary conjunctions to the chapter on the effects of the planetary years.</meta>

<page-num>87</page-num>

...with the winds. Now, where a "conflict" <note>original: *yuddha*</note> occurs with the Sun and the wind, it is called a "meeting" or "union" <note>original: *samagama*</note> in the three periods of the day. A meeting with the Moon is also called a "union," while the interaction of Mars and the other planets is called a "conflict" <note>original: *yuddha*</note>. This is the three-fold principle of the scriptures. As the teacher Vishnuchandra says: "A disappearance caused by the Sun is a 'union'; for the sons of the Cold-Rayed One (Mercury) and the sons of the Earth (Mars) and others, it is called a 'conflict,' except when they are in their exaltation." Some have said that in the victory or defeat of the Sun, the opinions of Galava should be cited.

Thus ends the eighteenth chapter, titled "The Chapter on Unions," in the *Samhita-vritti* composed by Bhattotpala.

### The Chapter on the Effects of the Planetary Years
Now, the <term>Chapter on the Effects of the Planetary Years</term> <note>original: *Graha-varsha-phala-adhyaya*</note> is explained. What kind of good or bad results occur in which specific year? The teachers previously promised that after observing the results of the year and the month as prescribed by the Sages, one should apply them to the specific day and year. First, he describes the results of the Sun's year:

# Everywhere the forests are sparse of grass, filled with wild beasts, predators, and tusked animals. The rivers do not flow with much water. Medicines for diseases are not very potent or effective.
# The Son of Aditi (the Sun) shines intensely even during the cold season. The clouds, though looking like moving mountains, do not release water. The groups of stars and the Moon have lost their luster. The herds of deer suffer, distressed by the heat.

Regarding this:
In the year, month, or day belonging to the Sun <note>the Day-Maker</note>, such results occur. As the Lord of the Greeks <note>Yavanesvara</note> says: "Whatever characteristics are attributed to the lords of the winds, the same should be understood for the months, days, and seasons, determined by the position of their lord." Thus, the results for the year, month, day, and hour should be understood. In that year, month, or day, what are the results? Everywhere, the forests are sparse of grass and are joined or filled with wild animals. What kind of tusked animals? Those who are driven by fate <note>original: *daiva*</note> and desire to eat. By the word "fate," past actions are meant. As it is said:
> "The actions performed in a previous birth, whether good or bad, are designated by the word 'Fate' in this birth by the wise."

Therefore, the forests are filled with tusked animals wishing to eat due to fate. The rivers <note>streams</note> do not flow with abundant water. The medicines—substances used for the pacification of diseases—are not "so," meaning they are not endowed with great strength or potency; they do not properly cure diseases. Furthermore, the Son of Aditi, the <term>Sun</term>, shines very fiercely and intensely even in the cold season <note>the months of Magha and Phalguna</note>. The clouds, though resembling mountains, do not release much water. The sky is such that the luster of the stars and the Moon is destroyed—meaning the groups of constellations have lost their brilliance.

<summary>This page concludes the discussion on planetary conjunctions and begins Chapter 19, detailing the astrological omens and environmental effects occurring during a year, month, or day ruled by the Sun, characterized by drought and predatory wildlife.</summary>
<keywords>Bhattotpala, Sun's year, planetary conflict, astrology, Yavanesvara, environmental omens, fate, medicinal potency</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical commentary on planetary conjunctions (Yogas) and their mundane astrological effects, specifically referencing the works of Varahamihira (Samasa-Samhita) and the sage Kashyapa.</meta>

Regarding the chapter beginning with "Society" and others: Yama refers to Saturn <note>original: Saurah</note> and Jiva refers to Jupiter <note>original: Guru</note>. When there is a meeting or conjunction <note>Sangama/Samyoga</note> of these two, and if any other planet also joins them, that is the configuration known as **Treasure** <note>original: Kosha-yoga</note>. 

When one planet has risen in the west—having emerged from the solar disc—and another, a second one, has risen in the east—also having emerged from the solar disc—and they both appear on the same day, that is the configuration known as **Collision** <note>original: Sannipata-yoga</note>. The absence of these specific positions is to be understood as the **Coming Together** <note>original: Samagama</note>. 

In that "Coming Together," if all the star-planets <note>the non-luminary planets: Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, and Saturn</note> are of unaltered appearance, with undistorted bodies, and are glossy, clear, large, and expansive, they are auspicious and bring blessings. Otherwise, they are inauspicious.

As it is stated in the *Summary Compilation* <note>original: Samasa-Samhita</note>:
"The configurations are Treasure, Collision, Whirlpool <note>Samvarta</note>, Coming Together, and Society. Delusion <note>Sammoha</note> is also mentioned; these are the arrivals of the end-makers..." 

When there is a union of the Son of the Sun <note>Saturn</note> and Jupiter, and both are joined by another, it is a "Coming Together"; otherwise, it becomes a "Collision." The "Treasure" configuration brings the fear of famine to the world. When one has risen in the west and another in the east, or if they strike each other with their rays, that is called "Collision." When a city-dwelling planet <note>Pauras - planets in certain stationary or slow states</note> is joined by a transit planet <note>Yayina - moving planets</note>, or when two such planets meet, that is designated as the configuration of **Society** <note>original: Samaja</note>. 

Now, if four or five transit planets should meet on a single day, it is a **Whirlpool** <note>original: Samvarta</note>, which is like the fire at the end of an era. Otherwise, it is a "Coming Together," as was said previously.

<margin>Root Text</margin> Those planets that are bright, pleasant, with stretched rays, turning to the right, very glossy, and of clear body—they bring safety and abundance of food.

Now he describes the results of these:
"The two known as Whirlpool and Coming Together, and Delusion and Treasure, bring fear to the subjects. In the one named Society, there are good years <note>or 'evenness'</note> predicted; but in Collision, there is indeed the provocation of hostility."

The sense is that the two configurations called Whirlpool and Coming Together are neutral; they do not produce inauspicious results. Whatever conditions existed before remain the same during these two. Delusion and Treasure bring fear to the subjects (the people). In the configuration named Society, "good years" <note>Susama</note> are predicted, meaning things become beautiful/prosperous. Some read the plural "good years," meaning the conditions that existed before become even better. In Collision, there is "provocation of hostility," meaning people become mutual enemies; the word "indeed" <note>khalu</note> is used here to indicate traditional authority.

So says the sage **Kashyapa**:
"Whirlpool and Coming Together are middling; Delusion is remembered as bringing fear. Treasure gives undesirable results, while the one called Society is quite moderate. In Collision, great hostility arises between one another."

And likewise in the *Summary Compilation*:
"Equality in Whirlpool and Coming Together; fear in Delusion and Treasure; good years in the name of Society; and the cessation of hostility in Collision <note>Note: the commentator notes a slight variation in interpretation here</note>."

Furthermore, there is this specific detail in the *Summary Compilation*:
"Famine, disease, thieves, and weapons are increased by these. These effects are to be known when the planets are in the Blue Path <note>Nila-vithi - a specific division of the ecliptic</note>. There is no loss in the [other] paths, but fear of weapons in the Deer Path <note>Mriga-vithi</note>, and diseases of the groin and cattle in the Jaradgava Path."

<summary>This page defines and classifies six specific planetary configurations (Yogas) involving Saturn, Jupiter, and other planets, detailing their names—such as Treasure, Collision, and Whirlpool—and their resulting effects on human society, ranging from famine and war to prosperity.</summary>
<keywords>Jyotisha, Astrology, Saturn, Jupiter, Samasa-Samhita, Kashyapa, Planetary Conjunctions, Famine, Mundane Astrology, Yogas</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the astronomical and meteorological discussions from the previous section, transitioning from the effects of planetary paths to the specific science of "pregnancy of clouds" (embryonic rain formation).</meta>

Page 92

In the Serpent Path <note>original: "Nagavithi," a specific segment of the sky or planetary track</note>, there is suffering for the kings of Rishabha and Dyavan. If this occurs in the Iravati and Elephant paths, it brings great joy; the Serpent and Dyavan paths everywhere would cause excessive joy through water <note>heavy rain</note>. Whether at the time of rising or during transit, these effects are unbearable. Having considered the course of these paths, one should announce the good or bad fortune to the world.

Thus ends the twentieth chapter, named "The Desert Triangle" <note>original: "Marushringataka"</note>, in the Commentary on the Samhita composed by Bhattotpala.

### Now the chapter on the Signs of Pregnancy <insert>of Clouds</insert> is explained.

At the very beginning, he speaks to show the purpose: "The life-breaths of the world reside in food, and food is dependent on the rainy season; therefore, the rainy season must be examined with great effort." The food of the world—of the entire universe—is its life-breath. Just as life does not persist in living beings without food, and that food is dependent on the rainy season (the time of monsoon), for this reason, the rainy season must be investigated and considered with extreme effort.

Now he says, "I shall describe those signs": "Those signs which were recorded by the Sages—composed by Garga, Parashara, Kashyapa, Vatsa, and others—are here put into meter." Those signs which are the causes for discerning the rainy season, recorded and composed by Sages such as Vashistha and others, are presented here in verse. Furthermore, those composed by Garga, Parashara, Vatsa, Kashyapa, and others, as well as the teachings of Badarayana, Aitadeva, and Devala regarding the qualities of the Sun—having seen all of these, this description of the signs of the rainy season is created in accordance with tradition.

Now, to praise the knower of the signs of cloud pregnancy, he says: "The word of a diviner whose mind is undistracted and who watches the signs of pregnancy day and night becomes like that of a Sage; his intellectual direction never fails in this land." The diviner <term>Daivavit: a knower of fate or an astrologer</term> who is focused and observes the signs of cloud pregnancy day and night—his intellectual guidance in predicting rain does not become false or fruitless, like the speech of an ancient Rishi (Sage); rather, it attains the quality of truth.

Regarding the praise of this science, he says: "What other science is greater than this, by which a man becomes a seer of the three periods of time <note>past, present, and future</note>, even in this destructive age?" Is there any other science more excellent or praiseworthy than this science of cloud pregnancy or the science of Jyotisha <note>astronomy/astrology</note>? By knowing this science according to the rules, even in the Kali Yuga <note>the current dark age</note> which destroys all sciences and where truth is diminished—since the destruction of all sciences occurs day by day in the Kali age—one becomes a knower of the past, present, and future.

Here, he mentions the opinions of others and his own: "Some say the days of pregnancy begin after the end of the bright half of the month of Karttika; however, that is not the majority opinion. I shall state the opinion of Garga and others." Some teachers, such as Siddhasena and others, claim that the days of cloud pregnancy begin after passing the end of the bright half (the full moon) of the month of Karttika <note>October-November</note>.

<summary>This page concludes a chapter on planetary paths and begins the "Garbhalakshana" (signs of cloud pregnancy), emphasizing that predicting the monsoon is vital for human survival. It cites ancient authorities like Garga and Parashara to establish the scientific lineage of meteorology.</summary>
<keywords>Bhattotpala, Garbhalakshana, cloud pregnancy, monsoon prediction, Indian meteorology, Garga, Parashara, Jyotisha, Karttika month</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of "pregnancy" (gestation) of clouds and rain, specifically debating the starting dates for observation and the duration of the gestation period based on lunar mansions (Nakshatras).</meta>

They say and declare it to be so. And thus says **Siddhasena**: "Passing beyond the bright fortnight of the month of *Karttika* <note>October–November</note>, one should consider the origin of the 'pregnancies' <note>the formation of rain clouds</note> as the cause of the abundance of autumn crops." However, that is not the opinion of the majority. 

As for the statement that the days of pregnancy begin after the end of the bright half of *Karttika*, that is the opinion of many [other] teachers. Therefore, how can the opinion of **Garga** and others—such as the sage **Parashara**, his son, and **Kashyapa**—regarding the development of the pregnancy be ignored? 

The experts say: "Beginning from the first day of the bright fortnight of the month of *Margashirsha* <note>November–December</note>, when the Moon enters the lunar mansion of *Purva Ashadha*, the signs of the pregnancies are to be known." Starting from the first day of the bright fortnight of the month of *Margashirsha*, when the Moon reaches *Purva Ashadha*, it is the time to understand the meaning [of the signs]. In the bright fortnight of *Margashirsha*—
<margin>at that time</margin>
—when the Moon stays in *Purva Ashadha*, from that point on, the signs of the pregnancies must be known. 

And thus **Garga** says on this: "When the Moon stays in *Purva Ashadha* at the beginning of the bright half of *Margashirsha*, then one should first declare the signs of the pregnancies." 

Regarding **Kashyapa**: "On the first day of the bright half of *Margashirsha*, when the Moon has gone to *Purva Ashadha*, the 'conception' of the pregnancies occurs." 

In whichever <term>Nakshatra</term> <note>lunar mansion</note> the Moon is located when the pregnancy occurs, by the power of that Moon, the delivery <note>rainfall</note> comes exactly one hundred and ninety-five days later. Whichever pregnancy occurs in whichever Nakshatra the Moon is situated, that pregnancy comes to delivery by the power of the Moon—that is, by the power of the Nakshatra joined with the Moon. 

By how much time? He says: "After one hundred and ninety-five days have passed—which is after six and a half months—the delivery comes in that very same Nakshatra." This is what is meant: in whichever Nakshatra the Moon was when the pregnancy was formed, after one hundred and ninety-five days have passed, the delivery occurs when the Moon is again in that same Nakshatra. 

It is said that delivery comes "by the power of the Moon." Does this mean delivery occurs in one hundred and ninety-five days according to the lunar measure? By saying "in that very Nakshatra," he demonstrates that in whichever Nakshatra the Moon was at conception, the delivery occurs in that same Nakshatra according to the <term>Savana</term> <note>solar/civil day</note> measure. 

But if it is said to be "by the power of the Moon," how is the *Savana* measure understood? It is said: because of the statement "the delivery comes in that very [Nakshatra]," on the days calculated by the body [of the calendar], when one hundred and ninety-five days are added, the Moon returns to the Nakshatra that formed the pregnancy. Otherwise, in exactly one hundred and ninety-five days, the Moon would not be in the pregnancy-Nakshatra at all; it would fall short of the previous position. 

By the "power of the Moon"—that is, by the movement and position of the Nakshatra joined with the Moon—the Teacher has shown the measurements: the Yuga, the half-month, the total days, the solar measure, the intercalary month, and the lunar measure. 

Thus, the Teacher has stated another verse in the *Samasa Samhita* <note>The "Brief Compilation" by Varahamihira</note>: "From the beginning of the bright half of *Pausha* <note>December–January</note>... the bright halves of *Shravana* <note>July–August</note> etc. are to be pointed out; after six and a half months, the delivery occurs in that Nakshatra." Therefore, he speaks of the delivery of the pregnancy according to the *Savana* measure. 

Now, he further explains the knowledge of the time of delivery for the pregnancies that have occurred: "That which is born in the bright fortnight [is delivered in the dark fortnight]; that which is conceived in the day is born at night; that which is conceived at night is born in the day; that which is conceived at twilight is born at twilight." Born in the bright fortnight...

<summary>This page details the astrological timing for the "conception" of rain clouds, citing authorities like Garga and Kashyapa to establish that the gestation period is exactly 195 days (six and a half months) using the Savana day-count.</summary>

<page-num>93</page-num>

<margin>A3</margin> The five or seven <term>embryos</term><note>Sanskrit: *garbha*. In Indian meteorology, clouds are thought to "gestate" like embryos before releasing rain.</note> formed previously come to delivery <note>Sanskrit: *prasava*, meaning birth or rainfall</note> after **one hundred and ninety-five days** have passed. Those formed during the bright fortnight <note>The waxing moon phase</note> are delivered during the dark fortnight <note>The waning moon phase</note>. Likewise, those originating in the dark fortnight <gloss>dark</gloss> reach delivery in the bright fortnight. Embryos formed during the night reach delivery during the day. Even those formed during the night reach delivery during the day; those born during the twilight hours result in rain during the twilight itself. Those born in the morning twilight result in rain during the evening twilight, and those born in the evening twilight result in rain during the morning twilight.

And so says the sage **Garga**:
"The embryo that forms by day is ripened and delivered at night. That which originates in the bright fortnight rains in the dark fortnight. That which is born on the Full Moon rains on the New Moon; that which originates on the New Moon rains on the Full Moon. That which originates in the morning twilight rains in the evening; that which originates in the evening rains in the morning. That which originates in the eastern direction rains later at night in the west. That which is in the west at night is delivered in the forenoon. That which arises at midday is delivered at midday; that which arises at midnight is delivered at midnight."

Now, he describes the specific characteristics and timing of the embryos:
Embryos starting from the <term>Mrigashirsha</term><note>A lunar mansion/constellation corresponding to late November/early December</note> period and those born in the bright fortnight of the month of **Pausha** have little fruit <note>result in little rain</note>. One should point to the dark fortnight of **Pausha** for the measurement of the bright fortnight of **Shravana**.

Embryos arising from the bright fortnight of **Magha** reach delivery in the dark fortnight of **Shravana**. One should point to the dark fortnight of **Magha** for the bright fortnight of **Bhadrapada**. Those arising in the bright fortnight of **Phalguna** are to be assigned to the dark fortnight of **Bhadrapada**. Those originating in the dark fortnight of that same month [Phalguna] result in rain in the bright fortnight of **Ashwayuja** <note>Also known as Ashvin</note>. Embryos born in the bright fortnight of **Chaitra** are the rain-givers in the dark fortnight of **Ashwayuja**. Those originating in the dark fortnight of **Chaitra** rain in the bright fortnight of **Kartika**.

Regarding "starting from Mrigashirsha": this refers to the month of **Margashirsha**. The embryos born in the first fortnight of that time have "little fruit," meaning they give scant rain. The "first fortnight" is the bright fortnight; those born there, as well as those born in the bright fortnight of **Pausha**, have little fruit. In this system of cloud-embryo characteristics, the months starting from the dark fortnight of **Chaitra** must be understood. For instance, the bright fortnight of **Chaitra** and the dark fortnight of **Vaishakha** together constitute the month of **Chaitra**; a similar calculation should be made for the other months.

Thus, according to this lunar measure: by the dark fortnight of **Pausha**, one should indicate the measure of the bright fortnight of **Shravana**. This is what is meant: an embryo formed in the dark fortnight of **Pausha** is delivered in the bright fortnight of **Shravana**. Similarly, embryos arising in the bright fortnight of **Magha** reach delivery in the dark fortnight of **Shravana**. Furthermore, by the dark fortnight of **Magha**, one should indicate the bright fortnight of **Bhadrapada**. <margin>A3</margin> Embryos formed in the bright fortnight of **Phalguna** are to be declared as belonging to the dark fortnight of **Bhadrapada**. Those embryos originating in the dark fortnight of that same **Phalguna** rain in the bright fortnight of **Ashwayuja**. Those born in the bright fortnight of **Chaitra** are the rain-givers in the dark fortnight of **Ashwayuja**. Those originating in the dark fortnight of **Chaitra** rain in the bright fortnight of **Kartika**.

And so says **Garga**:
"Through **Magha**, one knows **Shravana**; through **Phalguna**, **Vaishakha** <note>The text here seems to vary slightly from the prose list above</note>. They say **Ashwayuja** is known through **Chaitra**, and **Kartika** through **Vaishakha**." By the bright fortnight...

# On the Reversal of Winds and Clouds

The observation must be conducted with systematic care. **Fortune!** It is determined that there is a reversal of conditions at the time of the result <note>the time of actual rainfall</note>. This is also the opinion of the sage **Parashara** <note>A legendary Vedic sage credited with early astronomical and meteorological treatises</note>. Now, he describes the characteristics of the clouds and the wind:

Clouds that arise in the East during the first half <note>during the formation of the "embryo"</note> appear in the West at the time of delivery <note>rainfall</note>. For the remaining directions, the reversal occurs in the same manner for both clouds and wind. Specifically, clouds born in the East during the gestation period appear in the West during the birth period. Those born in the West during gestation appear in the East during the rain. In the same way, for the other directions, the reversal is opposite: what arises in the South appears in the North; North in the South; Southeast in the Northwest; Northwest in the Southeast; Northeast in the Southwest; and Southwest in the Northeast. This same reversal must be stated for the wind as well. Just as the displacement of directions was described for the clouds, it must be applied to the wind: a wind arising from the East during gestation will blow from the West during the time of heavy rain. This logic should be applied everywhere.

As **Parashara** says: The month of *Shravana* <note>July-August</note> is linked to *Phalguna* <note>February-March</note>; *Bhadrapada* <note>August-September</note> to *Chaitra* <note>March-April</note>; *Ashwayuja* <note>September-October</note> to *Vaishakha* <note>April-May</note>; and *Karttika* <note>October-November</note> to the bright fortnight...

## Characteristics of Cloud-Embryo Formation

Now he describes the signs of successful "embryo" formation:
A wind that is delightful, blowing from the Northeast <note>the direction of Shiva</note> or the East <note>the direction of Indra</note>; a clear sky; the Moon and Sun surrounded by a steady, thick, and large halo. The sky should be filled with large, thick, steady clouds, shaped like elephant tusks <note>or "cloud-teeth"</note> or streaks, and tinted with red.

<margin>Steady =</margin>
The sky should have the dark luster of a crow’s egg <note>a deep, glossy blue-black</note>, with the moon and stars shining purely. A partial rainbow, thunder, lightning, and a beautiful twilight at a great distance are auspicious. The deer and birds should be peaceful in the directions of the Moon, Shiva <note>Northeast</note>, and Indra <note>East</note>. The clouds should be large, moving clockwise, and the winds should be oily <note>moist</note>, sweet, and undisturbed. When trees are seen with fresh sprouts, these are all signs that nourish the "embryos." However, there is a specific distinction for each season; I shall now describe the disturbances to the embryos caused by the nature of the seasons.

In the ancient period of embryo formation, these are the auspicious signs: The wind is pleasant, gentle, and soft to the touch. It arises from the North, Northeast, or East. The sky is spotless. The "Cold-Rayed One" <note>the Moon</note> and the Sun are both present, not too high, and surrounded by a thick, dense halo. The sky is wide and filled with "tooth-shaped" clouds <note>cirrus or jagged formations</note> and joined with red-tinted clouds. The sky has the luster of a crow's egg—*Kaka-anda* means the egg of a crow, which is dark blue; *Mechaka* means a dark, shining color. The moon and stars are pure and stainless.

In this determination, just as at twilight or during the latter half of the day, the appearance of Indra’s bow <note>the rainbow</note> accompanied by the sweet, deep rumbling of Indra’s thunder—the sound of clouds—and lightning flashes, which are like a second sun: these are auspicious and praised. Likewise, groups of birds and wild animals <note>mṛga</note> situated in the directions of the Moon, Shiva, and Indra—the Moon’s direction being the North, Shiva’s the Northeast, and Indra’s the East—are favorable if they are calm and emit sweet sounds while facing away from the sun. Furthermore, the planetary disks should be large, expansive, and moving in a clockwise direction <note>pradakṣiṇa</note>. Regarding the constellations, they should move toward the North; as stated previously, the movement of planets is auspicious when heading North, or if the Moon moves clockwise. Thus, those that move clockwise, are always pleasant, clear, and free from fearful portents or calamities <note>upatpāta</note> are praised. Similarly, trees whose sprouts are undisturbed and free from blight, and humans and cattle who are joyful and delighted—all these signs indicate that the "pregnant" clouds will be nourishing. These signs should be observed from the first day of the bright fortnight of the month of Margashirsha <note>approx. November-December</note> until the end of the month of Vaishakha <note>approx. April-May</note>. Now, regarding the specific characteristics of these cloud pregnancies produced by the nature of the seasons, I shall describe the particular conditions for the growth and development of these pregnancies.

> And so says the sage Parashara: "Now, the formation of the pregnancy occurs during the four months beginning with Margashirsha... <unclear>the connection of the waters</unclear>... they flow during the rainy season. There, the future thunder and rain are contained in the pregnancies. The appearance of the rain-signs occurs when the moon dwells in certain constellations; the retention of the pregnancy lasts for six and a half months."

Now he describes those characteristics: In the months of Pausha and Margashirsha, a red glow at twilight and clouds with halos <note>pariveṣa</note> are auspicious. In Margashirsha, it should not be excessively cold, but in Pausha, a fall of snow <note>himapāta</note> is favorable. In the month of Magha <note>Jan-Feb</note>, strong winds are prescribed, and the Sun and Moon should appear dimmed by frost. The rising and setting of the Sun while behind clouds is fortunate. In the month of Phalguna <note>Feb-March</note>, harsh, fierce winds are good, while clouds should be glossy and halos complete; a Sun that appears tawny or copper-colored is auspicious. In Chaitra <note>March-April</note>, clouds accompanied by wind and rain are good; in Vaishakha <note>April-May</note>, white "pregnancies" with halos, wind, water, lightning, and thunder are praised.

To elaborate: In the months of Pausha and Margashirsha, there should be a "twilight glow," meaning a redness at both dawns. The clouds should have halos. In Margashirsha, it should not be too cold, whereas in Pausha, a fall of snow is auspicious. In Magha, the wind should be fierce and strong, and the Sun and Moon should have their brilliance dimmed by frost—meaning their clarity is obscured by the cold. Extreme cold is expected. The rising and setting of the Sun <insert>the Aditya</insert> when accompanied by wind and clouds is "blessed," meaning auspicious. In the month of Phalguna, the wind should be rough and fierce...

The speed and the excellent characteristics of the clouds are as follows: The sun, clouds, and the halos around the sun <note>parivesha: atmospheric halos or rings around the sun/moon used in divination</note> are observed. Fragments of clouds, the sun, and the sun being of a tawny or copper color are auspicious. In the month of Chaitra <note>March–April</note>, the "embryos" of clouds <note>garbha: the formative stage of rain-clouds months before they actually release rain</note> are formed by the wind. If they are seen with clouds and halos, it is auspicious. In the month of Vaishakha <note>April–May</note>, if they are struck by the south-western wind and water, and are accompanied by thunder, these embryos are beneficial.

**As stated by the sage Kashyapa:**
"One should examine the cold, the wind, and the halos around the sun in the month of Magha <note>January–February</note>; this indicates rain in the month of Shravana <note>July–August</note>. If there is an accumulation of clouds, rain, and thunder in the month of Phalguna <note>February–March</note>, and if the winds blow from the front as described, it is auspicious for the month of Bhadrapada <note>August–September</note>. When trees have many flowers and fruits, and the wind carries the scent of gravel <note>the earthy smell of first rain</note>, one can predict cold rain and showers from Chaitra through Ashvin <note>the entire rainy season</note>. If the winds are swift, moving clockwise from the front in Vaishakha, those signs result in rain during the month of Kartika <note>October–November</note>."

**And likewise in the *Samasa Samhita*:**
Clouds that are calm, not harsh, and possess a cool, snowy wind—appearing in the shapes of deer or bulls—and are free from the heat that destroys the wind despite the net of thunder and lightning, are significant. In the dark and light fortnights of Vaishakha, the clouds created by the day and night should be observed in the twilight, joined with the evening glow in that direction.

### Now, the characteristics of clouds during their gestation period are described:

Clouds that resemble pearls or silver, or have the luster of a peaceful blue lotus, or take the shapes of aquatic creatures, are "wealthy" embryos containing abundant water. Clouds that are intensely heated by the rays of the sun and moved by gentle winds will surely release rain in torrents at the time of "delivery" <note>prasavakala: the time, usually 195 days later, when the gestated clouds finally rain</note>.

Those clouds which resemble pearls and silver—meaning they are of an extremely white color—or those resembling the *Tamala* leaf, the blue lotus, or collyrium <note>anjana: black eye-pigment</note>—meaning they are extremely dark—and those shaped like aquatic creatures—such as the *Makara* <note>a mythical sea-monster</note>, fish, crocodiles, tortoises, and porpoises—these are the types of embryos that provide abundant water and heavy rain.

Regarding the phrase "heated by the intense rays of the sun": those clouds whose "bodies" are scorched by the fierce rays of the sun and are accompanied by very little wind during the gestation period, will, after 195 days have passed, release water at the time of delivery like an angry person <note>original: "rushta" (enraged)</note> pouring out torrents; this means they become givers of excessive rain.

**And likewise in the *Samasa Samhita*:**
Clouds that are broad, thick, and numerous, shaped like aquatic creatures, are auspicious embryos. When the moon is surrounded by a glossy, dark, and thick halo, it brings water. When birds and deer are joyful and their voices are unimpaired, and the sky is clear, then the gestation is of the highest quality. Glossy suns, "sun-fish" <note>mock suns or parhelia</note>, Indra's bow <note>the rainbow</note>, and peaceful sounds from deer, birds, and humans in the direction of the lord of the sign and the wind are all signs to be observed.

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of meteorology and omens related to "cloud embryos" (garbha), specifically focusing on the atmospheric disturbances that can destroy them or indicate heavy rainfall.</meta>

The appearance of a <term>Rāhu</term> <note>the lunar node, often associated with eclipses</note> and a rainbow, along with three types of portents, indicates the state of the <term>Garbha</term> <note>literally "embryo," referring here to the formation of rain-bearing clouds months before the monsoon</note>. These are the observed signs of injury to the embryos; without pure conditions, the embryo is struck and perishes by these signs. They are as follows: the falling of meteors, a lightning strike, a fall of dust, dust-storms, the coloring of the quarters <note>unusual atmospheric hues in the sky</note>, the "burning" of the horizons, earthquakes, tremors, the appearance of a <term>Gandharvanagara</term> <note>a "city of celestial musicians," a technical term for a complex mirage or Fata Morgana</note>, the appearance of "spikes" <note>kīlaka, or solar spots/pillars</note>, comets, the "crested" celestial bodies, and the sound of a thunderclap from a clear sky. Likewise, unnatural rain such as blood, or transformations like the raining of flesh, fat, marrow, or oil.

The characteristic of a <term>Parigha</term> <note>literally "iron bar"</note> is a horizontal line of clouds appearing at sunrise or sunset. If a rainbow—the bow of Indra—is seen within it, it is a sign of the <term>Parigha</term>. The appearance of <term>Rāhu</term> and these three types of portents—celestial, atmospheric, and terrestrial—indicates that the embryo is "slain" or destroyed. This means that if any of these aforementioned portents occur, the embryo is killed.

<margin>
Rain of horses
<note>original: Aśvavarṣa</note>

</margin>

As the sage Garga says: <gloss>Thus</gloss> "Rain of clarified butter, rain of honey, rain of flesh and blood, meteors, thunderclaps, earthquakes, and the falling of lightning; halos, horizontal cloud-bars, and the bows of Indra <note>rainbows</note>; thunder from a cloudless sky, the burning of the horizons, the unseasonable appearance of flowers and fruits, and rain falling upon the earth during battles—these are signs of destruction."

And as Parāśara says: "The rising and setting of portents, meteors, thunderclaps, lightning strikes, mirage-cities, and the unnatural coloring of the sun's rays or the movement of the earth indicate the destruction of the rain."

Furthermore, he states a specific rule: "The growth of the embryos is caused by signs that are natural to the current season and by general auspicious characteristics; by the opposite of these, the reverse <note>destruction</note> occurs." In the months of Pauṣa and Mārgaśīrṣa <note>December–January</note>, the wise look for the twilight glow and halos. These are the seasonal characteristics of the embryos. General characteristics include the positions of planets, the wind, and the direction of Indra. If the embryos possess these natural seasonal signs, they thrive; if they possess the opposite, they are harmed. This implies that a medium set of signs yields a medium result.

Now, he describes the <term>Nakṣatras</term> <note>lunar mansions/constellations</note> in which an embryo, if formed, yields abundant water: When an embryo is formed in the two Bhadrapadās, Viśākhā, Pūrvāṣāḍhā, or Rohiṇī, it yields much water in all seasons. <note>The text specifies: Pūrvabhādrapadā, Uttarabhādrapadā, Viśākhā, Uttarāṣāḍhā, Pūrvāṣāḍhā, and Rohiṇī.</note> If the embryo is formed in these five constellations and is nourished by qualities such as the twilight glow in the months of Pauṣa and Mārgaśīrṣa, it becomes a giver of much water during the rainy season.

Next, he lists the constellations that cause the embryos to flourish with great results: "An embryo formed when the moon is in Śatabhiṣak, Āśleṣā, Ārdrā, Svātī, or Maghā is auspicious." If it is free from the three types of portents for many days, it is beneficial. An embryo joined with any one of these five—Śatabhiṣak, Āśleṣā, Ārdrā, Svātī, or Maghā—is auspicious and brings good results.
