# Page 9

Nothing else brings the fruits to maturity; the causes of nourishment have been described as joy, softness, and so on. When the <term>Garbha</term><note>Literally 'embryo' or 'fetus'; in Indian meteorology, this refers to the early formation of rain-clouds months before they actually discharge rain.</note> is fully developed in the sky, it is not destroyed unless struck by the three types of <term>Utpātas</term><note>Portents or extraordinary natural phenomena.</note>—those of the heavens, the atmosphere, and the earth. <margin>Burning/Heat</margin> This means it does not perish. This is what is said: the embryo does not rain for as many days as it is struck by portents such as falling meteors and the like. The characteristics of these three types of portents have been stated more clearly by the Teacher <insert>Varāhamihira</insert> in the *Samāsa Saṃhitā* <note>A condensed treatise on astrology and natural science.</note>:

-> "A red glow in the sky, terrifying sounds, and fierce winds in the quarters; meteors and halos in the sky—these are the effects of the atmosphere." <-

And likewise regarding the groups <insert>of stars</insert>:
The ten constellations—Rohiṇī, Mṛgaśīrṣa, Āśleṣā, Ārdrā, Svātī, Anurādhā, the two Āṣāḍhās, and the two Bhādrapadās—if the embryo is formed when the moon is in these, it leads to an abundance of wealth. However, if it is struck by a meteor or occupied by a comet, the embryo does not result in wealth and it does not rain.

Now, he concludes the calculation of the number of days for the result:
In the months starting with Mārgaśīrṣa <note>November-December</note>, add sixty, sixteen, and twenty; then twenty-four, and finally three days respectively. When the embryo is developed by any one of the five previously enumerated constellations in the months starting with Mārgaśīrṣa, the rain falls after one hundred and ninety-five days have passed. Thus:
*   In **Mārgaśīrṣa**: after 195 days.
*   In **Pauṣa**: after 190 days <note>195 minus 5</note>.
*   In **Māgha**: after 180 days <note>195 minus 15</note>.
*   In **Phālguṇa**: after 175 days <note>195 minus 20</note>.
*   In **Chaitra**: after 171 days <note>195 minus 24</note>.
*   In **Vaiśākha**: after 168 days <note>195 minus 27</note>.

The quantity of rain is determined by the specific position of the sun. The son of the Sage <note>referring to the lineage of Garga or similar rishis</note> says the same thing:

> In Māgha sixteen, in Phālguṇa sixteen and eight (24), in the month of Chaitra twenty, and in Vaiśākha three and two (5). In Mṛgaśīrṣa eight and in Pauṣa six; in these it rains.

This means that after these many days have passed—specifically after the lapse of one hundred and ninety-five days—it rains. The embryo becomes "full of water" (heavy rain) by this very rule. The Teacher has stated the quantity of water in the *Samāsa Saṃhitā* based on five causes: a cloud formed by five causes rains over a hundred <term>Yojanas</term><note>A measure of distance, roughly 8-9 miles.</note>, half that if by fewer causes. The five causes which the Teacher will describe are: wind, water, lightning, thunder, and clouds. When the embryo possesses these five forms, it becomes "full of water." These causes are counted in increasing order starting from the month of Mārgaśīrṣa. Because it was said "the embryos of Mārgaśīrṣa and the bright half of Pauṣa have little fruit," it indicates a weak result for those months.

Then he states the exception: In all seasons, a fully developed embryo becomes "full of water." Therefore, an embryo joined with the five causes is nourished and produces abundant rain.

<margin>

</margin>

The causes for the nourishment of the <term>cloud embryos</term><note>garbha: the Sanskrit term for the early stage of cloud formation, likened to a developing fetus</note> have been stated, and the omens were shown previously. A cloud embryo joined with specific omens becomes a great giver of water. Regarding this, it is said: An embryo with five omens rains for a hundred <term>yojanas</term><note>yojana: a traditional unit of distance, roughly 8 to 9 miles</note>.

Half of that distance is rained upon if the embryo possesses fewer omens. One that is formed with all five omens rains for a full hundred yojanas in all directions. By applying the rule of reduction, the distance is halved for each missing omen: an embryo with four omens rains for fifty yojanas; one with three omens rains for twenty-five yojanas; one with two omens rains for twelve and a half yojanas. If an embryo is joined with only one of the five types of omens, it rains for five yojanas in all directions. These five signs are specified as the characteristics of the years. Regarding the quantity of rain for embryos joined with individual omens, the following is said: A <term>drona</term><note>drona: a measurement of rainfall volume</note> of rain falls from a five-omen embryo. Specifically, six <term>adhakas</term><note>adhaka: a smaller unit of rainfall; 4 adhakas make 1 drona</note> fall due to wind, nine due to clouds, twelve due to thunder, and <insert>eight</insert> due to lightning. This is the knowledge for determining the drona measurement.

->The sage Parashara says: Know that four adhakas make one drona by measure. A drona is that which, when falling, saturates the earth to the depth of a thumb's breadth. In a vessel twenty fingers wide and eight fingers deep, when it is filled, that is known as the rainfall of an adhaka.<-

And so the Teacher says: A small bowl one hand-span wide and one hand-span deep is the standard for measuring water. Five hundred of these make an adhaka. In a five-omen embryo, one drona of rain falls. Following this: if the embryo is joined with lightning, six adhakas fall; if joined with clouds, nine adhakas fall at the time of discharge. The time of discharge is said to be 195 days after the conception, at which point the rain comes forth. Regarding this very matter, the Elder Garga says:

->One should know three adhakas are produced by wind, twelve adhakas by thunder, nine by clouds, and six by lightning. When all five omens are present, Indra <note>the god of rain and storms</note> pours down a full drona.<-

Here, a specific distinction is made: If the embryo is joined with "cruel" <note>malefic</note> planets, it results in the falling of <term>hail</term><note>karakasha</note> and stones. If the Moon or Sun is joined with or aspected by "benefic" <note>auspicious</note> planets, it results in beneficial rain. If the constellation of the embryo is occupied by malefic planets, the embryos give rain accompanied by hail and stones. "Hail and stones" refers to a downpour of ice. If the Moon or Sun are situated there and are joined with or aspected by any of the benefic planets—Mercury, Jupiter, or Venus—then the embryos are very beneficial and provide much rain. Now, regarding the specific results of the state of the embryo: If the embryo is well-nourished at the time of conception, the rain is praised as being one-eighth of a drona or more. At the time of the embryo's formation...

It cites ancient authorities like Parashara and Garga to define standardized units of rain measurement like the drona and adhaka.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of cloud pregnancy (garbha) and rainfall prediction, following the principles of ancient Indian meteorology (Jyotisha). The text is a commentary by Bhattotpala on the Brhat-samhita.</meta>

Excessive rain that occurs without the proper natural causes <note>original: "nirnimitta," meaning spontaneous or unseasonable</note> is problematic. Natural causes for rain include the rising and setting of planets, their conjunctions, their movement through the celestial circles, and the five-fold forms of solar transition. Usually, rainfall follows the lunar cycle. Therefore, excessive rain that lacks these causal signs leads to the "miscarriage" or destruction of the cloud-embryo <note>The text uses "garbha" (embryo/fetus) as a metaphor for the developing rain clouds</note>. In a region of two hundred and five hundred <note>likely referring to a specific measurement of land or distance</note>, if the rainfall exceeds twenty-five *palas* <term>pala: a traditional unit of weight/measurement</term>, the embryo is considered "leaked" or "flowed out," meaning it will not provide water during the actual rainy season.

Regarding this specific distinction, it is said: If the embryo, during its gestation, is afflicted by planetary strikes <note>malefic planetary influences</note> and is not observed at the time of delivery, it instead yields water mixed with hail at the time of its own natural cycle. During the period of the "yoga" <note>conjunction</note>, if at the time of delivery—specifically 195 days after conception—it is struck by planetary influences, it becomes unproductive. For example, when Mars is in the constellations of Rohini or Shravana. 

According to the *Aditya Purana*: If the rain is not seen due to celestial, atmospheric, or terrestrial portents, then at the time of the second embryo-conception, it gives water mixed with hail <note>original: "karaka," meaning hailstones</note>. 

An analogy is provided here: Just as milk becomes hard over time.
<margin>Note: abbreviation for "vidhra" or "dhr" likely referring to a specific citation or correction</margin>
Just as the milk of a milch-cow, if kept for a long time, turns into ghee or hardens, so too does the water that has passed its time reach a state of hardness. The water attains the nature of stones <note>hail</note>.
<margin>Note: abbreviation for "vidhra"</margin>
The water <gloss>hardness</gloss> **attains a hardened state**. It turns into hailstones.

### Characteristics of a Nourished Embryo

Now, the signs of a healthy, nourished cloud-embryo are described: That which is accompanied by wind, water, lightning, thunder, and clouds, and possesses these five forms, becomes a great giver of water. If it does not "leak" its water during the gestation period, then when the time of delivery arrives, it produces cool water. 

"Wind" refers to air; "water" is the liquid; "lightning" is the flash; "thunder" is the sound of the clouds; and "clouds" are the visible masses. An embryo endowed with these five elements is "well-nourished" and provides abundant rain. Such an embryo, if it does not release its water prematurely during the gestation phase, will wait for the delivery time—usually after 195 days have passed—and then shower cool droplets of water.

***

Thus ends the nineteenth chapter, titled "Characteristics of the Cloud-Embryo," in the *Commentary on the Samhita* composed by Bhattotpala.

***

### The Chapter on the Retention of the Embryo

Now, the chapter on the "Retention of the Embryo" <note>Garbha-dharana</note> is explained. First, the signs of the deities/elements are given: The four days starting from the eighth day of the bright fortnight of the month of Jyeshtha <note>May–June</note> are the days for "Wind Retention." On these days, soft and auspicious winds are praised, as is a sky covered with smooth, thick clouds. In the month of Jyeshtha, during the light (waxing) fortnight, those four days starting from the eighth are the days of Wind Retention...

Those days are to be sustained by the wind, and thus their delivery <note>procreation/rainfall</note> occurs. Those days are to be sustained by the wind, and thus their delivery occurs. And those days are praised as auspicious when they are gentle. A "gentle" wind is one that is pleasant to the touch, auspicious, and blowing from the North, North-East, or East <note>the directions of the Moon, Mercury, and Indra</note>. Gentle and auspicious winds are praised, as are those days where the sky is clear and not covered by clouds. In those days, the sky should not be obstructed by clouds that are "without enmity" <note>peaceful or non-threatening formations</note>.

Now, he describes the specific nature of these days:

In that same month, starting from the Swati <term>Nakshatra</term> <note>the 15th lunar mansion</note>, the eight days—or rather the four in sequence—are known as the months starting from Shravana, which are the "perfected holdings" <note>Dharana: the period when clouds are said to conceive rain</note>.

In that same month of Jyeshtha, during the bright fortnight, starting with Swati and the four following it, if rain occurs or conditions are met in Swati, Vishakha, Anuradha, and Jyeshtha in sequence, then the four months starting from Shravana are to be understood as fulfilled. These are Shravana, Bhadrapada, Ashvayuja, and Kartika. These "holdings" become perfected; they become givers of water. This is what is being said: if it rains in Swati, there is rain in Shravana; if in Vishakha, then in Bhadrapada; if in <term>Anuradha</term> <note>the 17th lunar mansion</note>, then in Ashvayuja; and if in Jyeshtha, then in Kartika.

So says the sage Kashyapa: "On the eighth day of the bright fortnight of Jyeshtha, when the lord of the gods <note>Indra</note> is the presiding deity, four 'holdings' are proclaimed, stirred by gentle winds. With clouds resembling blue collyrium <note>eye-liner</note>, with lightning, thunder, and wind, with sparks, dust, and smoke, and when the Moon and Sun are of a uniform appearance—these are to be known as auspicious. If they are interrupted or inconsistent, they are remembered as inauspicious. In such cases, there is pain caused by non-Aryans <note>hostile outsiders</note>, taxes, and thieves, as well as by reptiles. Therefore, from the <term>Swati and following</term> <note>Swati, Vishakha, Anuradha, Jyeshtha</note> asterisms, the four months from Shravana onwards become full, auspicious, gentle, and bring happiness and abundance. If it rains in Swati, then in Shravana; if in the deity of Indra <note>Vishakha</note>, then rain in Bhadrapada; if in the Friend <note>Anuradha</note>, then in Ashvayuja; and if in the Lord of the Gods <note>Jyeshtha</note>, then in Kartika. This is the collection of rules for rain. If there is no rain in these, then it is a sign of famine."

Again, he states a specific detail: "If they are of a uniform nature, they are auspicious. If they are interrupted or inconsistent, they do not lead to welfare; they are said to bring fear from taxes." On this point, the verses of Vashishtha are cited: "If those 'holdings' are of a uniform nature and similar across all four days, then they are auspicious. If they are interrupted, specifically dissimilar, they do not lead to welfare or peace; they are said to bring fear of taxes and fear of thieves."

Regarding this matter, these verses composed by Vashishtha are written: "Now he describes them: Those 'holdings' accompanied by lightning, rain, dust, and wind, and by halos around the Sun and Moon, are auspicious holdings. Even when they are without lightning, if they appear excellent and auspicious, then also they lead to the increase of all prosperity. Accompanied by dust and rain, or heat, they are auspicious, or even the play of children—such as children playing in the wind, dust, or water. Halos around the Sun and Moon, even if not very thick, should be known as achieving the purpose of all prosperity. Clouds that are steady, reddish, and moving in a clockwise direction..."

->...movements; then, from within, the observation is for the prosperity of all living beings.<-

Such types of cloud retentions are auspicious. What are they like? They are **accompanied by lightning**; they appear surrounded by the **sun and the moon**. Because they are covered, the sun and moon are obscured; in that case, the lightning flashes are excellent and beautiful. These indicate that **auspicious quarters** are present—the auspicious quarters being the directions of the Zodiac, Shiva <note>Northeast</note>, and Indra <note>East</note>. When they arise from these directions, the **wise scholar** <term>vicakṣaṇa: a learned astronomer or priest</term> should declare the production of all types of grain for all living beings.

Regarding **rain with dust**: If rain falls mixed with dust, or if it is accompanied by water, or if there is the **auspicious play of children**—beautiful activities or playing with dust or water as a result of merit-giving karma—or even with cow-dung and mud (implied by the word "etc."), and if **halos** resembling the light of the sun and moon are present, being **reddish** but not excessively red or distorted, then such rain is to be understood as achieving the welfare of all beings. This means that the goals of all beings are accomplished and all truths <note>or perhaps "all fruits/crops"</note> are brought to fruition.

Regarding **glossy clouds**: When such rain-clouds are seen, then for the prosperity of all beings—for the growth of every single living creature—the wind-driven rain shall occur. What are these clouds like? They are **glossy, reddish, compact, and dense**, and their **movements are circumambulatory** <term>pradakṣiṇa: clockwise/right-handed movement</term>. Their movement is such that those situated in the East move to the South, those in the South to the West, those in the West to the North, and those in the North back to the East.

**Thus ends the twenty-second chapter, titled "The Chapter on Cloud Retention," in the Commentary on the Saṃhitā composed by the illustrious Bhaṭṭotpala.**

**Now, the Chapter on Rainfall is explained.**

First, he states the characteristics:

> When the full moon of the month of **Jyeṣṭha** <note>May–June</note> has passed, the experts should predict good or evil, as well as the quantity of water, based on the rain that falls starting from the constellation **Pūrvāṣāḍhā**.

When the full moon day of the month of Jyeṣṭha has completely passed, starting from the asterism Pūrvāṣāḍhā through all the following constellations, the experts (scholars) must declare the **quantity of water** and whether it will be auspicious or inauspicious. If it rains, it is auspicious; if it does not rain, it is inauspicious. As **Garga** <note>An ancient sage and authority on astronomy</note> says: "Having passed the root of Jyeṣṭha, from the first day of the lunar fortnight in the next month, one should observe the omens to know the nature of the year's rain."

Now, he speaks regarding the measurement of water:

> Regarding the instruction on the quantity of water: using a **round bowl** one cubit wide, one should measure the fallen water; fifty *palas* make one **āḍhaka**.

Taking a **round bowl** <term>kuṇḍaka: a vessel used as a rain gauge</term> that is one **cubit** <term>hasta: approximately 18 inches</term> wide and perfectly circular, the instruction for the measurement of water should be carried out. One should measure the water that has fallen by this standard: fifty *palas* <note>a unit of weight</note> constitute one *āḍhaka* <note>a unit of volume</note>. This is what is meant: one should place the cubit-wide bowl outside when the god <note>Indra/the sky</note> is raining; the water therein...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on meteorology and rainfall prediction, specifically focusing on the measurement of rain and the geographical extent of rainfall as established by various ancient sages.</meta>

<page-num>98</page-num>
<sig>98</sig>

If [the rain] has fallen, one should have it measured. Even if it amounts to two hundred *palas* <note>A traditional unit of weight; approximately 48 grams</note>, then it is seen as a *drona* <note>A larger unit of measurement for rainfall</note>, as it is said: **"Fifty *palas* make an *adhaka*; four *adhakas* make a *drona*."** So it is also stated in the *Samasa Samhita* <note>A condensed astronomical work by Varahamihira</note>:

After the full moon of the month of Jyeshtha <note>May–June</note>, the first measurement of water should be taken as it rains into the sea, using the Magadha measure of a cubit <note>term: *hasta*; a unit of length from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger</note>. As it is said: **"When the month of Jyeshtha has passed, one should declare the good or bad [omens] and the quantity of water based on what is collected in the vessel."** Regarding this, he describes the standard of rainfall: That rain by which the earth is marked with impressions like water droplets on the surface—by that water, the first measurement should be declared.

By that rain which leaves the earth marked with impressions and free from dust, or when droplets of water are seen on the tips of blades of grass—by that rain, on the first day of the Purvashadha <note>The 20th lunar mansion or nakshatra</note> and others, the rainfall for the fields and rivers occurs. The quantity of water should be spoken of in the world by that [measure] and not by any other. If the clouds are "pregnant" <note>term: *garbha-dharana*; the period when clouds are said to conceive rain, roughly 195 days before they release it</note> but it does not rain during the time of discharge, then there will be no rain at the time of delivery; this is logical. The quantity of water should be declared at the time of delivery.

Now, he states the differing opinions: Some say the rainfall covers a circle of ten *yojanas* <note>A measure of distance; one yojana is roughly 8–9 miles</note>; others say twelve. This is the opinion of Garga, Vashistha, and Parashara. Some sages, such as Kashyapa and others, say that the rain falls only as far as it is actually seen to fall. If a beautiful rain is seen in one region during the rainy season, it will be so for Purvashadha and the others. Thus says Kashyapa: "If rain is seen in a rainy region during the rainy season, it will rain heavily there for months." Others, following Devala, say a *drona* is seen within a circle of ten *yojanas*. If seen within ten *yojanas* during the rainy season, the statement about water is consistent. Thus says Devala: "What is seen raining in a circle of ten *yojanas* during the rainy season will result in much rain there for months." The opinion of Garga, Vashistha, and Parashara is that it is twelve *yojanas*; if seen within twelve *yojanas* during the rainy season, the statement about water is consistent. Thus says Garga: "If seen within twelve *yojanas* starting from the first [rain], one should point to it as a beautiful rain during the rainy season."

Furthermore, he states a specific detail: In those lunar mansions <note>term: *nakshatras*</note> where rain is seen here, it usually rains [again]; if it is not seen in the first ones, then there is a drought. In those mansions like Purvashadha where it disappears during the time of discharge, it usually rains abundantly in those same mansions during the time of delivery. If not even a little is seen in the mansions starting with Purvashadha, then a drought occurs during the time of delivery.

Now he states the rainfall measurements for the lunar mansions:
**Hasta, Saumya (Mrigashira), Chitra, Paushna (Revati), and Dhanishtha result in sixteen *dronas*. Shatabhishak, Aindra (Jyeshtha), and Swati result in four. Krittika results in ten. Shravana, Magha, Rohini, Bharani, and Mula result in fourteen.** <note>The text says "ten plus four"</note>

<summary>This page details the traditional Indian units for measuring rainfall (palas, adhakas, and dronas) and discusses the geographical range of rain clouds according to ancient sages like Garga and Kashyapa. It also correlates specific amounts of expected rainfall with the lunar mansions (nakshatras) in which the rain begins.</summary>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on Varahamihira's Brihat Samhita, specifically detailing the quantitative measurement of rainfall based on the lunar mansions (Nakshatras) and the planetary influences that affect these predictions.</meta>

In the month of Phalguna <note>February/March</note>, the measure is twenty-five; in Punarvasu, twenty *dronas* <term>drona: a traditional unit of volume used here to measure rainfall, roughly equivalent to 6.4 kilograms or 512 handfuls</term>; in Vishakha and Uttarashadha, twenty; in Ashlesha, thirteen; in Uttarabhadrapada and Rohini, twenty-five; in Purvabhadrapada and Pushya, fifteen are proclaimed; in Ashvini, twelve; and in Ardra, sixteen are stated. These amounts apply when the season is free from portents.

If it rains during the commencement of the rainy season under the following stars: Hasta, Purvashadha, Mrigashira, Chitra, or Revati, then at the time of delivery <note>the actual monsoon period</note>, it will rain sixteen *dronas*. Similarly, under Shatabhishak, Jyeshtha, or Swati, it rains four *dronas*. Under Krittika, it is ten *dronas*. Under Shravana, Magha, Anuradha, Bharani, or Mula, the amount is stated as forty *dronas*. 

To clarify the specific counts:
*   **Twenty-five *dronas*:** Purvaphalguni, Uttarabhadrapada, and Rohini.
*   **Twenty *dronas*:** Punarvasu, Vishakha, and Uttarashadha.
*   **Thirteen *dronas*:** Ashlesha.
*   **Fifteen *dronas*:** Purvabhadrapada and Pushya.
*   **Twelve *dronas*:** Ashvini.
*   **Sixteen *dronas*:** Ardra.

These measures of rainfall are valid only when the period is "free from calamities" <note>original: *nirupadruteshu*</note>. As stated in the *Samasa Samhita* <note>A shorter compendium by Varahamihira</note>: The water *dronas* starting from Ashvini and the following stars are determined by specific numerical sequences, adjusted by additions or subtractions of six.

The author further explains special conditions:
If a lunar mansion is afflicted by the Sun, Saturn <note>original: *ravisuta*, literally "son of the Sun"</note>, or a Comet (Ketu), or if it is struck by Mars <note>original: *kshititanaya*, "son of the Earth"</note>, or by various portents, there will be neither prosperity nor rain. 

<margin>Mars's affliction includes retrograde motion or striking the junction stars.</margin>

"Portents" refers to the three types: celestial, atmospheric, and terrestrial. Conversely, if the mansion is occupied by "benefic planets"—meaning Mercury, Jupiter, or Venus—and is free from the aforementioned calamities and planetary portents, then the rainfall brings prosperity and well-being.

As Garga <note>An ancient sage and authority on Indian astronomy</note> says: "When a mansion is occupied by the Sun or Saturn, or struck by Mars, or by the three types of portents, or even by a comet, one should know it as an ill omen for rain; in the opposite case, one should declare it auspicious."

**Thus ends the twenty-third chapter, titled "On Rainfall," in the Commentary on the Samhita composed by the illustrious Bhattotpala.** <sig>2</sig>

To demonstrate the traditional authority <note>original: *agama*</note> at the very beginning, he says: "From the crevices of the golden mountain <note>Mount Meru</note>, where water-flowers cling..."

In a garden filled with the humming of bees, the sweet warbling of various birds, and the soft songs of young celestial maidens, situated upon the peak of the mountain that is the abode of the gods, the sage Narada was instructed. <note>This refers to Mount Meru, the mythological axis of the world.</note> Garga, Parashara, and Kashyapa <margin>Knowledge</margin> were present in that assembly. Beholding them, I shall now speak briefly of the matters concerning the conjunction of the Moon with the constellation of the Creator <note>original: "Prajapatya," another name for the Rohini nakshatra</note> for the benefit of mankind.

The term <term>Prajapatya</term> refers to the constellation <term>Rohini</term> <note>The fourth lunar mansion in Hindu astrology, associated with growth and creation</note>. Its conjunction is its meeting with the Moon. Regarding the auspicious and inauspicious effects of this, Brihaspati <note>The preceptor of the gods, associated with the planet Jupiter</note> spoke to the sage named Narada. In what place? Upon the peak of the mountain that is the home of the gods. This "home of the gods" is Mount Meru. On the ridges of that mountain is a garden. What kind of garden? It contains heaps of golden rocks, and in the crevices of those rocks grow trees. In the flowers of those trees, the bees are clinging and humming. In that garden, there is the warbling of many kinds of birds and the soft, sweet sound of the singing of the young women of the gods and the Vidyadharas <note>Semi-divine celestial musicians</note>. In that assembly sat <term>Garga, Parashara, and Kashyapa</term> <margin>Knowledge</margin> <note>These are the foundational sages of Indian astronomy/astrology</note>. Having observed these festivals and considered them, I am now prepared to speak briefly, like a small "desire-fulfilling tree," on the meaning of the conjunction in <term>Prajapatya</term> for the sake of the world.

### Now, the time for considering the Rohini Yoga (Conjunction) is stated:

> When the Moon, the maker of forms, approaches the constellation of the Creator during the dark fortnight of the month of Ashadha, the astrologer, following the teachings of the scriptures, shall declare the good or evil fortune of the world.

This conjunction should be predicted even before it has arrived. I have described the calculation of this union based on the size, motion, color, and path of the Moon, as well as through omens and the wind. In the dark fortnight <note>the waning phase</note> of the month of Ashadha <note>June-July</note>, when <term>Rohini</term> is joined by the Moon, the "maker of forms," the astrologer <note>original: "dyu-maru-chintaka," one who contemplates the sky-path</note> must declare the desired or undesired fate of the people based on the traditional scriptures. This "Yoga" or conjunction of Rohini is to be explained as it will occur in the future. The method for calculating this union was declared in the <term>Panchasiddhantika</term> <note>A famous 6th-century astronomical treatise by Varahamihira</note>. As it says in the <term>Panchasiddhantika</term>: "Having calculated the distance between the star of Brahma (Rohini) and the Moon, the conjunction with the star must be declared..." and so forth in the text. And thus says Brahma...

### According to the Established Doctrine
In the <term>Siddhanta</term><note>The established astronomical treatises of India</note>, if the <term>Dhruvaka</term><note>A constant or base point used in planetary calculations</note> is greater, it is subtracted from the latter; if it is less, it is added to the former. This union is similar to the conjunction of planets. Regarding the "measure of the moon," this refers to the measurement of the lunar disk. By following the prescribed path of the waxing moon, one should observe the rising sign (<term>Lagna</term>). From that position, one should announce the auspicious or inauspicious results based on contemporary omens such as meteors, earthquakes, or shifts in the wind.

### The Procedure for the Ritual
Now, the author describes the specific ritual procedure:
To the north or the east of the city, on a level ground, one who is intent on destroying the enemies of the king should draw the groups of planets and constellations. One should worship them with incense, flowers, and offerings. A twice-born priest (<term>Brahmana</term>) should prepare a <term>Sthandila</term><note>A leveled piece of ground consecrated for a sacrifice</note> covered with <term>Kusha</term> grass, adorned with water-pots (<term>Kalashas</term>) filled with gems, water, and medicinal herbs, and decorated with fresh sprouts from trees.

As the sage <term>Garga</term><note>An ancient legendary seer and authority on astronomy and omens</note> says:
"In a direction near the city, to the north-east, on a clean and level spot of earth, or in a temple, the astrologer appointed by the King—having purified himself, mastered his senses, being wise in the interpretation of omens, and dressed in white garments—should observe a fast on the eighth day of the lunar fortnight. Then, on the day following the eighth, when the Moon conjoins with the constellation <term>Rohini</term><note>original: "Prajapatya," the asterism presided over by the Creator</note>, he should observe the omens."

One should draw the groups of planets and stars on that ground, specifically the twelve suns and the constellations as described in the <term>Samhita</term><note>A compendium of Vedic or astrological knowledge</note>. Having drawn them, he should worship them with incense, flowers, and food offerings. On the sacrificial mound, the priest should place water-pots decorated with gems, jewels, and fragrant medicinal substances. These pots should be adorned with fresh, vibrant leaves from trees to ensure peace, and smeared with sandalwood paste. The mound must be covered with <term>Darbha</term><note>Sacred grass used in Vedic rituals</note> grass.

Then, what should be done there? The text explains:
-> Touching them with the <term>Maruta</term> mantra, having placed all types of seeds in a water-pot, they should be bathed in water containing gold; then, a fire-oblation (<term>Homa</term>) should be performed with the mantras of the Wind, the Water-God, and the Moon. <-

Then, all the seeds should be consecrated with the mantra dedicated to <term>Marut</term><note>The Wind God</note> and placed inside the pot. They should be immersed in water containing gold and <term>Kusha</term> grass. Finally, the fire-sacrifice should be performed using the specific mantras for <term>Marut</term> (the Wind), <term>Varuna</term> (the Lord of the Waters), and <term>Soma</term> (the Moon).

<meta>This page continues the discussion of ritual preparations, specifically focusing on the construction of ceremonial banners and the astronomical/geometrical methods for determining cardinal directions.</meta>

# Page 100

Regarding the abandonment of certain items, the oblation of <term>Caru</term> <note>a sacrificial porridge of grains boiled with milk or water</note> should be performed with mantras dedicated to Varuna and Soma. The <term>Sthalipaka</term> <note>a dish of rice cooked in a pot for domestic sacrifice</note> is offered into the fire with these same Varuna and Soma mantras.

What should then be done? It is said: one should prepare the marking and the banner. The banner should be three times the length of the staff. First, once the directions have been determined, when the moon is in conjunction with the asterism Rohini <note>the 'Red One', a prominent star in Taurus associated with growth and fertility</note>, one should raise the banner.

The banner, called a <term>Vaijayanti</term> <note>a 'victory banner' often characterized by its great length</note>, should be made of eight colors. It should be raised on a staff measuring four cubits <note>original: "chatur-hasta", roughly 6 feet</note>. As stated in the Puranas: "A bow is known to be four cubits; two bow-lengths make a staff." Thus, a banner of four cubits on a staff three times that length means the pole stands twelve cubits high. This is the meaning: one should fasten it to a wooden pole twelve cubits high.

First, after the cardinal directions are established, and when the moon is in conjunction with Rohini, one observes the wind via the banner to see from which direction it blows. This method of determining the directions was taught by the Teacher in the *Pancha-siddhantika* <note>The 'Five Treatises', a famous 6th-century astronomical work by Varahamihira</note>.

It is as follows: On a circle with a diameter of one <term>Shanku</term> <note>a gnomon or vertical pillar used to cast a shadow for astronomical measurements</note>, the directions are established by the entry and exit of the shadow. On a piece of ground leveled with water, one should draw a circle using a cord twenty-four digits long. The marking of that circle will be forty-eight digits in circumference. In the center of this circle, the <term>Shanku</term> (gnomon) is placed.

Then, when the sun is in the eastern hemisphere, a point should be marked where the shadow of that gnomon enters the circumference of the circle; that is the Eastern point. Then, when the sun is in the western hemisphere, a point should be marked where the shadow of that same gnomon exits the circumference; that is the Western point. Thus, by the entry and exit of the shadow, the East-West line is established.

Then, stretching a cord between those two points, one should draw a line; that is the East-West line. To find the North-South line, one must proceed as follows: Taking one point as the center and the other as the radius, draw a circle. Then, taking the second point as the center and the first as the radius, draw another circle. From the intersection of these two circles, a barley-shaped figure <note>a vesica piscis</note> is formed. A line drawn through the center of this figure is the North-South line. Thus, the four cardinal directions are established. Then, by one's own intelligence, the intermediate directions should be determined.

Once the directions are established, the banner should be placed at the top of the pole. When the moon is in conjunction with Rohini, one observes which way the wind blows. From this, one may predict good or bad fortune. Thus, the variations of the fortnights and watches of the day are considered...

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of meteorology and astro-omens, specifically focusing on the Rohini Yoga (the conjunction of the Moon with the star Rohini) to predict the rainy season.</meta>

<warning>The manuscript page has significant liquid staining and some foxing, which slightly obscures characters in the central and lower sections, though the text remains mostly legible.</warning>

For the purpose of predicting the rains, if the wind moves through the divisions of the day with strength and stability, it is always a bringer of good fortune. In that specific <term>Rohini Yoga</term><note>The astronomical conjunction of the Moon with the asterism Rohini, used anciently to forecast the year's weather.</note>, one should analyze the <term>Prahars</term><note>A traditional unit of time lasting approximately three hours; there are eight in a full day-night cycle.</note>, the nations, the regions, the virtues, the fortnights, and the various possibilities. For what purpose? For the purpose of the rains—to know the timing of the rainy season. 

This is what is said: In the day and night when the Moon is joined with Rohini, if a pleasant wind blows from the beginning of sunrise through the first *Prahar*, then the heavens will rain during the first fortnight of the month of <term>Shravana</term><note>July–August; the peak of the monsoon.</note>. If the winds are unpleasant, there will be a lack of rain; the same logic applies. 

In this way:
*   **Second Prahar (Day):** If the wind is pleasant, it rains in the second fortnight of Shravana.
*   **Third Prahar (Day):** It rains in the first fortnight of <term>Bhadrapada</term><note>August–September.</note>.
*   **Fourth Prahar (Day):** It rains in the second fortnight of Bhadrapada.

Likewise during the night:
*   **First Prahar (Night):** Pleasant wind brings rain in the first fortnight of <term>Ashvayuja</term><note>September–October.</note>.
*   **Second Prahar (Night):** Rain in the second fortnight of Ashvayuja.
*   **Third Prahar (Night):** Rain in the first fortnight of <term>Kartika</term><note>October–November.</note>.
*   **Fourth Prahar (Night):** Rain in the second fortnight of Kartika.

If the winds are unpleasant, there is a drought. This should be applied as possible. Regarding the "divisions of the day," the specific timing should be declared based on those portions. If a fortnight is determined by a full *Prahar*, then seven and a half days are determined by half a *Prahar*, and a quarter of a *Prahar* determines a smaller portion of time. Some say that here, the months are divided by the *Prahars*. Therefore, it is said that in this conjunction, the months are to be considered through the *Prahars*; this is the certain explanation. 

For the purpose of the rains, the days are determined by the subdivisions of that *Prahar*. A thirtieth part of a *Prahar* corresponds to a day. If a pleasant wind blows from sunrise for the duration of a *Prahar*, then it will rain for as long as the seventh part of Shravana. Thus: the second *Prahar* relates to Bhadrapada, the third to Ashvayuja, and the fourth to Kartika. If the wind is unpleasant, days of drought should be predicted. This explanation is excellent because it was stated immediately after by the Teacher.

I shall now state the opinions of **Garga** and **Parashara** <note>Famous ancient sages and authors of early astronomical and astrological treatises.</note>. According to the school of Garga and others, the months are divided by the *Prahars*, or by half-*Prahars*. As Garga says: "Dividing the day into four parts, one should observe the four months, including both the bright and dark fortnights." The Teacher has provided the instruction for the Rohini Yoga. In Garga's view, the months are mapped to the "Wind-Wheel" of the *Prahars*. 

As Garga says: "If the wind blows in the first half of the day, it rains for two months. If it blows in the western portion, it rains in the latter part of the season. If the wind blows at midday, the rains will be middling or even. These two months, Bhadrapada and Ashvayuja, are considered the middle. Their direction indicates the success of the rains and the crops." 

And so say the **Rishiputras** <note>Literally "Sons of Sages," referring to a specific lineage of traditional astronomers.</note>: "If the wind blows at midday, or from the East or West, the rain is divided between those two months according to the East and West. The wind of the day carries the characteristics of the time; the months beginning with Shravana should be pointed out according to those signs." Therefore...

It cites ancient authorities like Garga and Parashara to validate the correlation between wind direction, timing, and the success of the monsoon.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical commentary on the astronomical conjunction (Yoga) of the Moon with the star Rohini, a critical period for predicting agricultural success and weather patterns for the coming year. The text references established authorities like Rishiputra and Garga.</meta>

# Page 101

Therefore, in this context, the movement of the wind is auspicious. If it does not move through the center, or if it moves in a non-clockwise direction, it is not always beneficial at all times. The proper movement is as follows: starting from the East, it moves to the Southeast, then to the South, then to the Southwest, and finally to the West. By this method of movement, the gods <note>The divine forces governing the elements</note> grant prosperity. In whichever path the wind finds stability and firmness, that path is considered dominant. One should predict good or bad fortune based on this. Furthermore, it is said that the wind blowing at the time of sunset is not auspicious if it remains stagnant.

As the sage **Rishiputra** says: "The wind that blows against the direction of the primary wind, or the wind that is strongest at the end of the period, is the one whose results should be predicted."

### On Auspicious and Inauspicious Signs

Now, regarding the seeds: those seeds which have been placed in a water-pot <term>Kumbha: a ritual vessel used for divination</term> and have sprouted during this conjunction will flourish.
<margin>The sprouted portion</margin>
At the conclusion of the period, these will attain an increase in fruits and flowers; of this, there is no doubt. During the conjunction of the Moon with **Rohini**, one must observe the seeds previously placed in the pot. Those that have sprouted will exist <note>thrive</note> in the coming year. Even among those in the pot, the specific portion that has sprouted indicates that those specific crops will reach full growth and abundance; of this, there is no doubt.

Now, he describes what is observed during the **Rohini-Yoga**:

When the winds are calm, the quarters of the sky are filled with the sounds of birds, and the atmosphere is clear, it is highly praised. When the Moon enters Rohini, the results concerning clouds and winds are as follows:

When the Moon has reached Rohini, if the directions are filled with the sweet, gentle sounds of birds and forest animals, it is considered excellent. The sky should be stainless and the wind should be pleasant. As **Garga** says: "Winds that rise during the Yoga, bringing joy and comfort, moving clockwise from the East or North, are the best." Following this, I shall describe the results pertaining to the clouds and the wind.

He describes the clouds now:

-> In some places, they are black and white; in others, purely white; and in others, black like great serpents. Only their bellies and backs are visible as they coil. They are accompanied by quivering lightning and are vast in size. <-

-> They are as white as the interior of a blooming lotus, or tinged at the edges like the color of the young sun. The sky appears as if filled with various blossoms, or like the reddish-gold hue of the *Kimshuka* flower. <-

The sky is covered by many vast clouds. They are compared to serpents (*Bhujagas*). Just as with serpents, only the bellies and backs of their coiled bodies are visible. This is an analogy for the variegated clouds; since the bellies of serpents are white and their backs are dark, the clouds appear in some places black-and-white (mixed), in some places purely white, and in others purely black. The sky is filled with the sound of these clouds and the flash of lightning.

<meta>This page continues the poetic and technical analysis of atmospheric phenomena, specifically focusing on the visual characteristics of clouds during the monsoon season. The commentator uses "Sanskrit commentary style" (tīkā) to break down complex metaphors, comparing the sky to both a forest fire and a procession of royal elephants.</meta>

The sky is filled with clouds whose "tongues" are like the swirling currents of forest floods. They are as bright and pure as the inner womb of a fully blossomed lotus flower <note>original: "kamala-pankaja"</note>. These clouds are tinged with the crimson glow of the sun’s rays <note>original: "aruna-kara"</note>, making their edges appear beautifully colored. The vast sky is painted with these diverse, multi-colored clouds. What are they like? They are as white and pale as the *Kimsuka* flower <note>a bright flowering tree, Butea monosperma</note> after its nectar has been drunk by the bees <note>original: "bhramara"</note>.

**Furthermore:**

The sky appears like a forest overwhelmed by a wildfire <note>original: "dava-paritam"</note>. It is blocked out by dark, heavy clouds, yet streaked with flickering lightning <note>original: "tadit"</note> and the shimmering colors of rainbows <note>original: "surachapa"</note>. This creates a scene that resembles a forest thrown into chaos by herds of wild elephants and buffaloes. The clouds themselves are compared to these animals: the dark masses are like the buffaloes, and their movement is like the frantic energy of a forest caught in a conflagration.

**Or alternatively:**

The sky is covered by clouds that resemble heaps of stone from a mountain of black antimony <note>original: "anjana-shaila"; a dark, mineral-rich peak</note>. Or perhaps, the sky is hidden by clouds that possess the brilliant luster of frost, pearls, conch shells, and the rays of the moon. This describes the white, shimmering clouds that appear alongside the dark ones.

**Furthermore:**

The sky is surrounded by "herds of elephants" <note>original: "varanaugaih"</note>—which are actually the clouds themselves. These "cloud-elephants" have the following features:
*   **Golden Girth-ropes:** The flashes of lightning are the golden bands tied around their bellies.
*   **Tusks:** The rows of white cranes <note>original: "balaka"</note> flying in front of the clouds are their prominent tusks.
*   **Ichor:** The fresh streams of falling rain are the rut-fluid <note>term: "dana/mada" - the scented discharge from a temporal gland of an elephant in heat, signifying power</note> trickling down their temples.
*   **Trunks:** The swaying edges of the clouds are their moving trunks.
*   **Banners:** The multi-colored rainbows are the tall banners raised atop these elephants.

These clouds are as dark as the *Tamala* tree or a swarm of black bees. 

**Another description of the clouds:**

In the sky, which is reddened by the twilight, the clusters of clouds possess the dark beauty of blue water-lilies <note>original: "indivara"</note>, appearing like a figure wrapped in yellow garments...

...stealing the splendor <note>original: "kāntiṃ... corayanta"</note> when the clouds <note>original: "ghanānāṃ meghānāṃ"</note> are situated in the sky, colored by the twilight glow. The water-givers <note>clouds</note> release floods of water upon the earth. > Clouds that resemble curds, white lotuses, or the color of copper and dawn; clouds like pearls, silk, or madder-dye, or shining like molten gold; those that rise with unbroken bases like mountain peaks—these clouds, filled with lightning and thunder, bring peace and prosperity.

And as **Parashara** <note>Another foundational sage of Vedic astrology and agriculture</note> says:
> During the conjunction of the moon with the Rohini asterism <note>original: "rohiṇī-yoge"</note>, if there are gentle, clockwise winds, and the sky is adorned with rainbows, and the quarters of the sky are clear and cool, it leads to rain, safety, and a good harvest.

For as many days as these favorable omens persist, for that many years there will be prosperity. If these signs last up to seven nights, it is said that even those living in holes <note>animals/snakes</note> will emerge, and men, women, and children will rejoice. Birds will feast on fruits and flowers, and the trees will be heavy with growth.

As it is said in the **Samasa Samhita** <note>A "Brief Compilation," likely by Varahamihira</note>:
In the dark fortnight of the month of Ashadha <note>June-July</note>, if the moon is joined with Rohini and the sky—

<margin>The sun <note>original: "sahasrāṃśuḥ," the thousand-rayed one</note> is spotless and possesses extremely sharp rays.</margin>

# On the Characteristics of Clouds

The author describes the characteristics of inauspicious clouds: Clouds that are harsh, small, and scattered by the wind, or those that resemble camels, crows, ghosts, dogs, or monkeys <note>original: *shakhamriga*, literally 'branch-animals'</note>, or other condemned forms, accompanied by harsh sounds, bring neither welfare nor rain.

<margin>Clouds</margin> In this way, from such harsh clouds, there is no welfare (prosperity) and no rain occurs. What kind of clouds? Those that are very fierce, small or light, and whose bodies (forms) are driven and scattered in all directions by the wind.

<margin>Direction</margin> These forms resemble a camel, a young camel, a crow, a ghost, a dog, or a monkey. Those clouds that possess such an appearance, or the appearance of other sinful and condemned creatures like dogs, cats, or owls, and are accompanied by harsh sounds, are inauspicious. As the sage Garga says: "Clouds that are shaped like insects, needles, or harsh eyes, or those that imitate sinful creatures, are givers of sinful (bad) results."

**Now he describes the auspicious characteristics:** If the sun has soft rays in a cloudless sky, it creates water (rain). Similarly, if at night the stars in the sky are pure, clear, and shine like a white water-lily <note>original: *kumuda*</note> in full bloom, it leads to immediate and excellent rain. When the sun <note>original: *vivasvan*</note> in the sky is free from clouds and possesses gentle rays rather than fierce heat, it acts as a rain-maker in this manner. Furthermore, if the clear firmament at night shows stars as bright as a blossoming lily, it indicates a beautiful rainfall is imminent.

**Now, the chapter on the results of clouds according to their direction:**
<margin>Note =</margin> From those appearing in the East, there is the completion of the harvest and rain. From those gathered in the Southeast <note>original: *agneya*, the direction of the fire-god Agni</note>, there is danger from fire. From those in the South, the harvest perishes. From those in the Southwest, half the harvest is lost. From those arising in the West, there is excellent rain. <margin>Perish =</margin> From those in the Northwest, there is wind-driven rain, occurring only in some places and not others. From those arising in the North, there is full and abundant rain. From those seen in the Northeast <note>original: *aishanya*, the direction of Shiva/Isha</note>, the harvest is abundant and superior. In this same way, just as the clouds are said to give good or bad results, the wind also gives results according to its direction. This is what is meant: just as the harvest is completed by the clouds in the East, so too is the harvest completed by a wind blowing from the East. This logic should be applied to the other directions as well. He now speaks for the sake of other regions...

Falling meteors, lightning, thunderbolts, atmospheric explosions, earthquakes, and the cries of wild animals and birds—just as these indicate the behavior of the clouds, so too do they reveal the coming results. <note>The text suggests that natural disturbances act as portents for weather patterns.</note> Regarding falling meteors and lightning: the Teacher <note>referring to the author of the root text, likely Varahamihira</note> has already explained these in the preceding sections. "Atmospheric explosion" refers to a great sound in the sky; "earthquake" refers to the shaking of the ground. The "cries of wild animals" refers to creatures of the forest, and "birds" refers to those known as winged creatures. By these means, the clouds are described, meaning the success or failure of the crops is determined by these specific signs.

Now, he describes the knowledge of good and evil omens using the four pots previously placed in the cardinal directions:

-> **By those pots marked with names, starting from the North and moving clockwise, assigned to the months beginning with Shravana; if they remain full, the giver of water (rain) is granted. If they are empty, a lack of rain is to be expected.** <-

With those pots situated in the North and other directions—moving in a clockwise fashion—and assigned to the four months starting with Shravana <note>July-August</note>, the following is said: The pot in the North is to be imagined as the month of Shravana. The one in the East is <term>Bhadrapada</term> <note>August-September</note>, the one in the South is <term>Ashvayuja</term> <note>September-October</note>, and the one in the West is <term>Kartika</term> <note>October-November</note>. If a pot is found to be full at the end of the observation period, the "giver of water" (rain) will be present in the month assigned to that pot. This means the gods will send rain in that month.

To clarify: if the Northern pot is full, rain is predicted for Shravana; if the Eastern pot is full, for Bhadrapada; if the Southern, for Ashvayuja; and if the Western, for Kartika. If all pots are full, rain is predicted for all four months. If the pots are broken or have leaked, it indicates a struggle or lack of rain. If they are partially full, the rain should be judged as "medium" according to one's own intellect, following the proportion of the water remaining.

And so says the sage **Garga**:
> One should know Shravana from the North, Bhadrapada is said to be in the East, Ashvayuja is known in the South, and they know Kartika in the West. If all pots are perfectly full and clear like the sky, the god Indra <note>original: "Vasava"</note> will rain throughout all four months. If all are empty, there is no rain; if half-full, medium rain. One must announce the rainfall according to the exact measure of the water.

## On Other Pots
Furthermore, regarding those pots marked with the names of kings, or marked with the names of countries, or others: if they are full of clear water, it indicates prosperity; if not, it indicates fear or the absence of that quality.

Regarding those other pots marked with the names of kings <note>original: "nṛpa-nāma-cihnaiḥ"</note> or identified by the names of various countries: these are placed to understand the good or bad fortune of those specific rulers or regions. If the pots are full of clear water, it signifies success; if they are empty or the water is foul, it signifies the opposite.

<summary>This page explains how to predict the rainfall for the four months of the monsoon season by observing four water pots placed in the cardinal directions. It also mentions that similar divination can be used to predict the fate of kings and countries.</summary>

# On the Auspicious and Inauspicious Omens

The wise must speak of the auspicious and inauspicious results. This is what is meant: if those jars <note>referring to ritual or symbolic vessels used in divination</note> are full and very auspicious, the results are excellent; if they are filled with water that causes disturbance, the results are middling. As the sage **Kashyapa** <note>An ancient legendary sage often cited in technical treatises</note> says: "When the jars are broken or leaking, or if they are filled with water from a harlot's house, they bring misfortune to the people."

**Now, he describes the specific auspicious and inauspicious results based on the position of Rohini:**

Whether the Moon is far away or near, if it is situated in the southern corner and joins with **Rohini** <note>The fourth lunar mansion, associated with the star Aldebaran</note>, one should predict total misery for the world. Whether the Moon is distant or close, if it stays in the southern direction and joins with Rohini (the star of **Prajapati** <note>The creator deity who presides over Rohini</note>), then one should predict hardship, inauspiciousness, famine, and plague for the inhabitants of the land.

**As it is said:**

If the Moon rises while just touching the southern corner of Rohini and then moves northward, there will be many disturbances and afflictions, yet there will be good rain—meaning rain that falls at the proper time. Alternatively, if the Moon does not touch Rohini at first but joins it from the north, it brings beautiful, abundant, and widespread rain, as well as prosperity and well-being.

**Now, he describes the result when the Moon enters the "Cart" of Rohini:**

When the Moon is situated in the middle of the **Cart of Rohini** <note>original: *Rohini-shakata*; a specific V-shaped formation of stars in Taurus</note>, people are left without shelter and wander everywhere, begging for food for their children. They are reduced to drinking water that has been heated by the sun in parched vessels. 

The calculation for the <term>Shakata-bheda</term> <note>The "Piercing of the Cart," a rare and dreaded astronomical event where a planet passes through the V-shape of the Hyades</note> is described thus in the <term>Brahma-siddhanta</term> <note>A foundational astronomical treatise by Brahmagupta, c. 628 CE</note>: "When the latitude is more than two degrees at the seventeenth degree of Taurus, the Moon pierces the Cart of Rohini." 

When the Moon stays in the middle of Rohini's Cart, people become homeless; they go here and there, some even fleeing to other lands. What is their condition? They are "beggars of children's food," meaning they must plead for even the smallest morsel to feed their young. Furthermore, they drink water that has been scorched by the sun's rays in storage jars, indicating a severe lack of fresh water. The "Cart" refers to the cluster of many stars in Rohini.

**Now, he describes the result when the Moon is to the west of Rohini:**

If the Moon (the "cool-rayed one") rises first, and Rohini follows behind it, then it is truly auspicious for the world. Women will be devoted to their husbands and remain under the influence of love.

<summary>This page details the astrological omens derived from the Moon's proximity to the constellation Rohini, specifically warning of famine and displacement if the Moon "pierces" the constellation's V-shaped formation (the Cart).</summary>

# Page 104

Women become afflicted by desire; under the influence of passion, they remain under the control of their lovers. Now, the result is stated if the Moon is situated to the east of Rohini:

If the Moon follows behind like a lover following a beloved woman, then men, pierced by the arrows of the <term>Makardhvaja</term><note>A name for Kamadeva, the god of love; literally "the one with the fish-symbol on his banner."</note>, become subject to the will of women. If the Moon (<term>Shashi</term>) follows behind Rohini—that is, rises after it—just as a lustful man follows a beloved woman, then in that year, men, afflicted by the arrows of passion, become subservient to women.

Now, the result is stated if the Moon is situated in the remaining directions relative to Rohini:

If the Moon is in the South-East (<term>Agneya</term>), there will be great calamities. In the South-West (<term>Nairitya</term>), crops are greatly troubled and meet their destruction through <unclear>afflictions</unclear>. When the Moon is in the direction of the Lord of Creatures <note>Prajapati, the presiding deity of Rohini</note> in the North-West, the truth is that the harvest is middling. When it goes to the direction of <term>Sthanu</term><note>The North-East, associated with Shiva/Sthanu</note>, there are many excellent qualities, such as true prices and good rains.

If the Moon is in the South-East of the asterism of the Lord of Creatures—this refers to Rohini—then in that year, there are very great disturbances or calamities. When the Moon is situated in the South-West, the crops are greatly troubled by <term>Nirritis</term><note>Forces of destruction or misfortune</note>, such as unfavorable rain, and they meet their end or destruction. When the Moon is in the North-West (<term>Vayavya</term>), the harvest is of a middling sort. When the Moon reaches the North-East (<term>Aishani</term>), many widespread benefits occur. What are they? True prices, good rains, and so on. "True prices and rains" refers to the abundance of crops and timely rainfall; one should also understand these "qualities" to include security, prosperity, and freedom from disease.

And so it is said in the *Samasa Samhita*:

> Even if it moves to the North, or is situated in the North-East or East, then the earth prospers and the people flourish.

<margin>U-x</margin> If it stays in the South-East or the North-West, what of those hardships? Here, by these omens, there is hunger and fear of pests like worms and locusts. In the South-West, there is no great nourishment. If the Lord of Lilies <note>The Moon</note> is in the West or North-West, the harvest is middling.

Now, the result of the Moon striking or obscuring the <term>Yogatara</term><note>The "junction star" or the brightest/principal star of a constellation used for astronomical calculations.</note> is stated:

If the Moon should strike the principal star, or if it obscures it with its body, there is imminent fear when it strikes, and the slaying of a king or a woman when it covers it. The "principal star" is the primary star of the cluster. As it has been said:

> Among the beautiful stars, that star which is the most luminous is called the <term>Yogatara</term> of the constellations by the ancients.

If the Moon should "strike" that principal star—meaning it disappears behind a part of the Moon's body—or if it covers it by overlapping with its physical form...

It gladdens [the heart]. In such a striking manner, they say <note>original: "ushanti"</note> there is a terrible and intense fear. Likewise, if bitten, there is a calamity caused by women <note>or "relating to the female body"</note> and the death of a king occurs. Now, he describes the knowledge of good and bad omens:

If, at the time of the cattle's entry, a dark-colored animal is seen in front by men, there will be abundant water; if it is variegated, the result is middling; if white, it is imagined by the wise to be [different]. At the time of the cattle's entry—the time when the cows return at the evening twilight—if a certain sight is seen in front at the city gate, or if a dark animal is positioned in front, then there will be much water (rain). If it is variegated <note>spotted or brindled</note>, the result is middling. If a white animal is seen first, the result is of that color's nature. If there is more [darkness] in the color, there is more water and grief; if less, then less. In the case of a white color, is there any auspiciousness? One must use their intellect to imagine the water levels based on other colors. This means that within the white color, the results manifest bit by bit.

<margin>7 22 Ru</margin> And so says Garga <note>An ancient sage and astronomer</note>: "When entering from the East, if a dark-colored animal is seen in front, it provides much water. At the time the cows enter, if they are seen climbing or rising, it is said to be significant. At the time of the Gauri [twilight], the wealth is middling. A white cow brings no rain; a tawny one is remembered for news. A pale-red one leads to the destruction of crops, and a white one is remembered for disease. A variegated one from a single region rains for two years. A pale-yellow one is middling, increasing the eyebrow-like clouds. A tawny one rains in the West, and a crimson one rains on the bed [of the earth]." And so says Parashara <note>Another foundational sage of Vedic astrology</note>: "Now, at the excellent time, one should observe the omens approaching the city gate. If deer, elephants, horses, or chariots enter first, there is victory for the city. If monkeys, donkeys, camels, mongooses, or cats enter, there is a stampede or disorder. If [unclear] enter, there is fear of lightning."

Now, he describes the result of the Moon's appearance:

If the Moon, joined with the star Rohini <note>Aldebaran</note>, is not seen in the sky because it is covered by clouds, then a terrible fear of wind and disease is present in the world. But if it is joined with water-bearing clouds, the crops will be joined with much water. If in the sky <note>original: "nabhasi"</note> the Moon joined with Rohini is not seen due to being covered by clouds, then in that year, people face intense fear of wind and disease; yet, it is said that the crops will be well-supplied with water from many clouds.

### **Thus ends the twenty-fourth chapter, named "The Conjunction of Rohini," in the Commentary on the Samhita composed by the illustrious Bhattotpala.**

### **Now, the chapter on the Conjunction of Swati is explained.**

At the very beginning, he points out the application of the results mentioned in the Rohini Conjunction to other times and places: Whatever result was given for the Rohini Conjunction, that same result should be considered for the Swati star or the month of Ashadha when the Moon is bright. In the bright half of Ashadha, when the Moon is with Uttarashadha, the entire result should be reflected upon without omission. This is what is said: whatever result was mentioned in the Rohini Conjunction regarding the wind and the clouds, all of that [applies here].

# Special Observations

The conjunction of the Moon with the stars **Swati** <note>Arcturus</note> and **Ashadha** <note>stars in Sagittarius</note> must be understood. Just as the proximity of the Moon to the "Yava-shashi" <note>literally 'Barley-Moon'</note> and other configurations yields specific results, all such outcomes should be understood here. Furthermore, at the time of the "Entry of the Cattle" <note>a ritual or seasonal timing for livestock</note>, one must observe the rain patterns and other signs.

As the sage **Parashara** <note>a legendary Vedic seer and father of Indian astrology</note> says: "All these yogas <note>astronomical conjunctions</note> occur in the Swati and Ashadha stars. Therefore, a wise man should observe them with effort and purity." When the Moon is joined with **Swati**, 

<margin>Special Note</margin> if the sky is filled with dark, fragrant clouds and the wind blows, it indicates a time of plenty; however, if there are portents of destruction or disturbances in the atmosphere, the opposite occurs. I shall now explain the specific details regarding this. In this Swati conjunction, I will describe what has been said regarding the **Rohini** <note>Aldebaran</note> conjunction.

Now, he describes the signs of good and bad fortune based on whether it rains during the night or the day:

<margin>Day/Night</margin> If it rains in the first part of the night during the Swati conjunction, all crops will perish. If it rains in the second part, sesame, mung beans, and black grams will thrive. If it rains in the third part, the autumn crops will not succeed. Regarding the three parts of the day: if it rains in the first part, it is a "Good Rain" <note>beneficial for all</note>. In the second part, the rain is said to be accompanied by pests and insects. In the final part of the day, the rain is moderate. If it rains throughout the entire night, the rain is considered continuous.

To clarify: In the Swati conjunction, if it rains in the first <gloss><unclear>portion</unclear></gloss> of the night, the remaining crops will waste away and dissolve. If it rains in the second of the three parts, sesame, mung, and black gram will reach their full potential. If it rains in the third part, only low-quality grains will grow, and the "Sharad" <note>autumnal</note> crops will not manifest. On a day of unfavorable signs: if it rains in the first part, it is a beautiful and auspicious rain. If it rains in the second part of the day, it is similarly a good rain, but it is "struck" by pests, worms, and snakes <note>meaning the rain brings or coincides with an increase in vermin</note>. If it rains in the third part of the day, the rain is middling—neither too much nor too little. If it rains for the full duration of the night and day, the rain is "faultless" and "pure," meaning it rains appropriately for the season everywhere.

And so says **Garga** <note>another ancient authority on astronomy/astrology</note>: "When the Swati conjunction occurs and it rains in the early night, the summer and autumn harvests will be successful. If it rains according to the divisions of the night, one should expect a harvest of mung, black gram, and sesame. If it rains in all three parts of the Swati conjunction, the summer crops succeed but the autumn ones perish. Here it is said: rain in the first part leads to a golden harvest; in the second, a beautiful rain but with poor crops and reptiles; in the third part, it makes the year average. When **Purandara** <note>the god Indra, lord of rain</note> rains day and night during the Swati conjunction, then all people are happy and free from fear."

Now he describes the good and bad effects when the Moon is near the "Waters' Calf" <note>Apan Vatsa, a specific small star near Spica</note>: "The star situated equally to the north of **Chitra** <note>Spica</note> is known as its companion. When the Moon is near that, the Swati conjunction becomes auspicious." To the north of Chitra, there are stars known as the "Waters' Calf." When the Moon is near that Waters' Calf—

It cites ancient authorities like Parashara and Garga to explain how rain at different times of the day or night affects specific crops like sesame and mung beans.</summary>

The conjunction of the Moon with the star Swati <note>The star Arcturus</note> is auspicious and brings well-being. Now, the author describes the timing and effects of this Swati conjunction:

If, on the seventh day of the lunar month, during the Swati conjunction, snow falls, or if there is darkness during the month of Magha <note>January/February</note>, or if a violent wind blows, or if water-bearing clouds thunder incessantly, or if the sky is filled with streaks of lightning so that the moon, sun, and stars are hidden—then one should know that the coming monsoon will be abundant, bringing joy to the countryside and a full harvest of all crops.

In the darkness of the month of Magha, specifically during the dark fortnight on the seventh day when the Moon is joined with the Swati constellation: if "snow" (frost) falls, or if a "violent wind" (a powerful gale) blows, or if a "water-bearing cloud" (a cloud filled with rain) thunders continuously, or if the sky is "filled with streaks of lightning" (pervaded by rows of flashes), and the "moon, sun, and stars are lost" (meaning they have gone out of sight because the sky is covered by clouds)—then this "monsoon" (the rainy season) should be understood as one that makes the "countryside joyful" (the people delighted) and is "endowed with crops" (joined with all varieties of grain).

Now, he describes the signs that may be less favorable:

In the same way, one should observe the Swati conjunction in the months of Phalguna, Chaitra, or during the dark fortnight of Vaishakha, and especially the conjunction in the month of Ashadha. Just as it was said, "If snow falls during the Swati conjunction on the seventh day," etc., one should likewise observe and consider the Swati conjunction in the month of Phalguna, and in the dark fortnight of the months of Chaitra and Vaishakha. One should particularly investigate the Swati conjunction that occurs in Ashadha <note>June/July</note>. This is a non-canonical verse <note>original: "anarsheyam shlokah"; likely a later addition or a popular verse not found in the primary ancient sages</note>.

### Thus ends the twenty-fifth chapter, titled "The Conjunction of Swati," in the Commentary on the Samhita composed by Bhattotpala.

### Now, the chapter on the Ashadha Conjunction is explained.

Regarding this, all the results previously mentioned for the Rohini conjunction should also be understood to apply when the Moon is joined with the star Uttarashadha. This should be examined on the full moon day of the month of Ashadha.

As the sage Parashara says: "Thereafter, when the Moon is joined with the Ashadha stars, if the omens are observed in those divisions, they bear fruit in the beginning, middle, and end of the monsoon. In that regard, a soft-rayed sun and a competing wind from the East bring rain... <unclear>text obscured</unclear> ...but if the winds blow from the South-East, South, West, or North-West, the results are reversed. In the opposite case, there is heavy rain. A glow like silver or the Madhuka flower... <unclear>text obscured</unclear> ...the appearance of what was lost leads to the increase of the monsoon. The conjunction of the Ashadha stars with the Moon in the first, middle, or last parts of the night indicates rain at those respective times. If the Moon is to the north of the constellation, it brings rain; if to the south, it is wind-driven and harsh... <unclear>text obscured</unclear> ...there is suffering and heat. In this matter, a calm, pleasant, and clear atmosphere is praised; so it is remembered by the sages."

Now, he describes what must be performed during the Ashadha conjunction:

On the full moon day of Ashadha, after weighing and perfuming the various substances, on the following day... <unclear>text obscured</unclear>

# The Ritual of the Balance and the Growth of Seeds

The seed that increases in weight <note>literally "attains the state of the seed"</note> will flourish; that which decreases will not grow. This mantra is to be used for the consecration of the balance.

**The Full Moon of Ashadha** <note>The month of Ashadha usually falls in June-July, marking the onset of the monsoon in India.</note> refers to the day when the moon is in conjunction with the constellation *Uttarashadha*.

As the sage **Garga** says:
"Beginning with the constellation *Vasavya* <note>Dhanishtha</note> and the two gods, and that of *Prajapati* <note>Rohini</note>; among these, the foremost is *Rohini*, which one should observe."

On that Ashadha full moon, all varieties of seeds are gathered and measured equally, avoiding any excess or deficiency. These seeds—grains and the like—are weighed on a scale and then "perfumed" or consecrated <term>adhivasita; a ritual preparation where an object is infused with sacred energy</term> overnight while reciting the mantra. On the following day, they are weighed again. Whichever seed has increased in weight will see a double yield in the coming year. If it does not increase, it will not grow. This is the purpose of the mantra for the consecration of the balance.

As stated in the *Samasa Samhita*:
"Whichever seed increases in weight on the day after the Ashadha full moon, that crop will flourish. Here is the mantra for that purpose:"

### The Invocation of the Balance

-> **The Mantra** <-

"O Goddess Sarasvati, the embodiment of Truth, you are to be praised with this mantra. You shall reveal the truth to us, for you are established in truth and devoted to the vow of truth."

"By that same Truth through which the moon, the sun, the planets, and the hosts of stars <note>original: *jyotirgana*</note> arise in the east, remain visible, and then set in the west;"

"Whatever truth exists in all the Vedas, whatever truth is held by the teachers of the Absolute <note>original: *Brahmavadins*</note>, and whatever truth exists in the three worlds—let that truth be visible here in this scale."

"You are the daughter of Brahma; you are celebrated as the daughter of Aditi <note>the mother of the gods</note>. By lineage, you belong to the clan of Kashyapa, and by name, you are widely known <gloss>famed</gloss> as the Balance <note>original: *Tula*</note>."

**Commentary:**
The Balance is to be praised with this specific mantra. She is the Goddess Sarasvati in the form of Truth. "You shall reveal the truth to us" means she will show the ultimate reality in this ritual. Her <insert>vow</insert> is truth itself. The sun and moon, the planets like Mars and others, and the clusters of stars—by the power of truth, they stay their course, rising in the eastern direction and setting in the west. Whatever truth is spoken by those who discourse on the Supreme Brahman, and whatever truth exists in the three worlds (Earth, Atmosphere, and Heaven), let that be seen here in this ritual of the scales. You are the daughter of the lotus-born Brahma, and you are called the offspring of Aditi. By lineage <note>gotra</note>, you are of Kashyapa, and by name, you are known as the Balance.

### Characteristics of the Ritual Balance

Next, the physical characteristics of the scale are described:
The balance should be made of wood, six fingers <note>a traditional unit of measurement</note> in length, and tied with a cord. The crossbeam should be six fingers wide between the two suspension points... <unclear>technical dimensions of the scale</unclear>.

<summary>This page describes a divination ritual performed on the Ashadha full moon where seeds are weighed to predict the coming year's harvest. It includes a beautiful prayer to the Goddess Sarasvati, personified as the scales of justice and truth.</summary>

<keywords>Ashadha, divination, agriculture, Sarasvati, Tula, Balance, Garga, mantras, seeds, harvest prediction</keywords>
