<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for ritual divination or the preparation of symbolic objects, specifically focusing on the construction of a weighing apparatus and the materials used for forecasting.</meta>

The benefits of this are the attainment of power. A linen cloth should be prepared where the substances are proclaimed. In that place, a cloth is used, bound with threads; it is tied with four threads.
A <term>shikhā</term><note>original: "shikhā"; a tuft, crest, or pointed end of a cloth</note> should be made on the cloth, measuring six fingers <note>original: "ṣaḍaṅgula"; a traditional unit of measurement based on finger-width</note> in length, and it is bound with threads of that same measure. Their total length is ten fingers. Both ends and the middle of the crest are prepared. Within this, at the waist-cloth, a measurement of [two] fingers should be made. By the word "waist-cloth," the group of materials is indicated. How should those substances be proclaimed? It is said:

-> In the southern crest, gold should be placed; the remaining substances should be placed by the sons <note>likely referring to assistants or secondary components</note>. With waters, with sweat, and with essences, the vision is low, medium, or excellent. <-

With the tusks of elephants, cows, horses, and others, with the hair of goats, with beeswax, and with those born of the twice-born <note>referring to the three higher social classes</note>; in this way, by years and months, all substances are situated in their own forms.

It should be said: in the southern crest, <term>kanchana</term><note>gold</note> or <term>suvarna</term><note>refined gold</note> is to be deposited and established. All remaining substances, as well as the three types of water, are to be deposited in the northern crest. This is what is meant: when gold is being proclaimed, it should be placed in the south; all others in the north. 

Regarding the "waters": with waters produced from the cloth, the vision <note>or forecast</note> is "low." With waters from rivers and such, the vision is "medium." With "essences"—that is, waters arising from the sap of trees—the vision is "excellent" or superior. Some say that "low, medium, and excellent" refers to the growth of crops, but this interpretation is not considered elegant.

Regarding "tusks of elephants": elephants are indicated by their tusks; they are to be proclaimed by their tusks. "Cows, horses, and others" refers to their hair; by the word "others," donkeys, camels, crows, and bees are included—all these are to be proclaimed by their hair. By "gold," kings are to be proclaimed. By "beeswax," the <term>twice-born</term><note>original: "dvija"; Brahmins, Kshatriyas, and Vaishyas</note> and others are indicated. The regions, such as the Middle Country, the years, the months, and the directions starting with the East, are all to be proclaimed for the sake of knowing good and bad fortune. The remaining substances mentioned are to be proclaimed in their own natural forms.

### The Characteristics of the Scale

-> A golden scale is the best; one made of silver is middling. In the absence of those two, it should be made of <term>khadira</term> wood. A man who is pierced by an arrow... the scale should be of the proper measure. <-

A golden scale is the best or highest. That which is made of silver is middling—neither purely auspicious nor inauspicious. In the absence of gold or silver, it should be made of *khadira* wood <note>Acacia catechu, known for its hardness and ritual purity</note>. Or it may be made of a reed. The scale should be made by a man of good character. The measure of that scale is a <term>vitasti</term><note>a span</note>, which is twelve fingers <note>original: "dvādaśāṅgulā"</note> long. 

Now, regarding those things that have been weighed...

# Knowledge of Auspicious and Inauspicious Omens

The text explains the knowledge of good and bad omens: **"Loss results from a decrease, growth from an increase, and stability when the weights remain equal on the scale."** This secret of the "Sorrow of the Balance" <note>original: *tulāśoka-rahasyam*; likely referring to the mystery or critical evaluation of the weighing ritual</note> has been declared; a man should apply this even during the conjunction of the Lord of Creatures <note>Prajāpati, the presiding deity of the Rohiṇī asterism</note>.

When two items are weighed and compared on a scale, a decrease is said to be a fault or a sign of destruction. An excess indicates growth. When the weights are equal, there is neither loss nor gain. As **Garga** <note>An ancient sage and authority on Indian astronomy/astrology</note> says:
"Those whose essence <note>referring to the weight of the sample</note> remains equal are considered balanced and peaceful. If their essence diminishes, one should predict their destruction. If they are equal, they remain equal when weighed on the scale."

And so says **Parāśara**:
"When the essence is maintained, there is prosperity; when it is greater, there is victory for kings. Medium prosperity occurs when the water level is middling, and it is least when the water is low. In whichever direction the garland, the bath, and the pure fragrance are found, in that direction the wise man should know there will be auspiciousness and health."

This "Sorrow of the Balance"—also called the "Storehouse of the Balance"—is considered a primary teaching. A man should perform this "Balance-Treasury" ritual not only during the conjunction of the month of *Āṣāḍha*, but also during the conjunction of the Lord of Creatures <note>the Rohiṇī conjunction</note>. In all three of these conjunctions, the presence of malefic planets is not auspicious. If there is an intercalary month <note>an extra month added to the lunar calendar to synchronize with the solar year</note>, one should still perform the conjunction rituals.

The conjunctions of the Moon with the constellations *Svāti*, *Āṣāḍha*, and *Rohiṇī* are not praised if they occur with malefic planets. If an intercalary month occurs, it doubles the significance of the two conjunctions. When the Moon is joined with *Svāti*, or with the *Āṣāḍha* stars, or with *Rohiṇī*—if the malefic planets Mars, Saturn, Rahu, or Ketu are situated in these three conjunctions, they are not praised; they do not bring good fortune.

As **Garga** says: "When conjunctions are afflicted by malefic planets, one should predict inauspiciousness for the subjects, such as famine, national distress, and plague. With benefic planets, one should announce prosperity."

And so says **Parāśara**: "Through conjunctions with Venus and Mercury, there is the destruction of white grains, sesame, and mung beans. If Saturn is in the middle, there is a lack of hope; if Mars, there is conflict and strife with weapons. If the Moon passes through the middle of *Rohiṇī*, it brings abundance, health, and growth. However, when an intercalary month doubles these conjunctions at this time, and the first month is *Āṣāḍha*, then one should observe the two conjunctions of *Rohiṇī* and *Āṣāḍha*."

This means: when there are two *Āṣāḍha* months, one should observe the two conjunctions over the two-month period. Because the second month is considered "soiled" <note>*malamāsa*, an astrologically impure intercalary month</note>, it is said that the ritual should not be performed then, yet the doubling effect of the intercalary month means the results are intensified. Because the *Svāti* conjunction is generally prescribed, it is mentioned alongside the others.

Now, regarding the three conjunctions, he explains the specific results of the *Svāti* and *Rohiṇī* conjunctions:
**"When all three conjunctions yield similar results, then that result should be declared without a doubt. If there is a discrepancy, the result produced by the Rohiṇī conjunction is to be considered the most significant."**

Even when all three—*Svāti*, *Āṣāḍha*, and *Rohiṇī*—are similar in their fruits...

It details how planetary conjunctions with the constellations Rohiṇī, Āṣāḍha, and Svāti, especially during intercalary months, determine national prosperity or famine.</summary>

# Page 95

Then, whether the result is auspicious or inauspicious, it should be declared without any doubt. With three auspicious or inauspicious signs, the result is determined. If there is a contradiction or variation in the signs, the result follows the dominant factor. In different regions, if there are varying results, that which arises from the Rohini conjunction <note>The alignment of the Moon with the star Aldebaran</note> is to be considered definitive. Because of its importance, the wind's power overrides other factors.

### Now he describes the results of the winds on the full moon of Ashadha:
<gloss>He speaks of the results of the wind's pride/strength.</gloss>

Success of crops, fear of fire, meager rain, medium rain, excellent rain, abundant water and wind, nourishment, and prosperity—these are the results of the winds starting from the East.

In the eight directions starting from the East, the winds blowing in sequence bring these results:
1. **East:** If the wind blows from the East, there is "success of crops" <term>Nishpatti</term>; all crops will reach fulfillment.
2. **South-East:** Fear of fire and trouble from kings <note>Political instability or war</note>.
3. **South:** Meager or very little rain.
4. **South-West:** Medium rainfall.
5. **West:** Excellent or superior rainfall.
6. **North-West:** Abundant water and high winds.
7. **North:** "Nourished" <term>Pushta</term>, meaning completely full and very beautiful crops.
8. **North-East:** Prosperity and highly beneficial rain.

Since it was already stated that the wind in these directions during the Rohini conjunction brings results, the repetition here regarding crop success and fire hazards is intended to remove any confusion for the student.

**Thus ends the twenty-sixth chapter, titled "The Conjunction of Ashadha," in the Commentary on the Samhita composed by Bhattotpala.**

---

Beyond this, some people read a "Wind-Circle" <term>Vata-chakra</term> text, but it was not composed by Varahamihira. Because the verses "Success, fire, meager, medium, excellent..." would be redundant, and it is seen in many different versions. However, because of its elegance, we shall explain it for the benefit of the students.

He describes the characteristics of the wind situated in the North-East and other directions:
If there is a North-Eastern wind on the full moon of Ashadha at the time of sunset, then the success of crops like grain and others will be of the highest quality.

Now he speaks of the East:
When the wind comes from the sky in the East, tossed by the splashing of the crests of the waves of the Eastern Ocean, mingled with the mass of rays from the Sun and Moon like a beautiful tuft of hair, or increased by the friction of dark cloud-masses—then the entire earth shines, adorned with an abundance of white grain.

On that same full moon of Ashadha, when the Eastern wind blows from the sky—what kind of wind? One that is swirled by the movement and splashing of the tips of the waves of the Eastern Ocean. It is mixed with the "hair-tuft" <note>A poetic metaphor for the spreading rays</note> of the rays of the Sun and Moon. At that time, the earth becomes... <note>Text breaks off</note>

### 97: [The sky] is beautified, facing the remaining directions, covered by all [clouds]. Here the Sage Maya says: When the wind, in the path of Varuna <note>The western direction, governed by the deity Varuna</note>, is seen with an unbroken body...

<warning>The page has significant water staining and some ink bleed-through from the reverse side. The paper is aged and slightly frayed at the edges.</warning>

Everywhere there are masses or groups of clouds of an intensely dark blue color, by which the sky is beautified. Similarly, it possesses the essence of the autumn season, filled with the prosperity of all autumnal fruits and grains. It is also adorned with the most abundant waters born of the spring season. This is how the sky should be <term>observed</term> <note>Sanskrit: drashtavya; implying a technical observation for divination</note>; however, it does not happen in that specific conjunction when the sun sets. Furthermore, the earth constantly vomits a heap of ash, its surface embraced by the peaks of blazing flames, as if spoken by the voices of the skin <note>This likely refers to the crackling sound of parched earth or heat haze</note>. When at that time the wind—the breeze—flows in the path of Varuna, and likewise in the sky, being of an "unbroken body" (meaning its movement is steady and uninterrupted), and when this occurs during the Ashadha conjunction as the sun sets, then the earth is always burning. At all times it is illuminated, its surface embraced by the crests of fire—that is, the flames of fire. Because of this heat, it is heard from the voices of the body that it vomits a mass of ash in many ways.

### Regarding the South

In this conjunction, if a harsh-sounding wind flows from the south, making the monkeys dance among the branches of the forest of palm leaves and creepers, then the clouds—distressed by the hooks of the lightning-creepers arising from that conjunction—release only slow and scanty droplets of water, like a farmer <note>Sanskrit: kinasha; can also mean a person of low status or a plowman</note> in distress. In this Ashadha conjunction, when the wind flows in the southern quarter, what does it do? It moves the leaves of the palm trees and the expanse of the creepers and trees. By moving these, it is as if it makes the monkeys dance; due to the speed of the wind, the palms and others shake, and by that shaking, it is as if they are dancing. In the same manner, if a very sharp sound arises at sunset on this full moon day, then at that time the clouds—like elephants being struck by the hooks of the wind—release only small drops or droplets of water, like a miserly man; they do not release a full flow of rain.

### Regarding the Southwest

When the southwestern wind flows without ceasing at sunset, churning the clusters of cloves and cloves-vines and the ginger plants... <note>The text here describes the wind carrying the scents of the southwestern coast of India</note>. On that full moon of Ashadha, at the time of the setting of the sun, if the southwestern wind [blows]...

<meta>This page continues the analysis of wind omens observed during the full moon of the month of Ashadha (June-July), specifically focusing on the South-West, West, and North-West directions. It follows the previous discussion on the North and North-East winds.</meta>

If a South-West wind blows incessantly and continuously—a wind that tosses and whirls clusters of Sandalwood, Lavali <note>a type of sour fruit tree</note>, and Clove trees—then in that year, the earth appears in a terrifying state near the shores of the Southern Ocean. How is it described? It is covered by the bone fragments of humans. This means that people, overcome by intense hunger and thirst, perish; the earth is strewn with the shards of their bones. Because of this vast quantity of bones, the earth appears to be draped in a white garment, resembling a "Ghost-Bride" <note>original: "Pretavadhu"</note>, intoxicated and frantic, striking fear into the hearts of men.

## On the West Wind

When the wind blows from the West at the time of sunset—a wind made turbulent by rising dust storms and as restless as the tossing mane of a lion—then, although the year may be endowed with abundant crops, there will be fierce battles joined by throngs of elite warriors. In various places and regions, the earth will be continuously soaked with fat, flesh, and blood. This signifies that while food may be plentiful, there will be great slaughter in war, leading to much suffering.

## On the North-West Wind

If, during the time of the Ashadha full moon <note>the festival of Ashadhi Parva</note>, at the exact moment of the sun's setting, a forceful wind arises from the North-West—a wind with a body as dense as a cloud and moving like a serpent <note>original: "Pannaga"</note>—then one should know the following: the earth will be gladdened by streams of water <note>rain</note>, and the air will be filled with the croaking of frogs. While crops will shine in some places, the overall atmosphere will be heavy and formidable, as if the earth itself were sighing. At the time of the Lord of Rays' <note>the Sun's</note> setting, if this North-West wind blows with a fierce sound, possessing a continuous and thick form like a snake...

The snakes <note>original: "sarpa"</note> imitate the sections of the earth, meaning they are connected to the dust and the underlying systems of the land. Some are like the crawling serpents—the snakes—which Garuda <note>The divine eagle-king and enemy of serpents</note> consumes. Similarly, because of its extreme velocity, the wind is characterized by great speed. In that year, one should know the earth <note>original: "urvi"</note> to be like this: gladdened by streams of water, like the throats of frogs released and rejoicing. By the streams of water, which are received with great intensity, the frogs are delighted, joyful, and intoxicated; their throats are swelling rapidly. Thus, on the seventh day, there is an emergence like a seal on the forehead. Because of the lack of knowledge among the ignorant regarding these materials, it is like an army <note>original: "chamu"</note> appearing in the future.

Now, he speaks of the position of the sun: When the sun is at the base of the circle of rays on Mount Meru <note>The sacred five-peaked mountain of Hindu cosmology, considered the center of the physical and spiritual universe</note> at the end of the summer, and when the north wind blows, fragrant with the scent of the joyful Kadamba flowers <note>Neolamarckia cadamba, a tree that blooms at the onset of the monsoon</note>; when the clouds, possessing the collective beauty of a scholar's mind, are bent low; then they fill the body of the earth with water, even as the rays of the moon are lost. At the end of the summer, on the full moon of the month of Ashadha <note>June–July</note>, the sun's circle of rays is withdrawn behind Mount Meru. At that time, when the wind blows from the north—the quarter of Soma <note>The North</note>—it carries the fragrance of the Kadamba blossoms. Then, in that year, the clouds fill the earth with water. What kind of clouds? They are diligent, possessing the shape and knowledge of the brilliance that surrounds a scholar's intellect. Furthermore, they are high and thunderous, indicating rain. What kind of body? One where the moon's rays have vanished, meaning the cooling rays are hidden from sight.

### Now he describes the conjunction of Bhadrapada

If it rains on the fourth day of the dark fortnight of the month of Ashadha, under the constellation of Ajaikapada <note>The "One-footed Goat," an ancient deity associated with the constellation Purva Bhadrapada</note>, the clouds will behave accordingly. Having seen the full moon of Ashadha pass, if the rain-cloud pours on the fourth lunar day of the dark fortnight under the Purva Bhadrapada star, then the rainy season <note>original: "pravrit"</note> will be auspicious.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on meteorological predictions and the astrological signs of immediate rainfall, likely from a classical Sanskrit treatise on divination or natural phenomena (Samhita).</meta>

...if it does not rain, then it is not auspicious. He <note>the author</note> says further: "If the sky is devoid of the rays of the moon and sun, and the stars have vanished, I do not consider that to be the month of Bhadrapada <note>August-September, the height of the monsoon</note> if the Rain-God does not pour forth." The meaning of this is: in Bhadrapada, the sky becomes "devoid of the rays of the moon and sun," meaning the rays of the moon (Shashi) and sun (Surya) have disappeared or gone out of sight because the sky is covered with clouds. Likewise, it is "devoid of stars," meaning the stars or constellations have vanished from view. If it does not rain during such a Bhadrapada, we do not recognize it as a true Bhadrapada. This is the meaning of the verse.

### Now, the Chapter on the Signs of Immediate Rain is Explained

First, regarding the inquiry into the knowledge of rain, he speaks of the question (Prashna) concerning rain:

-> "In a query about rain, if the Moon is situated in a Watery Sign <term>Jalarashi; specifically Cancer, Scorpio, or Pisces</term> and is positioned in the Ascendant <term>Lagna; the zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon at the moment of the question</term>, or if it is in a Cardinal House <term>Kendra; the 1st, 4th, 7th, or 10th houses</term> during the Bright Fortnight <term>Shukla Paksha; the waxing phase of the moon</term>, it brings rain. If it is aspected by Benefics <term>Saumya; favorable planets like Mercury, Jupiter, or Venus</term>, it creates abundant water. If aspected by Malefics <term>Papa; unfavorable planets like the Sun, Mars, or Saturn</term>, it creates little water. During the rainy season, Venus (Bhargava) acts just like the Moon in this regard." <-

In a query about rain, if the Moon (Shashi) occupies a "Watery Abode" (Salila-nilaya), meaning it stays in any one of Cancer, Capricorn <note>specifically the latter half</note>, or Pisces, and has "gone to the Ascendant," meaning it is situated in the specific Ascendant at the time of the query—this is one yoga <note>astrological combination</note>. Or, if during the Bright Fortnight at the time of the query, the Moon is in a Watery Sign and is situated in any one of the other Cardinal Houses (the 4th, 7th, or 10th). Does this apply at all times? No, only in the "Bright Fortnight." By this, it is meant that if the Moon is in a Watery Sign during a query in the Dark Fortnight <note>Krishna Paksha; the waning moon</note>, it is not considered auspicious for rain. However, in the Bright Fortnight, if it is in a Watery Sign and in a Cardinal House other than the Ascendant, that is the second yoga. In either of these two combinations during the monsoon season, it "creates water," meaning it gives rain very quickly.

Regarding the scarcity or abundance of water: "If aspected by Benefics, there is abundant water." If the Moon, situated in the aforementioned combinations, is "aspected" or seen by any one of the Benefic planets—Mercury, Jupiter, or Venus—then it produces "abundant" or a great deal of water. If it is seen by any one of the Malefics—the Sun, Mars, or Saturn—it produces "little" or a small amount of water. <insert>Venus (Bhargava) is to be understood just like the Moon. This is what has been stated.</insert> Thus: in the Bright Fortnight, if [the planet] is in a Cardinal House, in both combinations, if aspected by Benefics there is much water; if aspected by Malefics there is little water; and if aspected by a mix of both, it creates a moderate amount of water.

Now, regarding the phrase: "If it has gone to the Ascendant or is in a Cardinal House during the Bright Fortnight." What is gained by this? It might seem redundant because in the Chapter on Definitions, the term "Kendra" (Cardinal House) already includes the four houses including the Ascendant. Therefore, mentioning the Ascendant separately seems unnecessary. To this it is replied: by saying "or if it is in a Cardinal House in the Bright Fortnight," the teacher <note>the author</note> allows for an alternative interpretation even for the Dark Fortnight...

<margin>110</margin> Detailed knowledge regarding <term>Savikalpaka</term> <note>knowledge involving conceptual distinctions or alternatives</note> has already been stated. During the bright fortnight <note>the waxing moon phase</note>, if the Moon is situated in the Ascendant or in any other <term>Kendra</term> <note>one of the four angular houses: 1st, 4th, 7th, or 10th</note> that is also a water sign, and is aspected by benefic planets, then there will be an abundance of water. If the aspect is mixed <note>from both benefic and malefic planets</note>, the water will be of medium quantity. If the Moon is in a <term>Kendra</term> and aspected by benefics, there is plenty of water; if the aspect is mixed, it is moderate. If the Moon is situated in a house other than a <term>Kendra</term>, the water is scarce. If Rahu is present, there is no water at all. How is this determined? Indeed, it is understood through these alternatives. If the Moon is in a <term>Kendra</term> during the bright fortnight, it is understood as a favorable sign. Outside of the Ascendant or <term>Kendras</term>, there is no need for further inquiry as the water will be insufficient. As stated in the *Samasa Samhita*: "During a query about rain in the rainy season, if the Moon or Venus is strong in a <term>Kendra</term> and a water sign, it brings much water; if aspected by malefics, it gives little." This has been stated in general terms. Now, another method of knowledge through inquiry <note>Prashna</note> is described:

If the petitioner touches two wet objects, or touches water, or touches a vessel, or is facing toward a task involving water, it should be told to the petitioner that there will undoubtedly be water soon. Or, if at the time of the query, the word "water" is heard. Or, during a query about rain, if the petitioner touches two wet things, or touches something specific that is given, or touches an aquatic creature, or touches something named after water—such as milk or water—or if the petitioner is near water, or is about to depart for a task involving water, then the astrologer should say: "Water will certainly and quickly be present without a doubt." Furthermore, if at the time of the query the word "water" <note>original: "Salila"</note> is heard, it should be said that water exists without a doubt.

As stated in the *Samasa Samhita*: "By touching two wet things, or by hearing or seeing the name of water..." Now, another method of knowing about rain is described:

If the Sun, situated on the sunrise mountain <note>the eastern horizon</note>, is difficult to look at due to its extreme brilliance, resembling molten gold or the luster of a polished beryl stone, then the Sun produces water on that very day during the rainy season. Also, if the Sun, having reached the middle of the sky, shines with extreme intensity. <margin>110</margin> <term>Vivasvan</term> <note>the Sun</note> situated on the sunrise mountain, impossible to look at due to excessive light, resembling <term>Dyuta-kanaka</term> <note>shining or molten gold</note>, and possessing the luster of a clear beryl gem—such a Sun produces water on that same day during the rainy season. The "time of water" refers to the rainy season for the purpose of giving results. If the Sun has gone to the middle of the sky...

### On the Knowledge of Immediate Rain
The text now describes the signs of approaching water:
When water becomes tasteless, or the sky takes on the color of a cow's eye <note>original: "gona-nibham"; likely a pale or grayish-blue hue</note>, and the directions (horizons) are clear and spotless; when there is an unusual alteration in the taste of salt <note>original: "lavana-vikriti"; perhaps referring to salt absorbing moisture from the humid air</note>; when the sky becomes the color of a crow's egg <note>original: "kaka-anda-bham"; a dark, iridescent blue-black</note>; when the wind dies down; when fish who usually stay in deep water travel to the shallows and leap about excessively; and when the repeated croaking of frogs occurs—all these are causes for the arrival of water. These are the reasons for the coming of rain.

### Further Signs of Immediate Rain
The text continues:
Cats scratching the ground intensely with their claws; a collection of rust on iron objects possessing a fishy odor <note>original: "visra-gandhah"; a raw, metallic, or fleshy smell associated with high humidity</note>; and when groups of children build bridges <note>term: setubandhah (miniature dams or embankments built by children in the dust of the streets)</note> in the roadways—these things announce that water has arrived and will fall without delay.

### Omens from Dogs and the Sky
If dogs sit upon the rooftops <note>original: "uru-pataleshu"</note> and howl, or if they look upward toward the sky with wide-open mouths; and if during the day lightning appears in the direction of the "Pinaki" <note>note: the Northeast, governed by the deity Ishana/Shiva who carries the Pinaka bow</note>, then the earth, whether high or low, will be leveled by a flood of water. This means there will be excessive rain <term>ativrishti (extreme rainfall or flooding)</term>.

### Omens from the Moon
When the moon <note>original: "hima-didhiti"; literally 'the cold-rayed one'</note> appears like the eyes of a parrot or a pigeon <note>note: implying a reddish or glowing amber tint</note>, or when it is the color of honey <note>note: a deep yellow-gold</note>; and when a "mock moon" <term>pratishashi (a lunar halo or paraselene)</term> shines in the sky, then water falls quickly and without delay.

### Signs of Night
Thunder at night, lightning...

If during the day the clouds appear as red as blood and stand upright like a staff, and if a cool wind blows from the east, then the arrival of water <note>rain</note> shall occur. If at night there is the sound of thunder, and during the day the clouds are seen as follows: glowing with a blood-red hue <note>original: "rudhiratnibha"</note> and standing in a vertical, staff-like formation, while the wind <note>original: "pavana"</note> blows cool from the eastern direction, then there will be a downfall of water.

### Another Method of Knowing Rainfall
If the new shoots of climbing plants point toward the sky, or if birds bathe themselves in water or dust, or if they frequent the banks of rivers, then the fall of water is imminent. The "creepers" <note>climbing plants</note> are well known; if their young leaves <note>original: "pravāla"</note> are upward-turning toward the celestial surface, and if birds <note>original: "vihaṅgā"</note> perform a "bath" using either water or dust/soil, then the fall of drinking water is very near; the meaning is that it will rain very soon.

### Another Method of Knowing Rainfall
When the evening clouds possess colors similar to the peacock, the parrot, the blue jay, or the cuckoo; or when they steal the luster of the hibiscus flower and the lotus; or when they resemble the waves of water, mountains, crocodiles, mud, boars, or fish, and appear in many-layered folds—then they shall give water without delay. Such evening clouds give water <note>original: "āpaḥ"</note> not after a long time, but quickly. At what time? When they share the colors of the peacock, parrot, blue jay, and cuckoo—all specific types of birds—meaning they are very dark or iridescent. Also, when they rival the luster of the hibiscus <note>a very red flower</note> and the lotus <note>which grows in the mud</note>, being extremely red. Furthermore, when they take the shapes of water-eddies, mountains, crocodiles <note>water creatures</note>, mud, boars, or fish; and when they have many-layered folds <note>original: "puta-sañcaya"</note> piled one on top of the other.

### Yet Another Method of Knowing Rainfall
Those clouds which are white as lime or the moon at their edges, but as dark as collyrium or bees in their center, appearing in many clean layers like a flight of stairs, and which move from the west toward the east—they shall soon release a flood of water upon the earth. Such clouds <note>original: "vārimucaḥ", literally 'water-releasers'</note> will quickly abandon or release a great deal of rainwater on the ground. What are they like? At their boundaries or all around, they are white like lime or the moon; but in their middle portion, they "hate" <note>surpass in darkness</note> collyrium <note>eye-black</note> and the bumblebee. They are stainless and characterized by many layers, appearing one above the other like steps.

<vocab>Rain-knowledge, blood-colored, staff-like, creeper/climbing plant, young shoots, birds, peacock-parrot-blue jay-cuckoo, hibiscus flower, clouds, moon, collyrium</vocab>

Meaning, they are situated. "Teeth-shedding water droplets" <note>original: "dāntastyajantojalakāṇa"</note> refers to those who possess water droplets; they are arranged like a flight of steps. This means they are situated in a row like a staircase. Similarly, the "garlanded one" <note>the sun or a cloud formation</note> originates from two sources: first in the eastern direction, then it travels or goes to the western direction. "Ambu-puvakas" <note>water-bearers/clouds</note> belong to the Varuni direction, meaning the West. Connected to that, the row of clouds moves toward the direction of rain.

-> **Now he describes the signs of immediate rain** <-

If at the time of the sun's rising or setting, the bow of Indra <note>rainbow</note>, the halo-bar, the mock sun, the red-streak, or lightning and halos are seen, then one should predict abundant water <note>rain</note> very quickly.

At the time of the sun's (Aditya's) rising or setting—either at dawn or at dusk—if these are seen, then quickly give/predict plenty of water. What are these? The bow of Indra (rainbow), the *parigha* <note>term: parigha; a horizontal line of clouds crossing the sun at sunrise or sunset</note>, and the mock suns. The *parigha* is a line of clouds situated horizontally at the sun's rise or set; he will describe its characteristics later as a line of clouds like a bolt. The mock sun is a second sun <note>parhelion</note>. The *rohita* <note>red-streak</note>—he will also describe its characteristics: a fragment of a rainbow appearing straight is called a *rohita*. The word "atha" is used in the sense of "and." Then lightning and the halos of the sun and moon—he will describe their characteristics as the rays of the sun reflected in the wind, etc. All these are signs.

-> **Now he describes the signs of immediate rain** <-

If the sky resembles the wing of a partridge <note>original: "tittiri-patra-nibham"; referring to dappled "mackerel" clouds</note>, and flocks of birds are joyful and calling out at the rising or setting of the sun, then the clouds will release water without delay.

If the sky (the atmosphere) resembles the wing of a partridge—meaning it is variegated or spotted like the feathers of the bird called the *tittiri*—and if flocks of birds and animals are joyful and making sounds; and if this occurs at the sun's rising or setting, or during the twilight of day and night, then the clouds will quickly discharge, release, or rain down water. If seen at sunrise, it rains during the day; if at sunset, it rains at night. This is a verse of great value.

-> **Now he describes the signs of immediate rain** <-

If the "unfailing rays" <note>original: "amogha-kiraṇā"</note> are seen at the setting-mountain like uplifted hands, and the cloud rests level with the earth, that is a sign of rain.

The one with a thousand rays is the Sun. If his "unfailing rays"—those that are not obstructed by clouds—are seen (he will describe their characteristics later as clear rays of the sun), and if these rays appear like uplifted hands from the mountain of setting; and if such rays are seen at both sunrise and sunset, as the Teacher <note>likely Varahamihira</note> will say: "If at the sun's rise or set the rays are long and unfailing..." and if the cloud (*ambuda*) rests or lingers level with the earth, then that is a sign of heavy rain.

So it is said in the *Samasa Samhita*: "Having seen at the time of rain, in the peaceful Varuna [Western] direction..."

The appearance of the quarters of the sky, the wind, and the mirrors <note>likely referring to the reflective quality of the atmosphere</note>... the appearance of the sun like a fish <note>a specific atmospheric distortion</note>, lightning in the northeast during the day, meteors, and clouds shaped like tails or trunks... these indicate the destruction of the wind. If the mountains appear dark like a heap of collyrium <note>black eye pigment</note>, or if birds bathe in water or dust, or if there is the mating of birds, or the appearance of two-tongued creatures <note>snakes</note>, or the migration of ants... these are signs. If the sun appears like the eye of a crow, a cuckoo, a pigeon, or a sparrow, or if there is a halo, or if the sky is spotless... these are omens for children. If the moon appears like honey, or there is a mock moon <note>paraselene</note>, or a cold wind from the east... these are said to bring immediate rain. These signs are determined by the sages; when the rays of the sun are intense and point upwards, it is certain.

### Now, regarding the signs of immediate rain: 
Among those mentioned, if the signs are strong, one should predict heavy rain; if middling, moderate rain; and if weak, little rain. **As Parashara <note>An ancient sage and author of early astronomical/astrological texts</note> says:** "When the omens are strong, predict wind and rain; when weak, a mere drizzle; when middling, speak of moderate rain, according to the nature of the omen. Meteors, thunderclaps, earthquakes, dust storms, and comets, as well as the unfavorable positions of planets, always influence the rain during the rainy season."

### Now, the characteristics of rain are described:
During the rainy season, if the Moon is in the seventh sign from Venus and is aspected by a benefic planet, or if Saturn is in the ninth, fifth, or seventh house, it leads to the arrival of water <note>rain</note>.
<gloss>The giver of rain at the beginning of the season...</gloss> In the rainy season, if the Moon is situated in the seventh sign from Venus and is beheld by a gentle planet <note>Jupiter or Mercury</note>, then the arrival of water occurs. Alternatively, if Saturn is in the ninth, fifth, or seventh house from the Moon and is aspected by a benefic, it leads to the arrival of rain.

### Now, the characteristics of rain are described:
<gloss>Generally, at the time of the rising or setting of the planets, or during a conjunction, or the entry into a celestial circle...</gloss>
Or at the end of a fortnight, or at the end of the sun's northern or southern course, or when the sun enters the constellation of Ardra <note>a star associated with the moist/rainy season</note>. At the time of the rising of the planets (emerging from the solar disk) or at the time of their setting; likewise, at the time of conjunction with the Moon, or entry into the "circles"—the six circles beginning with Bharani as described in the movement of Venus—at these points of entry, or at the end of the new moon or full moon phases. Likewise, at the solstices (the entry into Cancer or Capricorn), in these instances, rain generally occurs in abundance. Furthermore, when the Sun enters the constellation of Ardra, rain occurs by fixed rule.

### Now, the knowledge of rain for the year is described:
Water is produced by the conjunction of Mercury and Venus, or Mercury and Jupiter, or Jupiter and Venus. From the conjunction of Saturn and Mars, there is fear of wind and fire...

<meta>This page continues the commentary on natural omens and agricultural forecasting, specifically focusing on planetary conjunctions and the relationship between tree blossoms and crop yields.</meta>

Now, concerning the planets: When Mercury <note>original: "Jna"</note> and Venus <note>original: "Bhargava"</note> come into conjunction, there is water; this means it rains. Likewise, when Mercury and Jupiter <note>original: "Jiva"</note> meet in conjunction, water falls. However, when Saturn <note>original: "Sauri"</note> and Mars <note>original: "Angaraka"</note> meet, there is danger born of wind and fire. "Wind" refers to gales and "Fire" to conflagrations. But when is this danger certain? He says: when they are not aspected or joined by the "Benefics" <note>The term "Sangra" here refers to the auspicious group of Mercury, Jupiter, and Venus</note>. If they are not seen or joined by any of these, the danger of wind and fire occurs; if they are joined or aspected by them, it does not.

Now he describes the knowledge of planetary aspects: "When the planets following the Sun are either in front or behind, they then make the earth into a single ocean." "Following the Sun" means those planets that are inclined toward the Sun, meaning they are about to set <note>Heliacal setting/combustion</note>. Whether they are "below" (in front) or "behind" (following)—since fast-moving planets set by moving ahead of the Sun and slow-moving ones by being overtaken—whenever they set in this manner, at that time they make the earth "a single ocean," meaning entirely filled with water. The use of the plural indicates that many planets should be understood in this condition.

### ## Now the Chapter on the Flowering of Creepers is explained.

First, he speaks to demonstrate the purpose of this study: "By observing the abundance of fruits and flowers on the forest lords <term>Vanaspati: trees that bear fruit without apparent flowers, or large trees in general</term>, one should know the availability of goods and the success of the crops." By observing the "abundance of fruits and flowers"—the flourishing of fruits and blossoms—of the trees, the "availability" (affordability and plenty) of all goods should be known. Likewise, the "success" or prosperity of the various crops is to be understood.

Now he describes which tree indicates the growth of which crop: "By the *Sal* tree, the *Kalama* rice; by the Red *Ashoka*, the red rice; the *Panduka* rice by the *Kshirika*; and the *Shashtika* rice by the Blue *Ashoka*." By seeing the abundance of fruit and flowers on the *Sal* tree <note>Shorea robusta</note>, the success and availability of *Kalama* rice is known. This logic applies everywhere. Thus, *Kalama* rice is known by the *Sal*; red rice by the Red *Ashoka*; the *Panduka* variety of rice is known by the *Kshirika* <note>a milky tree, likely Wrightia tinctoria</note>; and the *Shashtika* rice <note>rice that ripens in sixty days</note> is known by the Blue *Ashoka*.

Further: "By the Banyan <note>Nyagrodha</note>, barley <note>Yavaka</note>; by the growth of the *Tinduka*, the *Shashtika* rice occurs. By the Sacred Fig <note>Ashvattha</note>, the success of all beings' grain is known." *Yavaka* is a type of grain known by the Banyan tree. *Shashtika* rice follows the growth of the *Tinduka* <note>Diospyros embryopteris</note>. By the *Ashvattha* <note>Ficus religiosa</note>, the success of the grain for all living beings is to be understood.

Further: "By the Rose Apple <note>Jambu</note>, sesame and black gram; by the growth of the *Shirisha*, the success of millet <note>Kanku</note>; and wheat by the *Madhuka*..."

# The Increase of Barley and the Seven-Leaf Tree

The increase of **Barley** <note>Sanskrit: *Yava*</note> is indicated by the **Seven-Leaf Tree** <note>Sanskrit: *Saptaparna*; Alstonia scholaris</note>. Through specific varieties of the **Rose Apple** <note>*Jambu*</note> tree, one should know the state of water-banks. From the flourishing of the **Siris** tree <note>*Shirisha*; Albizia lebbeck</note>, one predicts the discovery of hidden treasures. The **Priyangu** <note>Aglaia elaeagnoidea</note> relates to the *Kanja* <note>likely a type of grain or millet</note>. The **Mahua** <note>*Madhuka*; Madhuca longifolia</note> is a specific tree; through these Mahua trees, the abundance of **Wheat** <note>*Godhuma*</note> is to be known. As stated, the Seven-Leaf tree indicates the increase of Barley.

### On Cotton and Pulses

Now, in this context:
One should predict the prosperity of **Cotton** <note>*Karpasa*</note> from the **Atimuktaka** <note>a mountain creeper</note> and the **Kunda** <note>Downy Jasmine</note>. From the **Asana** <note>Terminalia elliptica</note> and **Beleri** <note>*Badari*; Jujube</note> trees, one predicts the state of **Horse-gram** <note>*Kulatthya*</note>. From the **Indian Elm** <note>*Chirivilva*</note>, one predicts the **Mung bean**.

The *Atimuktaka* is a specific tree; when it and the *Kunda* flowers bloom, from those two, one should deduce the state of Cotton. From the *Asana* tree, one should deduce the state of **Ghee** <note>clarified butter</note>. From the *Badari* trees, one speaks of Horse-gram. From the *Chirivilva* (also known as *Karanja*), one should predict the Mung beans based on their growth.

### On Linseed, Sesame, and Gems

Furthermore:
**Linseed** <note>*Atasi*</note> is known by the flowers of the **Reed** <note>*Vetasa*</note>. **Kodo Millet** <note>*Kodrava*</note> is known by the flowers of the **Flame-of-the-Forest** <note>*Palasha*</note>. From **Sesame** <note>*Tila*</note>, one knows of **Conch-shells**, **Pearls**, and **Silver**. From the *Kunda*, one knows of **Arrows**.

The *Vetasa* is a tree; from its flowers, the *Atasi* is to be understood. From the *Palasha* flowers—the flowers of the *Palasha* tree—the *Kodrava* is to be understood. The **Tilaka** <note>a specific tree, often used for forehead marks</note> is a special tree; by it, Conch-shells, Pearls, and Silver are indicated. "Conch" is well-known; "Pearls" refers to the fruit of the oyster; "Silver" refers to the white metal. These are to be understood. Immediately following, from the **Arrow-tree** <note>*Ishudru*</note>, a specific tree, the supply of Arrows is known.

### On Animals and Social Classes

Now, in this context:
**Elephants** are indicated by the **Tiger's Claw** <note>*Shvadamstra*</note>; **Horses** by the **Horse-ear Tree** <note>*Ashvakarna*</note>. **Cows** and **Sheep/Goats** are indicated by the **Trumpet Flower** <note>*Patala*</note>.

"Karins" refers to Elephants. These should be spoken of [in relation to the Tiger's Claw]. For Horses, the *Ashva* or *Ashvakarna* tree—both are well-known. The *Patala* is a royal species; from its [growth], the state of Goats <unclear>and</unclear> Sheep (rams) is determined. From the **Plantain** <note>*Kadali*; Banana</note>, these are overcome.

Now, in this context:
Know **Gold** <note>*Kanaka*</note> by the flowers of **Chickpea** <note>*Chanaka*</note>. The wealth of **Coral** <note>*Vidruma*</note> by the **Bandhujiva** <note>Scarlet Mallow</note>. By the growth of the **Kuravaka** <note>Red Henna</note>, the **Diamond** <note>*Vajramani*</note>. <insert>Kuravaka</insert> also indicates **Beryl** <note>*Vaidurya*</note>.

The Diamond is a specific gem. By the growth of the *Kuravaka* and the **Nandikavarta** <note>Pinwheel Flower</note>, Beryl is indicated.

Now, in this context:
Know **Pearls** by the **Five-Leaved Chaste Tree** <note>*Sindhuvara*</note>. **Emeralds** by the **Safflower** <note>*Kusumbha*</note>. By the **White Water-lily**, the **King**; by the **Blue Water-lily**, the **Minister** is declared.

The *Sindhuvara* is a tree; by it, one should know of Pearls. From the *Kusumbha*, Emeralds are produced. By the *Utpala* <note>Water-lily</note>, the King is indicated; by the Blue Lily, the Minister.

### On Prosperity and Calamity

Now, in this context:
The **Merchant** <note>*Shreshthi*</note> is indicated by **Golden Flowers** <note>*Suvarnapushpa*</note>. **Brahmins** by **Lotus** <note>*Padma*</note>. **Priests** by the **White Lily** <note>*Kumuda*</note>. The **Army Commander** by the **Saugandhika** <note>Fragrant Lily</note>. The increase of **Gold** <note>*Hiranya*</note> by the **Sun-plant** <note>*Arka*; Calotropis gigantea</note>.

The Merchant is known by Golden Flowers. By the *Padma* (Lotus), the Brahmins. By the *Kumuda* (Water-lily), the Priests and Teachers. The Army Commander by the *Saugandhika*. The increase of Gold (the metal) by the *Arka*.

Now, in this context:
By **Mangoes** <note>*Amra*</note>, **Safety** <note>*Kshema*</note>. By **Marking-nut** <note>*Bhallataka*</note>, **Fear**. By **Pilu** <note>Salvadora persica</note>, **Health**. By **Khair** <note>*Khadira*</note> and **Shami** <note>Prosopis cineraria</note>, **Famine**. By **Arjuna** trees, **Good Rain**.

By Mangoes (also called *Chuta*), one should know Safety. By the Marking-nut, Fear is proclaimed. By the *Pilu*, health (the state of being free from disease). By the flourishing of *Khadira* and *Shami*, Famine occurs. By the *Arjuna* trees, there is good rain—meaning a beautiful rainfall occurs.

Now, as follows: The **Pichu**...

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of meteorological omens, specifically focusing on the colors of the sky and cloud formations as indicators of political stability, weather patterns, and the fate of the ruling class.</meta>

Seen or observed at the times of sunrise, noon, or sunset, it causes fear of enemies; it brings about the dread of foes, distress, and calamity. The colors beginning with white—namely white, red, and yellow—in order, destroy or strike down the Brahmins <note>the priestly class</note>, Kshatriyas <note>the warrior/ruling class</note>, Vaishyas <note>the merchant class</note>, and Shudras <note>the laboring class</note>. In whichever direction it is seen facing or directly in front, it destroys the peace of that region. One-third of the sun is its spike <note>a sharp ray or omen</note>, and the other is its mouth.

## Now he describes the characteristics of clouds

Clouds that resemble curd <note>yogurt-like, meaning bright white and thick</note>, or are dark blue, or shaped like the sun's rays, or yellow-tinted and streaked, and those that are dense at the base, are bringers of abundant rain. Here, regarding the "cloud-tree" <note>a specific formation resembling a tree</note>: "resembling curd" means appearing white like curd. Likewise, "dark blue" refers to the color blue. "Shaped like the sun" means it covers the sky like a bolt. When such clouds are in the middle of the sky, and are "yellow-tinted"—meaning colored or dyed with a yellow hue—and are "dense-rooted"—meaning they are thick and solid from the very bottom—all of these cause great rainfall.

## Now he describes the auspicious and inauspicious signs for one about to travel upon seeing these clouds

If, while going in the direction of the wind, the cloud-tree disappears, there is the slaughter of the king's horses. If that same cloud-tree takes the form of a young tree and then vanishes, there is the death of the Crown Prince and the Minister. The "Crown Prince" is the one who shares half the kingdom, and the "Minister" is the counselor.

## Now he describes the characteristics of twilight again

Twilight that possesses the luster of the blue water-lily, the lapis lazuli gem, or the filaments of a lotus, and is free from wind and illuminated by the sun's rays, brings immediate rain. Such a twilight, in that very reddish glow, causes rain. What is it like? It has a radiance similar to the blue lotus, the lapis lazuli (which is blue-yellow), and the lotus filament. Furthermore, it is "free from wind," meaning undisturbed by the breeze, and "illuminated by the sun's rays," meaning made bright.

## Now he describes the characteristics of clouds [continued]

Clouds that are shaped like headless trunks, or resemble "cities of the Gandharvas" <note>a mirage or Fata Morgana</note>, or are joined with mist, smoke, and dust, certainly bring rain; otherwise, they bring extreme conflict with weapons. Clouds with "ill-formed shapes"—meaning those resembling donkeys, headless trunks, or cats—as well as celestial cities, mist, smoke, and dust; such a copper-colored twilight during the rainy season certainly brings great rain. Outside of the rainy season, when seen, it causes "weapon-fury," meaning it incites war.

## Now he describes the characteristics of twilight during the six seasons

From the cool season <note>Shishira</note> to the end of the rainy season, the colors crimson, yellow, white, variegated, lotus-like, and blood-like are the natural colors of twilight; they are auspicious. In the middle of the six seasons starting with the cool season, these colors are natural or inherent to the twilight; they are considered praised and bring good fortune.

<summary>This text details how the colors and shapes of clouds and twilight serve as omens for weather and politics, predicting everything from heavy rainfall to the death of government ministers or the outbreak of war.</summary>
<keywords>meteorology, omens, twilight, cloud formations, rain prediction, political prophecy, seasons, Gandharva-nagara</keywords>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of atmospheric omens and celestial phenomena, likely following a section on solar or planetary characteristics. The text transitions from seasonal colors of the twilight to specific cloud formations and their prophetic meanings.</meta>

For example: In the **Cool Season** <note>original: *Shishira*, the late winter months of Jan-March</note>, a crimson or blood-red twilight is auspicious. In **Spring**, yellow is auspicious; in **Summer**, white; in the **Rainy Season**, variegated or multi-colored; in **Autumn**, a non-red, pale or fair color; and in the **Early Winter** <note>original: *Hemanta*, Nov-Jan</note>, a twilight resembling the color of blood is auspicious. 

In Spring, yellow; in Summer, white; in the Rainy Season, variegated and red—these are all praised when they appear in their respective seasons. This is the "natural state." However, if these colors appear in the wrong season—for instance, if the colors described for the Cool Season appear in Spring—then it is a "perversion of nature" <term>Vikriti-vikara: an unnatural transformation or omen</term> and is considered inauspicious. 

So says the Sage **Garga**: 
"In Spring, the twilight should be the color of honey; in Summer, it is said to be white like dust or pollen. In the Rainy Season, the twilight is beautiful when it appears blue, red, or white. In Autumn, it is perceived as the color of madder <note>a reddish-purple dye</note> or like nectar. In Early Winter, it is seen as multi-colored or reddish-brown. In the Cool Season, a crimson twilight brings welfare. When the twilight is bright, clear, radiant, and calm, and when the directions, wild animals, and birds are peaceful, it is auspicious."

### On the Signs of Weapons in the Clouds
When a cloud shaped like a man bearing a weapon <note>an omen of war</note> moves near the sun, it indicates fear of the enemy. There is great danger regarding foreign foes. If a white "City of the Gandharvas" <term>Khapura: a mirage or Fata Morgana appearing as a celestial city</term> is touched by the sun's rays, it indicates the gain of a city; the ruler will conquer a new territory. However, if that celestial city is "pierced" or broken through by the sun, it signifies the destruction of the city and its looting by enemies.

### On the Rain-Bearing Clouds
If the Sun is covered on its southern side by clouds that are white or white-edged, it brings rain. Furthermore, if the "Lord of the Day" <note>the Sun</note> is covered to the south by clouds resembling clumps of *Vira* grass <note>likely a species of fragrant or thick meadow grass</note>, and if the directions are calm and free from disturbances, it brings immediate rain.

### On the Auspicious and Inauspicious Effects of the "Iron Bar" Cloud
A "bolt" or "iron bar" <term>Parigha: a horizontal line of clouds crossing the sun at sunrise or sunset</term> that appears at the time of the Sun's rising can have various effects. If it is white, it causes calamity for the King and may bring death. If it is blood-red, it causes "fever" or agitation in the army or among the military commanders. If it is the color of gold, it bestows an increase in the strength of the army.

### On the Halo
A halo <term>Paridhi</term> appearing at the sides of the rising Sun, variegated in its body, brings an abundance of water <note>heavy rain</note>.

# On the Characteristics of Solar Halos and Twilight Clouds

Now, the halos <term>paridhi: a circular halo or atmospheric ring around the sun</term> that surround the horizon in all directions are not always the cause of rain. When two fierce halos of the Sun appear joyfully on both sides—situated on the left and right flanks—and they are shaped like a body, streaked with red, and joined with the Sun, it is because the Sun itself is their "body." Such halos bring abundant water and provide for the people. However, when these halos surround the horizon in all directions and encompass all quarters, they are not for the sake of water; that is to say, it does not rain.

### Characteristics of Twilight Clouds

Now he describes the characteristics of twilight clouds:
Those clouds that take the form of **banners**, **parasols**, **mountains**, **elephants**, and **horses** should be known as twilight clouds. If they are not reddish in appearance, but resemble a mass of straw-smoke, these clouds <term>balāhaka: a specific type of rain-bearing cloud</term> increase the strength of the armies of kings.

Clouds that are hanging low, resembling trees, shining with a fierce ruddy light, or shaped like cities, bring welfare and auspiciousness during the two twilights <note>dawn and dusk</note>. Clouds of this kind, seen at the twilights, are considered favorable for women as well. 

To clarify the shapes:
*   **Banners**: well-known signs made of various cloths.
*   **Parasols**: ceremonial umbrellas.
*   **Mountains**: peaks or hills.
*   **Elephants and Horses**: the animals of the cavalry and stable.

When they bear these forms and are extremely red, they are significant at the two twilights. Regarding the "straw-smoke": clouds that resemble the accumulation or mass of smoke from burning straw increase the power of the king's military forces. Those clouds that are "hanging" (pendulous), shaped like trees, and possess a fierce, deep red glow, or are shaped like a city <note>likely referring to the Fata Morgana or complex cloud structures resembling skylines</note>, bring about auspiciousness.

### Special Portents and Calamities

Now he describes special characteristics:
A twilight that is "assailed" by birds, jackals <note>original: "shiva," referring here to the female jackal whose howl was an omen</note>, or forest deer facing the sun and crying out fiercely, or one joined with "staffs" <note>vertical light pillars</note>, "bolts" <note>horizontal cloud bars</note>, and other such phenomena, or one joined with daily distortions of the sun—such a twilight leads to the destruction of the welfare of the country and its ruler.

<margin>The appearance of "Gandharva Cities" <note>mirages</note> or rainbows at dawn indicates impending change.</margin>

As the sage **Parashara** <note>A legendary Vedic seer and author of early astronomical/astrological treatises</note> says:
"The effects of the twilight should be predicted for the region within a distance of ten yojanas <note>roughly 80-90 miles</note>. If the twilight is joined with rays, it lasts seven nights; if it is windless and clear, it indicates..." <unclear>the text breaks off</unclear>

<summary>This page details the meteorological omens derived from solar halos and the shapes of clouds at sunrise and sunset, categorizing them as either auspicious for the kingdom or portents of royal ruin. It cites the sage Parashara to establish the geographical range and duration of these atmospheric effects.</summary>

<keywords>halos, twilight clouds, Parashara, omens, weather lore, kingship, military strength, atmospheric phenomena</keywords>

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of atmospheric omens and meteorological forecasting, specifically focusing on the interpretation of the twilight sky (Sandhyā) and its effects on the kingdom and environment.</meta>

# Page 117

When that twilight is clear and undisturbed, it is to be understood as a favorable union <note>yoga</note>. If it appears contrary, starting from the Brahmins <note>the priestly class</note> down to the lowest, objects will be harmed. In that same twilight, if it is circular, red, or green, there is a danger of war <term>shastra-kopa: literally "the anger of weapons"</term>. If it is the color of madder <note>a reddish-purple dye</note> or yellowish, there is disease among four-legged animals. If it is dark-brown, there is fear of thieves. If it is blue-yellow, there is a plague among cattle. If it is green, the death of the first minister by a weapon is indicated. If it is the color of a bud, there will be rain. If it is blue-red, there is drought. If it is dark-gray, it indicates the destruction of grain. If it is reddish-brown just as the sun has set, or excessively bright with heat-like radiance, one should expect rain. If it is scorched-white in that same form, the death of the King or the Commander-in-Chief will occur. If it is the color of wind <note>dusty or smoky</note> or blue, and in that same middle-period it turns red, there will be rain, but it will be destructive to limbs. Likewise, yellow and madder colors lead to the death of those who live by fire <note>blacksmiths, priests, etc.</note>. A golden-white twilight leads to prosperity and security <term>yoga-kshema: the preservation of what is earned and the acquisition of what is needed</term>.

And here it is said: A mock sun <term>pratisūrya</term>, Indra’s bow <note>a rainbow</note>, a staff-like cloud, a halo <term>parivesha</term>, the "Airavata" <note>a specific type of rainbow named after the celestial elephant</note>, and beautiful rays at the end of the day, lightning, and rays that are not harsh—if these colors are seen moving clockwise in the twilight, they give the fruit of immediate rain.

And so says the Sage Kashyapa <note>A legendary Vedic sage often cited in technical treatises on architecture and omens</note>:

> The twilights at the end of day and night are to be observed according to their true nature.
> Whether auspicious or inauspicious, the result begins from that moment.
> A sky free of impurities, directions that are clear, a glow like a ruby,
> A wind that blows fragrant, pleasant, soft, and cool—
> Such a twilight is to be known as auspicious; the opposite is considered inauspicious.
> When the twilight is decorated with the three-fold "staff," a halo, and the bow of the gods <note>rainbow</note>,
> It should be known as a giver of immediate rain, but also an increaser of disease.

<insert>Now, he speaks to define the timing of the results for these described twilight characteristics:</insert>

The morning twilight gives its fruit (good or bad) at that very moment. The evening twilight gives its fruit at night. If the result does not occur then, halos, dust, and "cross-bars" <term>parigha: horizontal clouds crossing the sun</term> produce results immediately; if not immediately, then within seven days. Likewise, the sun’s rays, the bow of Indra <note>rainbow</note>, lightning, the mock sun, clouds, and winds—these produce results on that very day. Birds and deer of seven types also indicate this.

The morning twilight <note>Prācī</note> produces its fruit, whether good or bad, at that very time. But not so the other. The other, the western or second twilight, produces its fruit at night. When it is said "if the fruit does not occur," it means if the twilight's result is not seen at the stated time, then it happens within three days. Halos of the sun and moon, dust, and "cross-bars"—which are cloud-lines situated horizontally at sunrise or sunset—these, when seen, produce results on that very day. The word "if" <note>original: "cet"</note> is used in the sense of a condition. If they do not produce it immediately, then they produce the good or bad result within a week (seven days). "Likewise" refers to the sun's rays (such as the "unfailing" rays), the rainbow, lightning, the mock sun, and the winds.

<summary>This page details the omens derived from the colors and phenomena of the twilight sky, predicting weather, social upheaval, and the health of the kingdom. It also provides a specific chronological framework for when these meteorological predictions are expected to manifest.</summary>
<keywords>twilight omens, meteorology, Kashyapa, rainbow, mock sun, rain prediction, social classes, ancient Indian science</keywords>

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of meteorological omens and their geographical influence, specifically focusing on the distance at which atmospheric phenomena produce effects.</meta>

All of these [omens] produce their good or bad results immediately; if they do not act immediately, then they do so on the twelfth day. The word "or" indicates an alternative: the seven types of wild animals <note>Sanskrit: *mṛga*, referring here to forest creatures used in divination</note> produce their sevenfold results, whether auspicious or inauspicious, without delay.

### The Explanation
In this manner, the glow of twilight illuminates a distance of one *yojana* <note>A *yojana* is a traditional Indian measure of distance, roughly 8 to 9 miles</note>. Lightning strikes illuminate six *yojanas*. The sound of thunder travels for fifteen—no, some say the sound does not carry that far in certain falls. 

By the light of twilight, one *yojana* is illuminated; therefore, the omen is effective within that one *yojana* radius. Similarly, a lightning strike illuminates six *yojanas* with its flash; it is effective for six *yojanas*. The thunder of the clouds travels five *yojanas*; it is effective within a five-*yojana* range. Some followers of Devala <note>An ancient sage and authority on astrology/law</note> say that in the case of a strike, the boundary is universal—it gives results everywhere.

**As Devala says:**
> Twilight shines for one *yojana*, lightning for six;
> The sound of clouds gives results for five *yojanas*.
> All these mentioned are givers of fruit, whether good or bad.

### On Halos and Rainbows
Now the middle [distances] are explained:
Each "mock sun" <note>Sanskrit: *pratisūrya*, a parhelion or atmospheric optical phenomenon</note> has a halo with a light of three *yojanas*. Halos are of five types. The solar halo circle [shines for] six or five, and the bow of the Lord of the Immortals <note>Indra's Bow, i.e., the rainbow</note> shines for ten *yojanas*.

The sun known as the "mock sun" has a halo with a light of three *yojanas*; its brilliance is seen for three *yojanas*, meaning it is effective within that distance. Halos are seen up to five *yojanas* and are effective therein. The halo circle is seen at six or five *yojanas* and gives results only within that measure. The rainbow of Indra—the Lord of the Immortals—appears for ten *yojanas*; this means its power extends for ten *yojanas*.

-> **Thus ends the Twenty-Ninth Chapter, titled "Signs of Twilight," in the Commentary on the Samhita composed by Bhattotpala.** <-

---

### Signs of the Burning of the Quarters
Now the characteristics of the "Burning of the Quarters" <note>Sanskrit: *Digdāha*, referring to an unnatural atmospheric glow or wildfire-like redness in the sky</note> are explained. First, he describes the results based on the different colors:

**A yellow sky brings fear to kings; a fire-colored sky brings destruction to the country; a tawny-red sky with a wind blowing from the left destroys the harvest.**

"Burning of the Quarters" refers to the sky appearing to be on fire. If it is **yellow**, there is fear for kings (monarchs). If it is **fire-colored** (the color of a sacrificial fire), it leads to the destruction or ruin of the country or district. If it is **tawny-red** (reddish) and accompanied by a wind from the left <note>Sanskrit: *apasavya*, an inauspicious direction in ritual contexts</note> while looking toward the sun, it causes the destruction of the harvest.

Next, he says:
That which creates an intense light through its brilliance, even casting shadows like the sun, causes the division of the kingdom and fear of armed conflict...

That direction which glows with an intense, fire-like brilliance creates a luminous light. It occurs like the sun; just as the sun creates shadows, this glow also produces shadows of visible objects. This signifies a form of royal calamity <note>original: "rajopastyamurupyam"; likely referring to an omen of distress for the sovereign</note>. If the glow is blood-red—meaning dark red—it causes the provocation of weapons <note>i.e., war or armed conflict</note.

### Results in All Directions

Now, he describes the results according to all the cardinal directions:

In the **East**, it affects the <term>Kshatriyas</term> <note>the warrior and ruling caste</note> along with the lords of men. In the **South-East**, there is suffering for artisans and young princes. In the **South**, <term>Vaishyas</term> <note>the merchant and agricultural caste</note> are afflicted by fierce men and disease. In the **South-West**, there is suffering for remarried women <note>original: "punarbhu"; a woman who marries a second time</note>. In the **West**, the <term>Shudras</term> <note>the laboring caste</note> and those who live by agriculture suffer. In the **North-West**, there is suffering caused by thieves and horses. In the **North**, the <term>Brahmins</term> <note>the priestly and scholarly caste</note> suffer. For the **North-East**, he indicates fear for heretics <note>original: "pakhandino"; those outside the Vedic fold</note> and traders.

In the East (the forward direction), a seen glow of the directions causes suffering for the Kshatriya caste and the rulers of men, as well as those living in their kingdoms. Likewise, in the South-East (the direction of Agni), it causes suffering for artisans—such as blacksmiths and goldsmiths—and for young princes. In the South (the direction of Yama), the Vaishya castes meet with suffering and disease caused by fierce thieves. In the South-West corner, remarried women and young women meet with suffering. <note>The text here provides a grammatical derivation for "Punarbhu," explaining it as one who is "born again" or "taken again" in marriage according to specific rites.</note> In the West, the Shudra castes and those living by farming meet with suffering. In the North-West (the direction of Vayu), the glow causes suffering through thieves and horses. In the North, the Brahmins meet with suffering. For heretics and the merchant classes, one should point to fear. <insert>In the South-East for princes; in the South for Vaishyas; in the South-West for peace/remedy; in the West for Shudras; in the North-West fear of thieves; in the North for Brahmins; and in the North-East, suffering for heretics and merchants.</insert>

### Auspicious Signs

Now, he describes the auspicious characteristics:

If the sky is clear and spotless, the stars are bright, and the wind blows in a clockwise direction <note>original: "pradakshina"; moving from left to right, considered lucky</note>; and if the glow of the directions is as pure as gold—shining like gold—then it exists for the welfare and benefit of the King and the people of the realm.

# Now, the *Sasyajataka* <note>The Birth of Crops; a treatise on agricultural astrology</note> is explained.
## Here, at the very beginning, it is stated to demonstrate the traditional scriptural authority.

Regarding the entry of the Sun into Scorpio and Taurus: the auspicious and inauspicious combinations for summer and monsoon crops, as spoken by Badarayana <note>An ancient sage and authority on astrology and philosophy</note>, have been adopted here. This is what was said:

When the Sun—the solar deity—enters Scorpio and Taurus respectively; specifically, upon the entry into Scorpio for summer crops <note>original: *graishma-sasya*; refers to crops harvested in summer or the monsoon cycle</note> such as barley and the like, and upon the entry into Taurus for autumn crops <note>original: *sharad-sasya*</note> such as rice and the like. The auspicious and inauspicious combinations <term>yoga: planetary alignments</term> taught by the teacher Badarayana are what I have set forth here.

This is the meaning: On the day the Sun’s transit into Scorpio <term>Sankranti: the moment the Sun enters a new zodiac sign</term> occurs, one should designate Scorpio itself as the Ascendant <term>Lagna: the rising sign</term> and investigate the "birth" (prospects) of the summer crops according to the method to be described. Similarly, at the time of the Taurus transit, one should designate Taurus as the Ascendant and investigate the birth of the autumn crops.

## Now, he speaks to demonstrate the combinations for that alone:

When the Sun enters Scorpio <note>original: *ali-pravesha*</note>, if the angular houses <term>Kendra: the 1st, 4th, 7th, and 10th houses from the Sun</term> are occupied by benefics <note>original: *saumya*; literally "gentle" planets like Mercury, Jupiter, and Venus</note> or are aspected <note>viewed through astrological sight</note> by powerful benefics, there will be an increase in the summer crops.

This is the analysis: At the time of the Sun's entry into Scorpio, if the angular houses from that position are occupied by benefics—namely Mercury, Jupiter, and Venus—the growth of summer crops is indicated. Here, the position of the Sun's entry into Scorpio is specified as the starting point. Thus, it is said: when the angular houses from the Sun are occupied by or joined with benefic planets, there is an increase in summer crops and good rainfall.

Alternatively, if the angular houses of the Sun are occupied by benefics, or if the Sun is aspected by powerful benefics situated elsewhere, there is an increase in summer crops. Because the teacher <insert>Badarayana</insert> is fond of concise and friendly expression, he uses the phrase "aspected by powerful benefics." From this, we understand that if the angular houses are joined by benefics—whether powerful or weak—there is growth. But if powerful benefics aspect from the trines <note>the 5th or 9th houses</note> or the house of gains <note>the 11th house</note>, then there is a *great* increase in the summer crops. If they are weak, there is only a slight increase.

As Badarayana says:
*“When the Sun is in Scorpio and is aspected by powerful benefics, or when those same benefics are in the angular houses, one should predict an abundance of summer crops.”*

## Now, he mentions another combination:
When the Sun is in the eighth sign, and Jupiter and the Moon are in Aquarius... <note>The text cuts off here</note>

->**The Success of Crops**<-

When Jupiter and the Moon are situated in Leo, or one is in Leo and the other in Aquarius <note>literally "the pot-house"</note>, there is a successful production of ground crops. When the Sun has reached the eighth sign—meaning when it is situated in Scorpio—and Jupiter and the Moon are respectively in Aquarius and Leo (Jupiter in Aquarius and the Moon in Leo), or alternatively in Leo and Aquarius (Jupiter in Leo and the Moon in Aquarius), even then, the production of the first crops <term>prathama-sasya</term><note>The first seasonal harvest</note> occurs.

Now, another planetary combination is described:
When Venus is in the second house from the Sun, and Mercury is in the third, or if both are together in the second, or even if both are in the twelfth house from the Sun, the production of crops occurs if they are aspected by Jupiter. Specifically, from the Sun situated in Scorpio, if Venus is in the second and Mercury is in the third, or if both Mercury and Venus are together in the second, or in the same manner if they are in the first <insert>twelfth</insert> house from the Sun, there is a production of grains. If they are aspected by Jupiter—that is, when Jupiter looks upon the Sun—then there is an exceeding production of crops. As the sage Badarayana <note>A celebrated ancient authority often identified with Vyasa</note> says: "When Mercury or Venus are in the second from the Sun, or both together, or even in the twelfth house, such production occurs; it is even greater when aspected by Jupiter."

Now, another planetary combination is described:
When the Sun is between benefics, and Jupiter and the Moon are in the seventh house, there is excellent prosperity. If the Sun is at the end or beginning of a sign and Jupiter is in the second, there is half-production. When the Sun is in Scorpio and placed between benefics—meaning one of either Mercury or Venus is in the second house and the other is in the twelfth from Scorpio—and from the Sun so situated, Jupiter and the Moon are in the seventh house, there is the highest prosperity of crops. "At the end or beginning" means if the Sun is at the very start of Scorpio and Jupiter is in the second house, then a half-production of crops occurs; meaning only half the expected yield is produced.

Now, another planetary combination is described:
With planets in the eleventh, fourth, and second houses, this result is produced: When Venus is in the eleventh, and Mercury is in the fourth and second, there is the highest prosperity of crops. In this same yoga <note>planetary combination</note>, if Jupiter is situated in the tenth house <term>karmani</term>, there is not just prosperity but the ultimate abundance, as much as one could desire; meaning an abundance of grain.

Now, another planetary combination is described:
If Jupiter is in Aquarius, the Moon in Taurus, and Mars and Saturn are at the beginning of Scorpio and in Capricorn, there is production, but later fear of foreign armies and disease. When the Sun enters Scorpio, if Jupiter is situated in Aquarius, the Moon in Taurus, and Mars and the Sun <insert>Saturn</insert> are at the beginning of Scorpio and in Capricorn—in such a combination, there is indeed a production of crops, but afterwards, there arises fear caused by an enemy's wheel <note>an invading army</note> and fear of disease.

Now, another planetary combination is described:
When the Sun is between three malefic planets, the crops... <unclear>text breaks off</unclear>
