<meta>This page continues the study of Samudrika Shastra (physiognomy), specifically focusing on the predictive meaning of "pitaka" (marks, moles, or eruptions) on the lower body and extremities. It maintains the structure of stating a general rule followed by a supporting quotation from the sage Parashara.</meta>

Marks appearing on the back and the pair of arms respectively bring about the destruction of a collection of sorrows and the destruction of enemies. Those born on the back destroy a "collection of sorrows" <note>original: duḥkha-niścayasya</note>, meaning a great heap of grief. Those born on the pair of arms destroy a "collection of enemies" <note>original: ari-samūhasya</note>. Marks appearing on the wrist <term>manibandha: the joint where the hand meets the forearm</term> indicate a binding or restriction of the hands. Those born on the upper arms <insert>the region between the shoulder and elbow</insert> bring about the gift of garments and ornaments.

**As stated by Parashara:**
"On the two sides, they bring sorrow; on the chest, a loss of limbs; on the head, one or more bring great pain. On the two breasts, they indicate the gain of a son. On the arms, the destruction of enemies. On the wrist, they signify the gift of clothes, residence, and ornaments, or perhaps restraint."

### On Eruptions and Marks
Marks on the fingers, the belly, and the navel bring wealth, good fortune, and grief respectively. Below the navel, they indicate the theft of wealth by thieves. On the pelvis, they bring wealth and grain. On the testicles, they bring good fortune and the birth of sons.

To explain in sequence: Marks on the fingers bring an abundance of wealth. Those on the belly <note>original: udara</note> bring grief or sorrow. On the navel <note>original: nābhi</note>, they signify "excellent drink and food" <note>original: supānānna</note>, meaning high-quality nourishment. Below the navel, the appearance of a mark indicates the loss of wealth to thieves or robbers. On the pelvis <note>original: vasti</note>, they grant wealth and grain. On the testicles <note>original: vṛṣaṇa</note>, they grant good fortune and the birth of a son. On the anus <note>original: guda</note>, they grant wealth and financial gain.

**As stated by Parashara:**
"Wealth comes from the hands; good fortune from the fingers; sorrow from the belly. The navel brings the acquisition of food and drink. Below the belly, the taking of wealth by thieves. On the pelvis, the acquisition of wealth and grain. On the testicles, good fortune, the birth of a son, and the gain of a woman. On the anus, good fortune."

---

### On Eruptions and Marks
On the thighs, they bring the gain of vehicles and women. On the knees, they signify action against enemies. On the calves, <margin>Note: destruction</margin> they signify injury by weapons. On the ankles, they bring imprisonment and the giving of distress.

To explain: On the thighs <note>original: ūru</note>, they grant the gain of vehicles <note>original: yāna</note> and the "acquisition of a woman" <note>original: strī-prāpti</note>. On the knees <note>original: jānu</note>, they signify the destruction of enemy people. On the calves <note>original: jaṅghā</note>, they signify destruction by a weapon. Located on the ankles <note>original: gulpha</note>, they are "givers of distress" <note>original: kleśa-dāyinaḥ</note>, meaning they bring difficult times and the bondage of chains.

**As stated by Parashara:**
"Gain of vehicles and women from the thighs; from the knees, the destruction of enemies. From the calves, injury by weapons. From the ankles, the arrival of great distress and the binding of the feet."

---

### On Eruptions and Marks
Those born on the buttocks, the heels, and the feet signify the loss of wealth and constant wandering. On the fingers <insert>of the feet</insert>, they signify bondage. On the back of the feet, they signify honor from one's kinsmen.

Marks born on the buttocks <term>sphik: the fleshy part of the hips</term>, the heels, and the feet are as follows...

# Shalihotra

A bull whose beautiful eyes are encircled by a ring of white hair is known as *Mallikaksha* <note>literally "Jasmine-eyed"</note>. Such a creature is highly auspicious, bringing great fortune, and possesses pupils that are clear and steady. By these characteristics, the *Mallikaksha* is identified. Furthermore, his eyes may resemble water bubbles—a well-known trait. Thus, those with eyes like cat’s-eye gems or jasmine-like bubbles are highly regarded. Their eyelids and the surrounding skin should be thick and firm. Their hooves should be like those of a donkey, well-proportioned, un-split, and lustrous. Such animals are entirely excellent and are capable of carrying heavy loads, as described in the Indian treatises on horses and cattle.

### The "Bharata" Type
Now, for example: one who has wrinkles on the bridge of the nose, a face resembling a cat's, and is white on the right side; whose complexion has the luster of a red or blue lotus or red lac; and who possesses speed equal to that of a swift horse. He has a hanging lip and a belly shaped like a cloud, with a curved chest <note>the "kroda" or chest/brisket area</note>. This type is known as *Bharata*; he is incomparable and yields excellent results.

To explain further: "wrinkles on the bridge of the nose" means the snout is lined with folds. "Cat-faced" means the mouth resembles that of a feline. "White on the right side" refers to the coloring on the right flank. "Luster of lotus and lac" refers to a reddish-blue glow. "Speed equal to a horse" means his gait is exceptionally fast. Even if he has a "cloud-belly" (a large, rounded abdomen) and a curved chest—referring to the specific curvature of the hind legs and brisket—he is still superior.

As stated in the *Shalihotra* text: "With a curved chest and great speed." This "chest" refers to the space between the forelegs. One who possesses such a curved chest and is capable of carrying heavy burdens is known to be equal to a horse in speed. Such a creature is a bringer of auspicious rewards.

### The "Hansa" Type
Now, for example: one who is white in color, has tawny eyes, and copper-colored horns and ears is called *Mahavajra* <note>literally "Great Thunderbolt"</note>. He is also named *Hansa* <note>"Swan"</note>; he brings auspicious results and increases the prosperity of whoever beholds him.

One who is white-colored with "tawny eyes" (brownish-yellow eyes) and "copper horns and ears" (reddish-tinted) is the *Mahavajra*. He has a sturdy face and is known as *Hansa*. He is highly praised as one who increases the wealth and status of his owner.

### The "Kalbhasha" Type
Now, for example: one with a flowing tail, copper horns, and blood-red eyes, who is of the *Kalbhasha* variety, quickly makes his master the lord of Fortune <note>Lakshmi, the goddess of wealth</note>.

The bull who has a "flowing tail" (a long, thick tail-brush), "copper horns" (reddish horns), and "blood-red eyes" (deep red eyes), and is *Kalbhasha*—which refers to a variegated color made of a mixture of white, red, and yellow—will very quickly make his owner a master of great riches.

<summary>This page describes specific auspicious types of bulls (Mallikaksha, Bharata, Hansa, and Kalbhasha) based on their eyes, color, and physical proportions, promising wealth to their owners. It draws parallels between the strength and speed of high-quality cattle and that of horses.</summary>

<keywords>Shalihotra, Mallikaksha, Bharata bull, Hansa bull, cattle omens, veterinary tradition, Sanskrit animal husbandry, auspicious marks</keywords>

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary of Bhattotpala on Varahamihira's Brihat Samhita. It concludes the chapter on the characteristics of cows and transitions into the chapter on dogs.</meta>

<page-num>233</page-num>

One who has white feet and various colors is also a bringer of inauspicious results; however, one of mixed results may be accepted if a perfectly auspicious specimen is not available. One who is joined with white, bright feet, and is of any desired color—even if possessing all signs, both good and bad—brings auspicious results. Some read this as "with feet that are not white." If a "perfectly auspicious" specimen (one possessing every single positive mark) is not found, then one of mixed results—meaning neither purely good nor purely bad—should be accepted. The word "also" (api) indicates possibility. Some read "if not perfectly auspicious" to mean that if the auspicious signs are not outweighed by the inauspicious ones after weighing them against each other, then that one of mixed results should be accepted. 

And so says <term>Parashara</term><note>An ancient sage and authority on agriculture and animal omens</note>: 
"Those with soft, elevated tips, rounded and well-rubbed horns; with eyes the color of cat's-eye gems or resembling water bubbles, with red corners and red pupils; with a mane like a lion, a great chest, and a visible back and hump; whose tail drags on the ground, with a long and thick dewlap; who are raised in the front and well-proportioned in the back; with rounded limbs, thick bodies, and broad hips; with smooth, copper-colored thin horns, and firm, closely-set hooves; with even skeletal structure and heels that do not touch the ground; with rounded, thick, and high necks and well-formed humps—these are to be known as capable of bearing loads and are honored for pulling vehicles. One with very long testicles and a belly like a black cloud, with a broad chest and hips, is honored for bearing loads. One who is smooth, of a tawny-yellow color, white, with copper horns and evenness, and rounded limbs, is named 'Padmaka'—he increases the wealth of the cattle-owner."

Thus ends the sixty-ninth chapter, named "Characteristics of Cows," in the *Samhita-vivritti* composed by the illustrious **Bhattotpala**.

# Now, the Chapter on the Characteristics of Dogs

In this regard, the characteristics are first stated in a single verse:
"One who has five nails on three feet, but the right [front] foot has six nails; who has copper-colored lips and nose; who has the gait of a lion and walks smelling the ground; whose tail is hairy and curved; whose eyes are like a crocodile's; whose ears are hanging and soft—such a dog, being kept, quickly brings prosperity to the house."

A dog who has three feet (legs) possessing five nails each, while the right foot is joined with six nails; who has copper-colored lips and nose; who has a <term>lion-like gait</term><note>mrigeshvara-gati: a majestic, powerful stride</note>; who goes along smelling the earth; whose tail is described as "hairy" (satatam), meaning shaggy or bushy; whose sight is like a crocodile's (makara-drishi); whose ears are hanging or pendulous; such a dog, called "the mouse-catcher" (akhu-kura), quickly removes all misfortune for the protector (owner) and brings <term>Shri</term><note>Goddess of wealth and prosperity</note>.

<summary>This page concludes the omens regarding cattle by quoting the sage Parashara and begins the study of canine omens, describing the specific physical traits of a dog that brings good fortune to a household.</summary>

# Astrology

...he acts. And so says Garga <note>An ancient sage and authority on astrology and omens</note>: "Three feet, five nails, and a rightward turn; others are of a dark color, and he has the gait of a lion. He arrives with a fragrance like madder <note>a red dye plant</note>, and his tail is tangled. Fearless, proud, and with hanging ears... he protects the king and the people."

### Now he describes the characteristics of the bitch:

"She who has five nails on each foot, or six nails on the left foot, is called *Mallika* <note>literally 'Jasmine'</note>. She has a curved tail, is reddish-brown, has long ears, and is well-nourished; such a bitch protects the kingdom." 

On whichever bitch there are five nails (*kararuha*) on each and every foot; or, on the fourth or left foot there are six nails; and who is a *Mallika*—meaning she has markings resembling the Jasmine flower—such a *Mallika* is of this kind. She whose tail (*langula*) is curved or crooked, who is reddish-brown (*kapila*), and has long ears, and is well-nourished—she protects the nation and the king.

**Thus ends the sixtieth chapter, named "Characteristics of Horses," in the Commentary on the Samhita composed by Bhattotpala.**

### Now the Chapter on the Characteristics of Roosters is considered.
### Therein, he first describes the characteristics of the rooster:

> A rooster that is white like silver, with copper-colored nails and a curved crest, who crows loudly and sweetly at dawn, brings prosperity to the king, the nation, and the horses.

A rooster that is white-colored, resembling the color of silver, with distinct nails and wings, and toes like the branches of a peacock flower; having copper-colored nails, a copper-colored face, and a copper-colored crest (*chulika*); being white and crowing with a pleasant, sweet sound at the break of dawn—he bestows prosperity (*vriddhi*) upon the king, the nation (*janapada*), and the horses (*vajinam*).

And so says Garga: "White ones with copper nails are best; those with copper eyes, a thin tail, straight toes, well-formed bodies, and copper crests are praised. Those with long necks, the color of curd, and auspicious faces are praised; also those with thick heads and yellow feet. Those with broken voices, or who are discolored and discordant, are condemned."

### Now he describes the characteristics of the goat:

> One with a neck like barley, or resembling a jujube fruit, with a large head and many colors, or the color of honey and flowers—such a bird is auspicious for victory. One who is thin or has broken feet is not.

The bird (*vihaga*) whose neck is like barley—commonly known as "barley-headed"—or one who is red like a ripe jujube fruit (*badira*); with a large head and possessed of many various colors such as white, red, blue, yellow, and black; who is bright and clean—such a rooster is auspicious...

<meta>This page continues the commentary by Bhattotpala on Varahamihira's Brihat Samhita. The previous section likely concluded the discussion on the characteristics of roosters, transitioning here into the specific traits of hens and then moving into the chapter on tortoises.</meta>

# Now, the characteristics of the hen are described:

A hen is considered auspicious if she has a wound-like mark <note>original: "vrana"</note> or if she is honey-colored <note>original: "madhu-varna"</note>, which means yellow-colored. A honey-colored hen with the dark hue of a large bee <note>original: "bhramara-varna"</note> is also known to bring success. Any hen other than those described is not auspicious and does not bring good fortune. One that is emaciated, sickly, or has a broken or deformed foot <note>original: "vikalapada"</note> is also not auspicious.

**Now, the specific characteristics of a hen are stated:**

**"A hen that is joyful, soft to the touch, pleasant-voiced, of steady build, and possesses a beautiful face—she grants for a very long time the wealth of play, fortune, fame, victory, and heroic power."**

A hen that is joyful, endowed with softness, speaks with a beautiful and pleasant sound, has a steady and well-formed body, and a beautiful face—meaning one whose face and limbs are lovely to look at—she gives to the King for a very long time Lakshmi <note>the goddess of fortune</note>, fame, victory (specifically, triumph over enemies), and the abundance of heroic power and sovereignty.

**Thus ends the sixty-first chapter, titled "Characteristics of the Hen," in the Commentary on the Samhita composed by Bhattotpala. || 61 ||**

---

# Now, the characteristics of the tortoise are explained:

Regarding this, it is said: **"A tortoise that is the color of crystal or silver, variegated with blue lines, shaped like a water-pot, and possessing a beautiful spine; or one with a reddish body variegated with mustard-seed-shaped spots—such a tortoise, when kept in a palace, brings greatness to all kings."**

A tortoise that is crystal-colored (shining like a quartz stone), or silver-colored (having the appearance of silver), or variegated with blue-colored lines or streaks, or whose body is shaped like a water-jar <note>original: "kalasha"</note>, and has a beautiful spine <note>original: "charu-vamsha" - referring to a well-defined backbone that is not too high</note>; or alternatively, one with a reddish body <note>original: "aruna-vapu"</note> decorated with white spots the size of mustard seeds—if such a tortoise stays in the residence, it creates greatness and lordship for all kings. This means it makes one a King of Kings <note>original: "rajadhiraja"</note>.

**Furthermore, it is said: "One with a body as dark as collyrium or a bee, variegated with spots, with a complete body, a head like a serpent, and a wide neck—such a tortoise also leads to the great increase of a king's territory."**

Collyrium <note>original: "anjana"</note> is well-known as eye-black; a bee <note>original: "shringa"</note> refers to the large black bee. One whose body is dark like collyrium or a bee, variegated with spots or marks, and has an "unmutilated" body <note>original: "avyanga-sharira" - meaning a body with all limbs complete and perfect</note>, a head resembling that of a serpent, and a wide, expansive neck—such a tortoise exists for the increase and expansion of the kings' kingdom and nation.

**Furthermore, it is said: "One with the luster of a cat's-eye gem, a thick neck, a triangular frame, variegated, and with a beautiful spine is praised. Such a tortoise, full of enjoyments, should be kept in pleasure-ponds for the sake of auspiciousness by rulers of men."**

Luster like a cat's-eye gem <note>original: "vaidurya"</note>, a thick neck, a triangular shape like a water-chestnut <note>original: "shringataka"</note>, variegated in color, and possessing a beautiful, well-aligned spine—such a tortoise is excellent. Such a tortoise should be kept by "rulers of men" (kings) in pleasure-pavilions or ponds for the sake of auspiciousness...

Therefore, the third section discusses the navel and the waist. They say the fifth part describes the belly, the sixth the back, and the seventh the flesh. The eighth describes the lips and the cavity of the mouth. The ninth describes the eyes and the forehead, and the tenth the head. These are the ten parts of the body's structure. The five great elements <note>The Mahabhutas: Earth, Water, Fire, Air, and Ether</note> within the body reveal auspicious results through these signs, as shown by those skilled in the knowledge of characteristics <term>Lakshana-jna: an expert in reading bodily signs</term>. The quality of one's inner fire and intelligence is also revealed, acting like the light of a lamp.

As stated by **Namiputra**: "The characteristics of a firm body are proclaimed. As far as the shadow and luster extend, they are said to be of six kinds. The skin's beauty is clear, lovely, radiant, and illuminating."

->**The Characteristics of the Feet**<-

Here the Teacher explains the characteristics starting from the feet up to the hair of the head. First, he describes the signs of the feet:

"The feet of a lord of men <note>original: manujeśvara; referring to a king or a high-ranking leader</note> are free from sweat, have soft soles, and possess the internal glow of a lotus. The toes are well-set and not gaped, the nails are reddish and curved. The arches are high, free of visible veins, with hidden ankles and a shape raised like the back of a tortoise."

Such feet belong to a man who is a great ruler. **Free from sweat** means devoid of perspiration. **Soft soles** means possessing a gentle, supple texture on the bottom of the feet. **Internal glow of a lotus** means the inner part of the foot has a luster or complexion similar to the interior of a lotus petal. **Well-set toes** means the toes are closely aligned and fleshy, not widely separated. **Reddish nails** <note>original: tamra-nakha</note> refers to a copper-like healthy glow of the nails. Some interpret the phrase to mean the sides of the feet are well-proportioned. **Hidden ankles** means the ankle bones are deep-set and not protruding. **Raised like a tortoise** means the upper surface of the foot is curved and high, resembling the shell of a tortoise. Such are the feet of a lord of men—a king among people.

So says **Samudra** <note>The mythical founder of Samudrika Shastra</note>: "With feet that are even, glossy, well-fleshed, beautiful, elevated, sweatless, and without visible veins, one becomes a king of the earth."

And so says **Garga** <note>An ancient sage and astronomer</note>: "Those whose soles resemble the red lotus, the blue lotus, or the color of gold are to be known as kings; those with contrary signs do not share that fate."

Now he describes the inauspicious signs:

"Feet that are shaped like a winnowing basket, dry, pale, crooked, covered in veins, fleshless, and with widely spaced toes bring poverty and sorrow. If they are the color of wood or astringent-brown, they indicate the end of one's lineage; if the soles are yellow, one is prone to forbidden conduct."

Such feet are **shaped like a winnowing basket** <note>original: surpa-akara; wide at the front and narrow at the back, considered a sign of instability</note>, meaning excessively wide and thin. **Pale** means they have a white, ghostly appearance. **Crooked** means they are distorted or long. **Covered in veins** means the veins are bulging and visible. **Fleshless** means lacking muscle and substance. **Widely spaced toes** means there are gaps between the toes. Such feet grant poverty (lack of wealth) and suffering.

As **Samudra** says: "One with feet shaped like a winnowing basket, crooked, dry, veined, sweaty, pale, or rough is extremely miserable. If the path of the lines..."

<summary>This page details the physiognomy of the feet, contrasting the "royal" foot (soft, lotus-colored, tortoise-arched) with the "impoverished" foot (veined, dry, and basket-shaped). It cites ancient authorities like Garga and Samudra to validate these divinatory signs.</summary>

# Page 241

Regarding the ankles: Those whose ankles are raised and prominent from the middle, shaped like a <term>paduka</term> <note>a traditional wooden wedge-sandal</note>, are devoted to wealth and travel. However, ankles that are the color of <term>kashaya</term> <note>a dark, reddish-brown astringent decoction</note> or blackish-red, and those with prominent veins, are destructive and bring ruin. If they are well-rounded or have the appearance and luster of soft lotus stalks, they are auspicious; if they are yellow or pale in color, it signifies a man who is subservient to women.

As stated by **Samudra** <note>The mythical sage or authority on the science of body marks</note>:
*Raised ankles lead to constant travel; reddish-brown ankles destroy the lineage. Soft, lotus-like ankles are best, while yellow ones lead to subservience.*

### On the Characteristics of the Shanks, Thighs, and Knees

Those whose shanks are covered with hair that is not sparse, but fine and rounded, and whose thighs resemble the trunk of an elephant <note>original: "dvirada-kara", meaning elephant-hand/trunk</note>, are fortunate. Those with well-fleshed and symmetrical knees are likewise blessed. Conversely, those with shanks like a jackal are destitute of wealth.

To clarify: "Not sparse" means thick; "fine" means soft hair. Those with perfectly circular shanks and thighs resembling an elephant's trunk, combined with fleshy, even knees, are destined for success. Those with shanks like dogs or jackals are poor and miserable.

As stated by **Samudra**:
*Through excellent shanks, one points to lordship; those with jackal-shanks are afflicted by sorrow and are middle-class in wealth.*

### On the Characteristics of Body Hair

One hair per follicle belongs to kings <note>original: "parthivanam"</note>. Two hairs per follicle are known to belong to scholars and those learned in the Vedas <note>original: "shrotriyas"</note>. Those with more than that are penniless and partakers of misery. The hair of the head is also judged by this same standard.

One hair in each pore makes a man a king. Two hairs growing from a single pore belong to the wise and the performers of sacred rites. With "disturbing" hair—meaning three or four hairs per pore—men become destitute and suffer. In this manner, the growth of hair is either condemned as censurable or honored as auspicious.

*One hair: a king. Two hairs: great fame. Three or more hairs: a man devoid of fortune.*

### On the Characteristics of the Knees

He whose knees are fleshless dies in a foreign land. Small knees bring good fortune, while distorted or large knees lead to poverty.
<margin>241</margin>
Those with sunken knees are conquered by women; those with fleshy knees attain royalty; and those with very large knees have long life.

"Fleshless" means devoid of muscle; such a person dies while traveling on a road. "Small" means thin or petite, which brings luck. "Distorted" means excessively wide or misshapen, leading to being penniless. Those with "sunken" or uneven knees are dominated by their wives. In summary, knees well-covered in flesh signify a kingdom or kingship, and "great" or thick knees signify a long lifespan.

As stated by **Samudra**:
*He whose knees are fleshless dies in exile; with small knees, one finds good fortune.*

It emphasizes that "elephant-trunk" thighs and single-hair follicles are signs of royalty, while "jackal-like" legs indicate poverty.</summary>

...sale and poverty. One who is conquered by women <note>meaning henpecked or overly lustful</note> will be so; likewise, a ruler with low or depressed features joined with others. With very thick features, he lives for a long time joined with righteousness.

## Now he describes the characteristics of the penis

With a small penis, one has little wealth and is childless. Even with a thick one, if it is bent to the left, he is destitute and devoid of sons and wealth. If it is bent to the right, he possesses sons. If it is bent downwards, there is poverty. One with a penis covered in a network of veins has very few sons. If it is thick and joined with knots <note>possibly referring to anatomical irregularities or cysts</note>, he becomes a sufferer of diseases. A soft penis leads to an end caused by urinary disorders <term>Prameha: a group of urinary diseases including diabetes</term> and similar ailments of the lustful.

With a very small penis, one becomes wealthy but remains childless—devoid of sons. With an excessively thick penis, one becomes poor and destitute. In this matter, if the <unclear>penis</unclear> is bent to the left, he is devoid of sons and wealth. If it is bent to the right, he possesses sons. If the lower part is bent, there is poverty and lack of wealth. With a multitude of veins, there are very few sons. If joined with a thick knot, he is afflicted by diseases. With a soft penis, he meets his end through urinary disorders and the cycles of desire-born diseases; this is the meaning.

**Here he describes the characteristics of the sheath:**
When the penis is hidden within a sheath <note>retracted or well-covered by skin</note>, they become kings and the like. Those with broken or irregular features are devoid of wealth. Those whose penis is straight, rounded, and has small veins are wealthy. When the penis is skillfully covered by the sheath of skin, they become kings. If the penis is long and quivering, they are devoid of wealth and poor. Those whose penis is straight and clearly rounded, and those with small veins—meaning the penis has few veins—are wealthy and possessed of riches.

**And so says Samudra <note>The mythical sage Samudra, to whom the science of physiognomy, Samudrika Shastra, is attributed</note>:**
He whose penis turns to the right becomes a man with sons. One that turns to the left produces daughters and has little wealth. Men with thick penises covered in veins are partakers of poverty. Men with straight and rounded penises are partakers of happiness. He whose penis touches the ground while he is sitting on his feet <note>squatting</note> should be known as miserable and a partaker of poverty. But he whose penis touches the ground while his feet are placed on the earth <note>likely implying a different posture or exceptional length</note> is to be known as one who enjoys pleasures; likewise one with a small penis. If the penis is joined with thick knots, a man becomes a partaker of great death <note>severe illness</note>. A man with a penis like a sword dies afflicted by the "wheel" <note>possibly a specific disease or weapon</note>.

# Now he describes the characteristics of the testicles

One with a single testicle dies by water. With uneven testicles, one is fickle toward women. With even testicles, one is a king. With raised testicles, life is short. With hanging testicles, the lifespan is a hundred years.

One with a single testicle meets a "water-death"—he dies in the midst of water. With uneven or unequal testicles, he becomes fickle toward women, being very lustful. With even and equal testicles, he becomes a king. With raised testicles—those attached high up—he has a short life and brief living. He whose testicles are hanging or pendulous has a lifespan of a hundred years; he lives for a hundred years.

<summary>This page details the "Samudrika Shastra" (physiognomy) regarding male genitalia, correlating physical traits like shape, vein patterns, and symmetry with a man's future wealth, fertility, and lifespan.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of physiognomy and bodily marks from the previous section, specifically focusing on the characteristics of the male anatomy as interpreted through the Samudrika Shastra (the tradition of reading bodily signs).</meta>

<page-num>242</page-num>

<margin>242</margin>And so says the Sage Samudra <note>The traditional mythical author of the Samudrika Shastra, the science of body marks</note>: "One who has a single testicle <note>original: "Ekando"</note> will meet a death by water; one with uneven testicles is very restless. One with symmetrical testicles becomes a lord of men, while one with small testicles has a short life. A man whose testicles are long and symmetrical lives for a hundred years."

Now he describes the defects and characteristics of the <term>glans penis</term> <note>original: "Mani-lakshana"; literally 'the marks of the jewel'</note>. Men are wealthy if the glans is red like a jewel, but they are destitute if it is pale or dull. If it emits a sound or is silent, or if it is moist or dry, these carry different meanings. Regarding the lines or "veins" <note>original: "dhara"</note>: those with two, three, or four lines that are wound toward the right <note>original: "Pradakshina"; clockwise/auspicious direction</note> should be known as lords of the earth. Those with scattered or broken lines are devoid of wealth. If there is only a single line that is well-wound, it bestows beauty and grants offspring. Some say it grants sons who are themselves masters of beauty. Those whose anatomy is elevated and symmetrical are the enjoyers of wealth, women, and gems. If the center is depressed, they are poor and may even lose their fathers. If the center is elevated but not excessively so, they possess many cattle. As it is said: "Men are wealthy if the glans is red like a jewel." <insert>The word 'jewel' here refers to the male organ.</insert> Those with pale, white, or dark and dull organs are destitute.

Those with lines wound to the right are to be known as kings. Those whose lines are scattered are poor. A single well-wound line gives beauty and children. Those with high and even features are masters of riches, women, and jewels. Those with a depressed center are poor and may be "father-killers" <note>likely a karmic designation rather than a literal accusation</note>. Those with an elevated center possess many animals. If the organ is excessively high or thick, they are not masters of wealth.

And so: "He whose organ is shaped like a gem and shines evenly in the middle is always to be known as a king; such is the word of Samudra." One whose organ shines like a pearl or is red like coral becomes a king. Those with pale, dull, or small organs are poor. If the lines are wound to the right, he is a king and always victorious; such is the word of Samudra. One whose urine flows in two streams, is thick, and makes no sound, should be known without a doubt as one who enjoys many pleasures and possesses forests of cattle.

<margin>242</margin> Thus are the various characteristics of men. Now he describes the characteristics of the foreskin, urine, and intercourse. Those with a shriveled or contracted foreskin are known to be devoid of wealth and travelers on difficult paths. <unclear>...fragrance...</unclear>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Samudrika Shastra (the science of body marks), moving from the qualities of bodily fluids to the characteristics of the lower torso and hips.</meta>

<warning>The manuscript page has significant water staining and discoloration on the left side, although most of the text remains legible.</warning>

Kings are to be recognized by their semen. <note>The text uses "Shukra," which refers both to semen and the planet Venus; in this context of physiognomy, it refers to reproductive vitality.</note> One whose semen smells like honey will possess great wealth. One whose smell is like fish will have many children. One with thin semen will father only daughters. One whose smell is like meat will be a great enjoyer of pleasures. One whose smell is like wine will be a performer of sacrifices <note>original: "Yajva," a person who performs Vedic rituals</note>. One whose smell is like salt <margin>alkaline or caustic</margin> will be poor and a wanderer. Those who reach climax quickly in union are long-lived; conversely, those who take a long time are short-lived. 

Regarding the area between the navel and the genitals: if it is shriveled and without flesh, or if it sags, the person should be known as wealthless, poor, unfortunate, and disliked by people. However, if the area is full and well-rounded, they are fortunate. 

And so it is said: 
A waist that is broad and fleshy is praised in men. Those that are fleshless, rough, or white-spotted are remembered as givers of poverty. If one resembles a jackal, a deer, a donkey, or a camel in these parts, that man will be miserable, poor, and lowly. 

Those whose semen has the fragrance of flowers <insert>like a blossom</insert> are to be recognized as kings. 
*   **Honey-scented:** Great wealth and abundant riches.
*   **Fish-scented:** Many offspring, specifically many sons.
*   **Thin semen:** Devoted to wealth, but fathering only female children.
*   **Meat-scented:** A "Great Enjoyer" <term>Mahabhogi: one who lives in luxury and sensory satisfaction</term>.
*   **Wine-scented:** A performer of sacred sacrifices.
*   **Salt-scented:** Destitute and without means.

And so it is said: 
"A flower-scent makes a King; honey-scent brings many masters; fish-scent brings sons; thin semen brings female offspring; meat-scent brings great enjoyment; wine-scent brings the priest; those with a scent like salt are remembered as destitute men." 

He who is quick to move in the act of union <note>referring to coitus</note> is long-lived and lives for a long time. Otherwise, the man who takes a long time in union is said to be short-lived.

### || Now begins the characteristics of the buttocks ||

One with excessively large buttocks is destitute; one with fleshy, well-proportioned buttocks is prosperous. One whose buttocks resemble a tiger's <unclear>is fierce</unclear>, while one with buttocks like a frog is a lord of men. 

If the buttocks are overly thick, the man is without wealth. If they are well-fleshed, he is endowed with happiness and comfort. If the buttocks are shaped like a tiger's, he kills his enemies. If they are shaped like a frog's, that man becomes a king, a ruler of people. 

And so it is said: 
"A man with overly thick buttocks becomes poor. One with well-fleshed and well-formed buttocks, or those like a frog, is a ruler of men. One with buttocks like a monkey or a tiger is also described accordingly."

### || Now he speaks of the characteristics of the waist and belly ||

A man with a waist like a lion is a lord of men (a king). One with a waist like a monkey or a young camel is abandoned by wealth. Those with an even, well-proportioned belly are endowed with enjoyment. Those whose bellies resemble a pot <note>original: "Pithara," a pot or pan</note> are excellent but destitute. 

One whose waist is like a lion's becomes a king. One whose waist is like a monkey or a camel is devoid of riches. One with an even belly becomes a king. Those with bellies that are <unclear>curved</unclear> are enjoyers of life. A belly like a water-pot...

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page continues the study of Samudrika Shastra (the science of body marks), moving from the general torso to specific details of the flanks, waist, and navel. It maintains the traditional structure of presenting a core rule followed by a detailed commentary.</meta>

<margin>243</margin> Regarding the specific shape of the belly: those whose bellies resemble a water-pot are destitute and devoid of wealth. As it is said: "One whose belly is like that of a lion is undoubtedly a leader of men <note>literally: 'a king' or 'lord of men'</note>, while one whose belly resembles that of a dog, a jackal, a pig, or a camel is poor. Those with uneven or distended bellies are considered luckless."

### Now, the characteristics of the Flanks, Waist, and Abdomen are described:

Those with fleshy, well-formed flanks <term>parshva: the sides of the torso between the ribs and hips</term> are wealthy. Those with sunken or crooked flanks are deprived of enjoyments. Those with an even waist possess enjoyments, while those with a sunken navel-region are deprived of them. Those with a high or raised waist are kings <note>literally: 'protectors of the earth'</note>. Men with uneven waists are harsh. Those with an abdomen like a serpent are poor and gluttonous.

The region four finger-widths above the waist is called the "flank." Those whose flanks are "undiminished"—meaning full and fleshy—are wealthy lords. With sunken, crooked, or elongated flanks, they are "deprived of enjoyments," meaning they lack comforts. The word "waist" <term>kati: the hip or lumbar region</term> refers here to the middle part of the body. Those whose waists are level and symmetrical possess enjoyments. Those with a sunken navel or waist are devoid of pleasures. Those with high or elevated waists are kings. Men with "uneven" or asymmetrical waists are "harsh," meaning difficult or cruel men. Those whose abdomen is very long like a serpent's are "destitute," meaning poor, and "gluttonous," meaning they eat voraciously. As it is said:

> "Men with broad and well-nourished flanks are remembered as wealthy; those with sunken, crooked, or uneven flanks are excluded from enjoyments. An even waist brings pleasure, while a sunken one excludes it. Kings possess elevated waists, while those with uneven waists have wicked intentions. Men with serpent-like bellies are remembered as penniless and gluttonous."

### Now, the characteristics of the Navel are described:

With well-rounded, elevated, and broad navels, men are happy. Small, shallow, or sunken navels bring distress. A navel situated in the middle of skin-folds <term>vali: the natural folds or creases of the abdomen</term> that is uneven or protruding causes obstacles and constant poverty. A leftward-turning <note>counter-clockwise</note> spiral navel produces a deceptive mind, while a rightward-turning <note>clockwise</note> one produces intelligence. A navel extended toward the sides grants long life; extended upwards, it makes one a lord of a thousand; extended downwards, it grants abundance of cattle; and a navel with a central "seed" <term>kanika: the central protuberance of the navel</term> makes one a lord of men.

With "well-rounded"—meaning perfectly circular—and "elevated" navels, and with "broad"—meaning wide—navels, men become happy. "Small" navels (measuring only a finger-width) belong to those of little wealth. A shallow navel that is wide brings distress and yields little fruit. "Situated in the middle of folds" refers to a navel placed within the abdominal creases; if it is uneven or protruding, it causes obstacles. "Poverty" means the state of being without wealth. A "leftward-turning" navel creates a "shabby" or deceptive disposition, making one averse to the welfare of others. As it is said:

> "If one's speech does not match one's heart, though they seem intent on the task, their actions are contrary; such a person is called a rogue by the virtuous."

A "rightward-turning" <note>clockwise</note> navel produces "intelligence," meaning a refined intellect; here, it implies a mind endowed with merit. <margin>243</margin> As it is said:

> "Those with soft flanks and a steady flow of character are known for their wisdom and strength of science."

A navel wide at the sides makes one "long-lived." Extended upwards, it makes one a lord of a thousand. Extended downwards, it brings wealth in cattle and a hundred sons...

<summary>This page details the physiognomy of the torso, linking physical traits like a fleshy flank or a clockwise-spiraling navel to wealth, royalty, and intelligence, while sunken or serpent-like features indicate poverty.</summary>
<keywords>physiognomy, Samudrika Shastra, navel omens, waist characteristics, wealth, kingship, Indian divination</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page continues the study of physiognomy (Samudrika Shastra), moving from the general torso to specific details of the midsection and chest. It maintains the traditional structure of stating a physical feature followed by its karmic or social implication.</meta>

The center of the lotus-like navel, and below that, a navel shaped like the seed-vessel of a lotus <note>original: "padma-karnika-kara"</note> which is raised in the middle and expansive, makes a man a lord of men and a king. A navel that is circular, large, but shallow <note>literally "empty" or "hollow"</note> belongs to a lord of men. If the navel is barely visible or downward-sloping, it brings distress and toil.

A navel situated amidst the abdominal folds <note>term: "vali" (the natural horizontal folds or creases on the stomach)</note> that is fleshy indicates a destroyer. One that spirals to the left <note>counter-clockwise</note> indicates an enemy to the divine order, while one that spirals to the right <note>clockwise</note> and extends toward the flanks indicates a long life. A navel resembling the seed-vessel of a lotus brings lordship.

### The Characteristics of the Abdominal Folds (Vali)

Now, the characteristics of the upward-turning folds are described. According to the scriptures, one can know the status of a man—whether he is a seeker of pleasure, a teacher, or one with many sons—by the number of folds: one, two, three, or four. A man without any folds is a king.

Men with uneven folds <note>folds of different lengths or depths</note> are sinners who approach women they should not. Those with straight, even folds are enjoyers of happiness and are averse to the wives of others.

As it is said in the scriptures:
*   **One fold:** One who is skilled in the weapons and the sciences <note>original: "shastra-visharada"</note>.
*   **Two folds:** One who enjoys the company of women.
*   **Three folds:** A teacher <note>term: "acharya"</note> or one who gives instruction.
*   **Four folds:** One who is blessed with many sons.
*   **No folds:** One should know him to be a king or a sovereign.

Those men whose folds are uneven—some long, some short—are prone to forbidden conduct and are sinful. Those whose folds are straight and clear are possessors of happiness and possess a virtuous dislike for the wives of others. Furthermore: A man with one fold knows the scriptures; with two, he is remembered as a father and a pleasure-seeker; with three, they call him a teacher; with four, he has many offspring. He who is without folds is called a king, a performer of sacrifices, and one intent on charity.

### Characteristics of the Flanks (Parshva)

Now, the characteristics of the flanks <note>the sides of the body between the ribs and hips</note> are described. With fleshy and soft flanks that have clockwise-turning hair or no hair at all, men become kings. With the opposite characteristics, they are described as being devoid of happiness and servants to others.

The area of the body above the hips is called the "flank" <note>original: "parshva"</note>. With flanks that are fleshy (thick), soft (tender), and without hair or with hair turning to the right, men become rulers of the earth. With the opposite—thin, rough, with hair turning to the left—they become destitute, lacking in wealth, devoid of happiness, and become laborers or servants for others.

### Characteristics of the Nipples (Chuchuka)

Now, the characteristics of the nipples <note>gloss: "chuchuka" means the tip of the breast</note> are described. Men are fortunate if their nipples are not raised or prominent. They are poor if the nipples are uneven or long. They are lords of the earth <note>kings</note> if the nipples are firm, elevated, fleshy, and smooth; such men are enjoyers of happiness.

<meta>This page continues the systematic analysis of human physical characteristics (physiognomy) from the previous section, moving from the lower torso to the chest, neck, and throat.</meta>

<page-num>244</page-num>

<note>The following verse appears to be a concluding fragment regarding the waist or abdomen from the previous page.</note>
...with a waist that is swollen or bound, one is fortunate and shares in beauty; those with uneven, long, or fleshy waists are destitute.

# Now, the Characteristics of the Heart are Described

The heart <note>In this context, "heart" refers to the external chest area over the heart</note> that is not protruding, but broad, steady, and fleshy belongs to kings. For those without wealth, it is the opposite: trembling, covered in coarse hair, and veiny. A heart that is not raised high, but is wide, expansive, steady, and muscular belongs to kings. Conversely, a heart that is uneven, sunken, trembling, and lacking flesh belongs to the poor. Furthermore, a chest covered in coarse hair and filled with prominent veins belongs to the destitute. Those whose hearts are broad and high are worthy; those whose hearts are sunken, veiny, and hairy are partakers of misery.

# Now, the Characteristics of the Chest are Described

Those with an even chest are wealthy; those with thick, fleshy chests are heroes; those with thin chests are insignificant; and those with uneven chests are impoverished and meet their end by the sword. Those whose chests are even—neither sunken nor protruding—are wealthy. Those with thick, fleshy, and firm chests are brave. Those with thin chests, lacking muscle, are "nothing-doers" <note>original: "akinchana," meaning those who lack resources or influence</note>. Those whose chests are uneven or slanted are destitute and meet a "death by the weapon" <note>original: "shastra-nidhana," implying a violent death in battle or by execution</note>.

> As it is said:
> One with an even chest is wealthy; with a thick chest, a hero; with a thin chest, poor. Those with uneven chests are of low status and die by the sword.

# Now, the Characteristics of the Collarbone are Described

With uneven collarbones, a person is cruel; with wide or bony joints, they are destitute; with raised collarbones, they are enjoyers of life; and with fleshy ones, they are wealthy. The <term>Jatru</term> <note>The clavicle or collarbone area where the neck meets the shoulders</note> is the junction of the throat. With unequal collarbones, one is cruel. With bony, protruding joints, one is devoid of wealth. He whose collarbones are raised is a "bhogi" <note>an enjoyer of worldly pleasures and comforts</note> and fortunate. With sunken collarbones, one is penniless. With thick, full collarbones, one is a man of means.

> As it is said:
> Through uneven collarbones, one is cruel; through harsh joints, poor. One with raised collarbones is an enjoyer; with sunken ones, he is without wealth.

# Now, the Characteristics of the Neck and Back are Described

One with a flat neck is poor and lustful; one with a veiny neck is the same. One with a neck like a buffalo is a hero; one with a neck like a bull meets his end by the sword. One with a "conch-shell neck" <note>original: "kambu-griva," a neck with three graceful folds, considered a mark of great beauty and royalty</note> is a king. One with a long, dangling throat is a glutton. Those with a small, hairless neck are wealthy; this is auspicious, while other types are not. He whose neck is flat and thin is destitute. He whose neck is dry, fleshless, and covered in veins is likewise poor. One whose neck resembles a buffalo's is brave. 244 One whose neck is like a bull's—thick and sturdy—meets his end by a weapon. He who has three lines on the neck has a conch-neck.

> As it is said:
> A neck adorned with three folds is called a "conch-neck." He who possesses a conch-neck becomes a king. He whose throat is long and dangling is a "devourer" <note>original: "prabhakshano," implying one who consumes resources without accumulating them</note>, possessing a nature that does not save.

> As it is said:

<meta>This page continues the systematic anatomical analysis of the human body according to the Samudrika Shastra (the science of body marks), moving from the neck down to the arms.</meta>

<warning>Water staining and foxing visible along the top, left, and bottom edges of the manuscript. The ink is generally well-preserved and dark.</warning>

A man whose neck is round is considered wealthy. Those men with a <term>conch-like neck</term><note>original: "kambu-griva"; a neck with three graceful folds like a conch shell</note> are undoubtedly kings. However, men with long necks are partakers of great sorrow. Those with necks like a crane are deceitful and backbiters. Likewise, those with necks that are sunken, flat, or like a parrot's <note>original: "shuka-griva"</note> [face similar fates]. Necks that are not red and have visible veins are considered inferior. A back that is firm, even, and hairless belongs to those with wealth and means; conversely, a back that is hairy belongs to the poor. As it is said: "A back that is well-joined, fleshy, firm, and hairless is for the wealthy; the opposite is proclaimed for the poor."

### Now the characteristics of the armpits are described:

Armpits that are painless, fleshy, elevated, fragrant, and covered in hair are known to belong to the wealthy; those lacking these qualities belong to the poor. The word <term>Kaksha</term><note>armpit</note> refers to the base of the arms. Armpits that are painless (without sweat), fleshy, high, fragrant (having a pleasant smell), even, and covered with hair are to be understood as belonging to lords and wealthy men. Conversely, those that are sweaty, thin, low, foul-smelling, uneven, and hairless belong to those without wealth. As it is said: "Sweatless, fleshy, fragrant, and hairy armpits are for the wealthy; know the opposite for the poor."

### Now the characteristics of the shoulders are described:

Shoulders that are thin and covered in hair are for the poor; those that are broad, well-knit, fleshy, and hairy belong to the wealthy. The <term>Amsa</term><note>shoulders</note> that are meatless, covered in hair, trembling, or small belong to the destitute. Those that are broad, firm, well-joined, and very fleshy belong to the courageous and the strong. As it is said: "Men whose shoulders resemble the trunk of a plantain tree are to be known as kings, possessing great treasuries and great strength. Those that are thin and excessively hairy are proclaimed for the poor."

### Now the characteristics of the arms are described:

Arms that resemble an elephant's trunk, are rounded, reach down to the knees, and are even and fleshy belong to the lords of the earth; those of the lowly are hairy and short. Arms that are like an elephant's trunk, perfectly round, <term>reaching to the knees</term><note>original: "ajanu-vilambinau"; a traditional mark of a great man or hero</note>, symmetrical, and fleshy are known to be of kings. Those that are hairy and short (not long) are known to be of the poor. A man with bound arms <unclear>attains wealth</unclear>. According to the words of the Sage Samudra: "One with long arms becomes a king; one with dangling arms attains sovereignty and enjoyment. One with short arms becomes a servant or a messenger. Those whose arms have left-turning hair and are long, or those with full arms, are proclaimed as kings."

### Now the characteristics of the hands are described:

<meta>This page continues the detailed analysis of human anatomy and its prophetic significance, specifically focusing on the fingers, palms, and wrists as indicators of lifespan, wealth, and character.</meta>

# Page 245

Long fingers belong to those who live long lives; well-formed fingers belong to the fortunate; uniform fingers belong to the wise; and flattened fingers belong to those devoted to serving others. Those with thick fingers are devoid of wealth and relatives, while those with fingers lacking good qualities are insignificant. Those with hands like monkeys are wealthy, while those with hands like tigers are sinful.

Long and very slender fingers—fingers of the hand—belong to those who are long-lived <note>original: "chirāyuṣām"</note>. Fingers that are not twisted, but are clear and well-defined, belong to the fortunate who possess good luck. Uniform and thick fingers belong to the intelligent and wise. Here, some also suggest that the wise are wealthy as well.

As it has been said:
-> "The intellect that understands the past and the future, and knows the judgment of good and evil, is called wisdom by the steady-minded." <-

Those devoted to the work of others—servants—have flattened or winnow-shaped fingers. With excessively thick fingers, people become destitute and without wealth. Those with fingers that are bent outwards are destined to die by the sword <note>original: "shastra-nirguṇāḥ"</note>. Those whose hands resemble monkeys are masters of wealth. Those with hands resembling the paws of a tiger are sinful.

## On the Wrists

Regarding the wrist <note>term: "maṇibandha" — the junction of the hand and forearm, literally 'the jewel-bond'</note>: when the wrists are firm and the joints are well-knit and hidden, they belong to kings. When the wrists are weak, making a cracking sound, or are disfigured, they belong to the poor.

By the word "wrist," the joint is meant here. With even, firm, and well-knit joints where the vital points are well-connected, men become kings. With deficient wrists, there is a "cutting of the hand" <note>likely a metaphor for loss of power or physical injury</note>. With weak, loose, or noisy joints, they become impoverished.

## On the Palms

Men with sunken or hollow palms are deprived of their paternal wealth. Those with palms that are rounded and slightly hollowed are said to be wealthy. Those with raised or elevated palms are givers <note>generous donors</note>. With uneven palms, men are cruel and destitute. With palms the color of red lac <note>original: "lākṣābhair" — a deep red resin</note>, they become lords of wealth. With yellow palms, they are prone to pursuing forbidden women. With short palms, they are poor.

## On the Nails

Nails that resemble husks <note>original: "tuṣa" — chaff or grain husks</note> belong to the impotent. Those with flattened or split nails are abandoned by fate. Those with discolored or bad nails are servants to others and not masters. Those whose nails are crowded or resemble husks are known to be weak or foolish.

--- Page 103 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of human physiognomy and palmistry, specifically focusing on the interpretation of lines and signs on the hands to predict wealth, status, and character.</meta>

Those with flat, cracked, or broken nails are abandoned by wealth and become destitute. Those with raised, dull, or discolored nails <note>original: "vivarnair-vigatavarnair"</note> are like those who watch over cattle in the forest <note>implying a low social status or a life of wandering</note>. Those with copper-colored nails become lords and commanders of armies.

### On the Thumb and Fingers

Alternatively, those with barley-grain shaped marks <term>Yava: a grain-shaped mark on the thumb considered highly auspicious in Indian palmistry</term> at the base of the thumb possess children. Those with long finger joints are fortunate and long-lived. 

Those who possess these barley-shaped marks in the middle of the thumb become wealthy. Those with marks at the base of the thumb are blessed with sons. Those with long finger joints are endowed with good fortune and live a very long life.

### On the Lines and Spacing

Alternatively, those with deep, clear lines are wealthy, while the opposite is true for the poor. Those with wide gaps between their fingers <note>original: "virala-angulayo"</note> are penniless; those with fingers that press closely together <note>original: "ghana-angulayah"</note> are accumulators of wealth.

Deep, clear, and firm lines on the palm belong to the wealthy and powerful. Conversely, shallow lines belong to the indigent. Those whose fingers are sparse or widely set are poor, while those with closely-set fingers are formidable in acquiring and hoarding riches.

### Royal Signs and Sacred Symbols

Alternatively, three lines rising from the wrist <term>Manibandha: the bracelet lines at the wrist</term> and reaching the palm belong to a king. A hand marked with a pair of fish <note>original: "mina-yuga"</note> belongs to one who is constantly performing charity <note>literally: "giver of a sacrificial session" or "satraprado"</note>.

Lines shaped like a thunderbolt <note>Vajra</note> belong to the wealthy and the learned. Lines resembling a fish's tail belong to those possessing knowledge. Symbols resembling a conch, a parasol, a palanquin, an elephant, a horse, or a lotus belong to a king.

Those with marks of a water-pot, a banner, or a goad <note>Ankusha: a tool used to handle elephants</term> become guardians of treasures. Those with lines resembling a house possess great lordship.

Lines resembling a wheel, a sword, an axe, a tomara <note>iron club</note>, a spear, a bow, or a trident indicate a leader or a commander. Marks resembling a drum, a flag, or a granary belong to those with great wealth. A mark like a sacrificial altar <note>Vedi</note> indicates one who maintains the sacred fire <note>Agnihotra</note>.

Marks resembling a pond or a temple indicate a religious nature. Triangular lines at the base of the thumb indicate sons, while fine lines indicate daughters. A line reaching the forefinger suggests a life of a hundred years.

<column-break/>

Three lines rising from the wrist and reaching the palm belong to a king. A hand marked with a pair of fish belongs to a donor who is always providing for others. Lines in the center of the palm shaped like a thunderbolt belong to the wealthy. Those with marks like a fish's tail are seekers of knowledge. Symbols of the conch, the royal parasol, the palanquin, the elephant, the horse, and the lotus belong to kings. The water-pot, the banner, and the elephant-goad...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of palmistry (Samudrika Shastra), specifically focusing on the interpretation of lines and symbols found on the palm and their correlation to wealth, status, and lifespan.</meta>

# Page 246

Lines on the palm <note>Sanskrit: *karatala-rekha*</note> that resemble these [symbols] belong to the wealthy and to lords. They belong to a king or a ruler. A water-pot <note>Sanskrit: *kalasha* or *kumbha*</note>, or lines touching the middle that look like a banner <note>Sanskrit: *vaijayanti*</note> or an elephant goad <note>Sanskrit: *ankusha*—a tool used to guide elephants</note>; by the presence of lines in these shapes, men become guardians of treasures and protectors of established order. They become exceedingly wealthy. By lines that are brilliant and shaped like a bow, they become lords. By the *swastika* <note>A sacred solar symbol of auspiciousness</note>, which is a specific auspicious mark, and by lines of that form, one attains sovereignty.

The discus <note>Sanskrit: *chakra*—a divine weapon</note>, the sword, the battle-axe <note>Sanskrit: *parashu*</note>, the spear <note>Sanskrit: *kunara*</note>, the javelin <note>Sanskrit: *tomara*</note>, and the spear <note>Sanskrit: *shakti*</note> are well known. The bow and the *unta* <note>A specific type of weapon</note>—lines resembling these on the palm make one a lord of wealth or a commander of an army.

Lines shaped like an altar <note>Sanskrit: *uttaravedi*</note> make men performers of sacrifices <note>Sanskrit: *yajvana*—those who conduct Vedic rituals</note>. The sea-monster <note>Sanskrit: *makara*—a mythical aquatic creature</note> and the flag-sign; by these various marks, one becomes worthy of worship. From the banner symbol, a granary or treasury is foretold. By lines resembling these, men are endowed with great wealth and are exceedingly rich.

Above the wrist <note>Sanskrit: *manibandha*</note> is the "Brahma-tirtham" <note>A sacred spot on the hand used in ritual libations</note> at the base of the thumb. If the Brahma-tirtham is marked by a sign resembling a piercing or a trunk of an elephant, the person becomes a "triple-sacrificer" <note>One who has performed three major Vedic sacrifices</note>. A pond, a square, or a temple—those who have lines resembling these, or the "Shrivriksha" <note>The tree of fortune</note> and incense, perform religious duties and become most righteous. By triangles and lines shaped like a crossroad <note>Sanskrit: *shringataka*</note>, they act with great skill.

As many lines as there are at the base of the thumb, so many are the children. The thick lines indicate sons, while those resembling the [crescent] moon indicate daughters. Between the forefinger and the thumb—noting the plural usage here—three lines are to be understood. Lines reaching the forefinger indicate a "hundred-year-liver" <note>Sanskrit: *shatayusha*—one who lives a full century</note>. With fewer lines, one must estimate by their own intelligence; just as with superior, medium, or inferior marks, the calculation of the lifespan should be made according to the tradition.

By broken or faint lines, there is a fall from one's position. Those who have many lines [crossing] or those who have no lines at all are both said to be penniless. As it is said:

->With well-rounded, hidden, and firm wrists that make no sound [when moved], such men are proclaimed to be kings.<-
->Those with deficient hands or wrists are the vessels of poverty.<-
->He in whose palm there is a depression is deprived of paternal wealth.<-
->One with a firm and well-filled palm is wealthy and endowed with happiness.<-
->Those whose hands are held upward are givers, without a doubt.<-

Those with uneven or yellowish hands are poor. Nails should be glossy, copper-colored, and well-shaped; men whose nails are dry or discolored are like the dead. A man with spotless, reddish nails becomes a protector of the earth <note>A king</note>. Those whose fingers are pale and widely spaced are to be known as men who suffer the vessels of grief and poverty. He who has a line resembling a fish <note>Sanskrit: *mina*</note> attains success in all his actions.

<summary>This page details the "Samudrika Shastra" (science of body marks), interpreting various palm symbols like weapons, altars, and animals to predict a person's wealth, social status, number of children, and lifespan.</summary>
<keywords>palmistry, Samudrika Shastra, wealth, kingship, lifespan, ritual symbols, Vedic sacrifice, children, physical omens</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the study of Samudrika Shastra (the ancient Indian science of body features), moving from the interpretation of lines on the palm to the characteristics of the chin, lips, and teeth.</meta>

A man should be known as wealthy and possessed of many sons if a scale-like mark <note>original: "tula"; likely referring to a balance scale shape</note> is seen in the middle of his hand. There is no doubt that such a person will succeed in commerce. If marks resembling an altar <note>original: "vedi"</note>, a noose, a spear, a javelin, or a bow are seen on the palm, the sages know him to be a possessor of great wealth. If a thunderbolt <note>original: "vajra"</note> or a trident is seen in the center of the hand, he shall surely obtain an unshakable kingdom in this world. If a flag, a drum, or a conch shell is situated on the hand, one should know through the words of Samudra <note>The mythical founder of this science</note> that he will be peaceful and wealthy.

If a barley-corn mark <note>term: Yava (a grain-shaped island on the thumb indicating prosperity and intelligence)</note> is seen on the thumb of the right hand, that person becomes a speaker of all sciences and is highly distinguished. When the lines on the palm originate from the base of the little finger and reach the middle, they grant a lifespan of one hundred years. The small lines at the base of the thumb are declared to be the number of sons, as per the words of Samudra. If these lines are broken, it indicates a fall into sorrow; if they are faint, the person lacks power. One who has a mark at the root-shrine of the thumb <note>the fleshy mount</note> becomes a performer of sacrifices.

# Now he describes the characteristics of the chin, lower lip, and teeth.

Those with very thin or excessively long chins are destitute; those with fleshy chins are endowed with wealth. Those with lower lips resembling the Bimba fruit <note>a bright red tropical fruit used as a standard for beauty</note> become kings. Those with thin, fleshless lower lips are poor. Women with defective, cracked, broken, or discolored teeth are abandoned by wealth. Those with very sharp tusks <note>canines</note> and smooth teeth are auspicious.

The chin is the part below the lower lip at the end of the face. With very thin, fleshless, or very long chins, people become impoverished. With fleshy chins, they are wealthy. With lower lips that are red like the Bimba fruit and not crooked, they become lords and kings. With thin lips, they are destitute. With "corrupt" teeth—those that are cracked, broken, fragmented, or discolored (lacking luster)—they are abandoned by fortune and become poor. Teeth should be smooth, stainless, dense, and even. The four teeth in the middle are the incisors, and those on the sides are the tusks <note>canines</note>.

Furthermore: With fleshless and long chins, they are destitute and husbandless <note>referring to women's omens</note>; with fleshy ones, they are wealthy and respected as mothers of many sons. A man with red lips is a lord of men; one with clean lips is wealthy. One with thick lips and much hair <note>on the lip/face</note> is a consumer of women and cattle but remains miserable. Those with a prominent upper lip are wealthy but joined with folly. With broken or discolored teeth, they are merciless. Those whose teeth resemble jasmine buds or are white like a wall become kings. Those with teeth like monkeys or crows are always tormented by enemies. Elephant-like teeth are givers of boons; smooth teeth are endowed with virtues. With jagged, uneven, or long teeth, they live a life of sorrow.

Those with thirty-two teeth become kings. Those with thirty-one teeth are enjoyers of life. Men with thirty teeth share in both happiness and sorrow. Those with twenty-nine teeth live a life of misery. Those who have only twenty-eight teeth are vessels of extreme suffering.

# Now he describes the characteristics of the tongue and the palate.

A tongue that is red, long, and possesses good marks is considered auspicious and belongs to those who are prosperous <note>original: "bhogina," meaning those who enjoy wealth and pleasures</note>. White, black, or rough tongues are undesirable and belong to the wretched; the same applies to the palate. A tongue that is reddish, the color of blood, long, stretching, and marked with fine lines—thin and elongated—is praised and possesses a good luster; such should be known as belonging to the prosperous. White or pale tongues, and those that are black or dark, are undesirable; those that are cracked belong to the impoverished. 

The palate should be understood in the same manner as the tongue: just as a red and well-marked tongue is auspicious and beautiful, so too is a beautiful palate. One that is white, black, or <margin>247</margin> rough is considered unattractive. 

And so it is said: He whose tongue is black or appears dirty is a sinful mortal; the same is said if it is overly thick. Men with white tongues are known to be devoid of purity and proper conduct. A tongue resembling a lotus petal <note>original: "padmapatra"</note>, well-marked, very long, and very beautiful—neither too thick nor too wide—belongs to lords of men <note>kings</note>. If it is deep, long, curved, and red, he will undoubtedly be well-versed in all sciences. However, a tongue that is red but thin and crooked brings about the destruction of one's lineage. He whose palate is struck or split does not fare well. A white and thin palate belongs to those who serve kings, while a palate shaped like a lotus belongs to a king, and a white palate belongs to the poor.

# Now he describes the characteristics of the mouth.

A mouth that is gentle, well-closed, clean, well-marked, and symmetrical belongs to kings. The opposite belongs to those who endure hardship <note>original: "mleccha bhuñjānām," likely referring here to those living outside refined society or in toil</note>, the childless, and the unfortunate. A mouth <note>original: "vaktra"</note> that is gentle, pleasant, well-closed (rounded and not gaping), stainless, pure, well-marked, not overly fleshy on the sides, and symmetrical should be known as belonging to kings. The opposite characteristics—harsh, gaping, dirty, and asymmetrical—should be known as belonging to those who suffer and the poor. A very large and overly wide mouth belongs to the unfortunate. 

And so it is said: He whose mouth is gentle, well-closed, and beautifully clean shall always be a king; with the opposite traits, he is penniless.

# Now he describes the characteristics of the face.

A face resembling a woman's belongs to the childless; a round face belongs to the cunning <note>original: "shathavatam," referring to those who are deceitful or like jackals</note>; a long face belongs to the undesirable; and a timid face belongs to those who commit sinful deeds. A square, triangular, sunken, or crooked face belongs to those without sons. A very small face belongs to the miserly, while a full and lovely face belongs to the prosperous. 

A face <note>original: "mukhavadana"</note> like a woman's belongs to those without offspring. A circular or round face belongs to the cunning—those who are like jackals and ruin the work of others. A long face belongs to the undesirable and the poor. Those with a timid face, like that of a frightened person, are devoted to sinful acts. A square or four-cornered face belongs to the undesirable and poor <insert>it also belongs to those who deceive like elephants</insert>. A sunken, deficient, or crooked face—shaped like the back of a fish—belongs to those without sons. An extremely small face belongs to the miserly. A full face [indicates] intelligence and success in one's endeavors...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of physiognomy (the study of physical features to determine character), specifically focusing on the face, eyebrows, ears, and cheeks.</meta>

A full, fleshy, beautiful, and radiant face belongs to those who enjoy life and possess great wealth. Thus it is said: The face of those without children is like a woman's; a circular face belongs to those who consume little. A long face belongs to those who are destitute, while a crooked face indicates those with evil intentions. A square face belongs to the cunning, and a sunken face to those devoid of sons. A short face belongs to the miserly, and a flat face to those who live off others. A face that is curved, fleshy, glossy, symmetrical, and pleasant to look at belongs to merchants. One with loose joints <note>referring to the jaw or facial structure</note> belongs to those who constantly experience happiness.

## Now, the characteristics of the eyebrows are told

Eyebrows that are not split, are glossy, auspicious, and slightly raised are praised. Those with red, falling hairs are known to be thieves. Eyebrows that are glossy, charming, very soft, and slightly raised are auspicious and excellent. Some say "very well-raised." With red (blood-colored) hairs, or with very few hairs, such people are to be known as thieves or robbers. Thus it is said: Glossy, unsplit, and raised eyebrows are preferred; those with few or falling hairs are remembered as thieves.

## Now, the characteristics of the ears are told

Those with fleshless ears meet a sinful death. Those with flat ears enjoy many pleasures. Those with short ears are misers. Those with cone-shaped ears <term>Shanku-shravana: ears shaped like a spike or cone</term> are kings or army commanders. Those with hairy ears have long lives and possess wealth. Those with large ears are brave. Those with very hard ears covered in prominent veins are cruel. Those with long, pendulous, and fleshy ears are happy.

With fleshless ears—ears devoid of flesh—they die through sinful deeds. With flat or spread-out ears, they become great enjoyers of life. Those with short ears are "takers" <note>meaning they are stingy or prone to taking from others</note>. Those with cone-shaped ears—ears that are sharp at the top—become army commanders. Those with large ears—wide ears—are wealthy. Those with ears full of veins are cruel and inconsistent. Those with long, hanging, fleshy ears are happy.

Thus it is said: Men with short ears have great enjoyment, and those with large ears are likewise. Those with curled ears are wealthy, as are those with glossy ears. Those with cone-shaped ears have long lives; those with satisfied <note>well-formed</note> ears have great wealth. Those with long ears are like gods, and those with very long, pendulous ears are ascetics. Those with fleshless ears die a sinful death; those with flat ears are enjoyers. Those with hairy ears are remembered as long-lived and wealthy, and those with large ears are the same. Those with prominent veins are inconsistent, and those with fleshy ears are sharers in happiness.

## Now, the characteristics of the cheeks and nose are told

One who has cheeks that are not sunken is an enjoyer. One whose cheeks are full and fleshy shares in happiness. Those with soft, even, and radiant noses that are not too prominent are long-lived and fortunate. Those with flat noses are cruel, thoughtless, thieves, and enemies of women. Those with noses shaped like a barley grain <term>Yava-nasa: a nose bridge that bulges slightly in the middle like a grain</term> are wealthy. Those whose noses turn to the right are dividers <note>possibly meaning they are divisive or analytical</note> and cruel.

Regarding the "enjoyer": one whose cheeks are not sunken but high is a man of enjoyment and wealth.

Those whose cheeks are fleshy like beef <note>original: "gomāṃsa"; likely referring to fullness or firmness</note> and are even and smooth become ministers. He whose cheeks are neither too low nor too high is remembered as a man of enjoyment. Regarding the mouth: those whose nose is shaped like a parrot's beak <note>original: "śuka-samā"; a hooked nose</note>, slightly curved and reddish at the tip, will possess virtuous sons. He whose nose is shaped like a parrot's but is without flesh <note>thin or sharp</note> becomes long-lived. Those with broken or irregular noses are said to be prone to forbidden relationships <note>original: "gamyagāminaḥ"; those who approach women they should not</note>. A long nose brings good fortune. One that is slightly curved and distinct belongs to a brave man. A flat-nosed man <note>original: "cipiṭa-nāsaḥ"</note> is a slave to women, or rather, his wife dominates him. Those whose noses are curved at the ends are wealthy and earners of money. Those whose noses are bent or crooked to the right side are cruel, inconsistent, and fond of hoarding women. Noses that are straight, well-touched, with small nostrils and beautiful wings <note>nostril flares</note> belong to those endowed with great luck and fortune. 

**As it is said:**
One with a parrot-nose shares in happiness; those with small noses live long; those with broken noses go to forbidden women; long noses are joined with enjoyment; curved noses possess wealth; those with noses bent to the right are cruel; straight and well-formed noses are the best; flat noses are slaves to women; and those with crooked noses are thieves.

### Now the characteristics of the palms (and voice) are told:

For the wealthy, the sound <note>of a clap or voice</note> is great and resonant; for the long-lived, it is continuous and deep. For the wealthy and powerful, the sound is produced once and is distinct. Others explain it thus: for the wealthy, the sound is singular and clear, but for the long-lived, it is doubled or tripled <note>layered or echoing</note>, resonant, and continuous. For those who live long, the sound is "pindita" (compacted or multiplied). It is heard loudly like the sound of a victory cry. It is resonant, meaning even when produced slowly, it travels a great distance. It should be known as "samhṛta" (composed), meaning it remains unchanged in the beginning, middle, and end, and stays consistent despite anger, joy, or fear. Such a voice, which is pleasing to the minds of all people, is considered excellent. As the Sage Parashara <note>A legendary Vedic seer and author of early texts on astrology and physiognomy</note> says:

### Now the characteristics of the eyes are told:

One with eyes like lotus petals <note>original: "padma-dala"</note> is wealthy. Those with eyes reddish at the corners are recipients of fortune. Those with honey-colored or reddish-brown eyes <note>original: "madhu-piṅgala"</note> possess half-wealth. Those with cat-like eyes are sinful. Those with round eyes like a deer or those with crooked eyes are cruel. Those with squinted eyes or eyes resembling an elephant's are kings <note>original: "bhūpatayaḥ"</note>. Those with the depth and luster of a blue water-lily are learned scholars. Those with very black pupils and clear whites possess great excellence.
