<meta>This page continues the discussion of physiognomy (Samudrika Shastra), specifically focusing on the characteristics of the eyes (Netra-lakshanam) and beginning the section on eyebrows (Bhru-lakshanam).</meta>

Raised and thick-sighted eyes bring good fortune to those who possess them <note>literally: "like those of a dog," likely referring to a loyal or alert gaze</note>. A pitiful or weak gaze is censured, while wide eyes belong to those who enjoy great pleasures. Those with eyes like lotus petals <note>original: "padma-dalayaih"</note> are wealthy and endowed with riches. Those with reddish eyes are vessels of prosperity and possess fortune. Those with eyes the color of honey <note>original: "madhu-pingala"</note> are unsuccessful in their tasks. Those with eyes like a cat <note>original: "marjari"</note> or with a glassy tint are sinful. Those with eyes like a deer or circular eyes—perfectly round—are thieves if their eyes are also crooked or terrifying.

Blue-eyed individuals, those with deer-like eyes, and those with circular eyes <insert>are described further</insert>. Those with eyes like an elephant <note>original: "gaja-sadrisha"</note> become kings or generals. Deep-set eyes bring lordship. Those with eyes having the luster of a blue water-lily and similar pupils are learned scholars. 

<margin>Regarding the pupils</margin>
Those with white pupils may suffer the loss of their eyes. Those with large pupils and an outward gaze find friendship and obtain a wife. Those with eyes like a dog's have good fortune. A pitiful gaze indicates poverty. Clear and wide eyes belong to those who enjoy life's pleasures.

**As it is said:**
Those with eyes the color of cow's milk, with clear and large pupils that are pleasing and long, are auspicious. One whose eyes resemble the Atasi flower <note>flax flower, typically blue/purple</note> should be known as a king. Others are considered mediocre. One should avoid a man with honey-colored eyes. However, those with eyes yellow like clarified butter <note>ghee</note> are kings joined with enjoyment. Those with eyes like yellow pigment or elephant-yellow are lords of wealth, powerful, endowed with virtues, and kings of the earth. One whose eyes have the luster of molten gold is to be known as a king; such is the teaching of Sage Samudra. 

Those who blink in two measures of time are famous for wealth. Those who blink in three measures are known as men who live happily. Those with a four-measure blink are celebrated as wealthy. Those with a five-measure blink are long-lived and devoted to righteousness.

## The Characteristics of Eyebrows
**Now he speaks of the eyebrows:**
Those with very high eyebrows have a short lifespan. Those with sparse but high eyebrows are very happy. Those with uneven eyebrows are poor. Those with eyebrows curved like the crescent moon <note>original: "balendu"</note> are wealthy. Those with long, joined eyebrows are rich. Those with broken eyebrows are deprived of wealth. Those whose eyebrows are depressed in the middle are attached to the wealth of women. 

With very high eyebrows, some are very happy, while others with two <insert>specific traits</insert> are short-lived. With wide and high eyebrows, they are exceedingly happy. Those whose eyebrows are uneven or unequal are poor and destitute. Those with eyebrows like the crescent moon...

# Page 249

Those whose eyebrows are shaped like the crescent moon <note>original: "balachandra"</note> are very wealthy. Those with long eyebrows that reach the ears are long-lived and obedient to their masters. Those with broken or fragmented eyebrows are devoid of wealth and become poor. Men whose eyebrows are positioned in the middle—neither too high nor too low—are devoted to their wives and sons.

**As it is said:**
With very high eyebrows, one is poor; with very wide ones, one is honored by kings. Those with eyebrows raised in the middle are sinful men. Those with eyebrows shaped like the crescent moon are wealthy, while those with uneven eyebrows are poor. Those whose eyebrows do not meet in the middle are remembered as wealthy men. Those with broken eyebrows should be known as destitute, and those with uneven eyebrows are the lowest of men.

## On the Characteristics of the Temples and Forehead
With high and broad temples <term>Shankha: the anatomical region of the temples</term>, men possess wealth, sons, and prosperity. Those with uneven foreheads are wealthy, yet their wealth is like a difficult burden. Those with foreheads as wide as a mother-of-pearl shell <note>original: "shukti"</note> become teachers <term>Acharya: a spiritual preceptor or professor</term>. Those whose foreheads are covered in a network of veins are devoted to unrighteousness. Those with prominent, raised veins are rich, as are those whose veins are arranged in the shape of a Swastika <note>an ancient solar symbol of good fortune</note>.

Those with low or sunken foreheads are destined for execution or imprisonment and are devoted to cruel deeds. Those with excessively high foreheads are sinful, while those with narrow or contracted foreheads are misers. With high, broad, and expansive temples, men become wealthy lords. With temples that are low yet high <note>likely referring to a specific anatomical protrusion</note>, they are blessed with sons but may lack wealth.

**As it is said:**
With high and broad temples, men live happily; they are delighted by sons and wealth, while others are sharers of sorrow. Those whose foreheads appear uneven or contracted are destitute and poor. Those with foreheads resembling the half-moon or crescent are wealthy lords. With foreheads wide as a shell, one attains the status of a Teacher—a Teacher is one who instructs others. With foreheads full of veins, there is unrighteousness. With raised veins on the forehead, they are wealthy lords; if the veins are situated like a Swastika, they are also wealthy.

Those with low foreheads are fit for execution, imprisonment, and loss of wealth; they are constantly engaged in cruel, uneven, or deceptive actions. With excessively high foreheads, they are not wealthy but are sinful or may become army commanders. Those whose foreheads are contracted, circular, and small are misers—they are not of a giving nature.

By a forehead shaped like a half-moon, men become lords of the earth (kings). By a broad forehead, they are remembered as being endowed with great wealth. 

**Likewise:**
The uneven are known as the lowest; those with many veins are sinful mortals. With low or uneven foreheads, men are devoted to cruel deeds. With excessively high foreheads, they are sinful; with contracted foreheads, they are remembered as misers.

## On the Characteristics of Weeping
Weeping that is not pitiful, without excessive tears, and where the eyes remain bright is characteristic of great men. Pitiful weeping, or that which is like a god's or a demon's, is not auspicious for men. That weeping which is not dejected, which is steady... <note>The text breaks off here as it transitions to the mechanics of auspicious vs. inauspicious crying</note>

<meta>This page continues the study of Samudrika Shastra (the science of body marks), transitioning from the characteristics of the chest and laughter to the divination of lifespan through forehead lines.</meta>

->**The Characteristics of Laughter and Forehead Lines**<-

For men, a chest that is well-formed, broad, and firm is auspicious. However, a chest that appears depressed, weak, or excessively hairy is not beneficial for men. <note>The text suggests that physical robustness in the chest correlates with strength of character and fortune.</note> As it is said: A chest that is not depressed, is well-built, and appears pleasant is auspicious; a chest that is depressed or afflicted by defects is undesirable for men.

### On the Characteristics of Laughter

Now, the characteristics of laughter are described: Laughter that is auspicious is steady <note>original: "akampam" - without trembling of the body</note> and occurs with eyes slightly closed. However, the laughter of a sinful or wicked person is a loud, boisterous laugh <note>original: "attahasa"</note> that occurs repeatedly.

That laughter which is steady—meaning it is devoid of trembling—is considered excellent and auspicious. This refers to laughter where no shaking or movement of the limbs is produced. Laughter with eyes excessively squinted or closed belongs to the sinful (those who perform wicked deeds). Repeated, loud laughter belongs to the wicked or those intoxicated by pride. Furthermore, laughter that is constant and excessive belongs to the insane.

As it is said: Laughter without trembling is auspicious; otherwise, it belongs to the sinful. Frequent laughter accompanied by defects or squinted eyes is inauspicious.

### On the Characteristics of Forehead Lines

Now, the characteristics of the lines on the forehead <term>Lalata-rekha: the horizontal creases on the forehead used in Vedic astrology to determine lifespan and destiny</term> are described:

Three long, continuous lines across the forehead indicate a centenarian <note>one who lives for a hundred years</note>. With four such lines, one becomes a lord of the earth <note>a king or ruler</note> and the lifespan is ninety-five years. 

| Number of Lines | Predicted Lifespan / Trait |
| :--- | :--- |
| Three Long Lines | 100 Years |
| Four Long Lines | 95 Years and Kingship |
| Broken/Fragmented Lines | One who approaches the unapproachable <note>implies illicit or immoral behavior</note> |
| No Lines (Smooth) | 90 Years |
| Lines reaching the hairline | 80 Years |
| Five Lines | 70 Years |
| Many Lines (Six) | 50 Years |
| Thick/Coarse Lines | 40 Years |
| Lines curved to the left | 30 or 20 Years |
| Short Lines | Short life |

If the lines are broken or fragmented, the person is prone to improper conduct. If the forehead is smooth and devoid of lines, the lifespan is also ninety-nine years. <note>The text notes "api" (also) to include those with smooth foreheads in the high-longevity category.</note> 

Lines that reach up to the hairline indicate a life of eighty years. With five lines present on the forehead, the lifespan is seventy years. If the forehead is marked by many lines (six), the lifespan is half a century (fifty years). If the lines are thick, the life is forty years. If the lines are curved or crooked toward the left side, the lifespan is twenty years. Those with very short lines are short-lived. 

<margin>One must use their own intelligence to interpret the space between these lines.</margin>

<insert>The interpretation of three lines for a hundred-year life and four lines for ninety-five years is the standard measure.</insert> Between these specific counts, the translator/practitioner must use their own judgment <note>original: "svabudhya" - by one's own intellect</note> to determine the exact length of life based on the clarity and position of the marks.

# On the Signs of the Forehead, Head, and Hair

If a man possesses these marks, his lifespan is reckoned to be ninety years, and so forth. Thus it is said:

He who has five lines upon his forehead is considered wealthy; he lives for a hundred years and attains great power. With four lines, his life is eighty years; with three, it is seventy; with two, it is sixty; and with one line upon the brow, it is forty years. Those with a forehead devoid of lines <note>original: "arekhena lalatena"</note> become the guardians of treasures. Those with broken or irregular lines should be known as men devoted to sinful deeds; they are short-lived and always afflicted by disease. He upon whose forehead a trident <note>the *trishula*, a three-pronged symbol associated with Lord Shiva</note> or a spear-head is seen should be known as a man of great power, a general, or a leader of men.

## Now he declares the <term>signs of the head</term> <note>original: "shira-lakshanam"</note>:

Those with heads that are perfectly circular, or shaped like that of a cow or a tiger, become lords of the earth. Those with flattened heads are destroyers of their fathers and mothers. Those whose heads resemble a skull <note>original: "karoti," implying a hollow or bony appearance</note> meet death after a long time. Heads that rival the shape of a water-pot or are split into two <note>possibly referring to a prominent sagittal suture or malformation</note> belong to those with sinful hearts and no wealth. A head that is excessively sunken or depressed brings many calamities and punishments.

To explain further: By "circular" is meant perfectly round heads. Those shaped like a cow or tiger possess great abundance. Those shaped like a banner—that is, broad at the upper part—are kings and rulers of the earth. "Flattened" means those whose heads are pressed down; they are the slayers of their parents. "Skull-like" means heads resembling a dry cranium; for them, death comes after a long duration, meaning they are long-lived. A head rivaling a water-pot <note>original: "ghata-sparddha"</note> belongs to one who is long-suffering. A "double-head" belongs to a sinner who is abandoned by wealth and becomes poor. A "sunken head" is one that is excessively low or indented; such a head brings great misfortune and sorrow. Thus it is said: A sunken head brings about a loss of status and little enjoyment. One shaped like a banner,

<insert>or a head shaped like the frontal globe of an elephant, belongs to a king who enjoys the entire earth.</insert>

## Now he describes the characteristics of the hair:

When the hairs grow one from each pore, and are glossy, black, curled, and soft, such a man becomes a lord of men or a king. But those whose hair has many roots, is uneven, reddish-brown, thick, split at the ends, rough, excessively crooked, or very dense, are men devoid of wealth.

To clarify: "One from each" means only one hair grows from each individual pore. With such hair, the face appears radiant and the man becomes a king. "Many roots" refers to multiple hairs emerging from a single pore. "Uneven" means they are not uniform—some are short and some are long. "Reddish-brown" <note>original: "kapila," often a dull or tawny color</note> refers to the tint. "Split at the ends" means the tips are divided. "Rough" means dry to the touch. "Excessively crooked" means very tightly curled.

<summary>This page details the physiognomic interpretation of forehead lines, head shapes, and hair quality, correlating specific physical traits with lifespan, royal status, or poverty.</summary>
<keywords>Samudrika Shastra, physiognomy, forehead lines, head shapes, hair characteristics, lifespan, kingship, divination</keywords>

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of physiognomy (the study of physical features to determine character or destiny), specifically focusing on the hair and the defining characteristics of a "Great Man" or King.</meta>

Very thick, matted hair on the head belongs to those who are destitute and poor. **And likewise:** Hair that grows singly from each pore, is glossy, black, and not overly thick is praised; the opposite is said to belong to the poor. **The Teacher has said:** The <term>Samudravid</term><note>An expert in Samudrika Shastra, the ancient Indian science of body marks and physiognomy</note> speaks from his own intuition or tradition. We have cited these "Signs of Men" here, but it should be understood that the Teacher knows much more than this.

**Now he summarizes everything briefly:**
Whatever limb is devoid of flesh and thick with visible veins is declared unfavorable; the opposite of this is entirely auspicious. Any limb or body part that is sunken, lacking flesh, and covered in prominent veins is called unfavorable or inauspicious. Therefore, the opposite—limbs that are smooth, fleshy, and pleasant to the touch—is considered entirely auspicious.

**Now he describes the signs of a Great Man (Mahapurusha):**
A man is a King if he is broad in three parts, deep in three, raised in six, firm in four, reddish in seven, and long in five. **Now he provides the specific classification of these:**

The navel, the voice, and the <term>Sattva</term><note>Inner strength, character, or essence</note>—these three should be calm and deep in men. The chest, the forehead, and the face—these three being wide is praised in men. The armpits, the belly, the nose, the mouth, and the <term>Krikatika</term><note>The nape or back of the neck</note>—these six should be raised. Four parts should be firm: the penis, the back, the neck, and the shanks. The corners of the eyes, the feet, the palms, the nails, the palate, the lips, and the tongue—these seven are truly reddish in men of noble birth. Fine or thin features are found in fifteen places: the joints of the fingers, the hair, the skin, and the teeth; these are not found in those who suffer. The jaw, the eyes, the arms, the nose, and the space between the breasts—this group of five is long in Kings.

The navel is the center of the body. The voice is sound. <term>Sattva</term> is the quality of character; its sign is being imperturbable even when faced with the arrival of powerful emotions—these three are praised as "deep" in men. The chest, the forehead, and the face—this triad is praised when wide.

**Now the explanation continues:**
The belly is the middle part of the body. The nails are the "hand-growths." The nose is the organ of smell. The mouth is the face. The <term>Krikatika</term> is the back part of the neck. These six are praised when they are raised or elevated. The <term>Linga</term> is the penis. The back is the rear of the body. The neck and the shanks—these four are praised when they are firm and not overly long. The corners of the eyes, the feet, the hands, the palate (the upper part of the mouth), the lower lip <note>original: "ustra," likely a scribal error for "ostha" (lip)</note>, the tongue, and the nails—these seven are praised when they are reddish or copper-colored. The teeth and the joints of the fingers...

Lines, hair, the hair on the head <note>original: "murdhaja"</note>, skin, and the pores of the skin—these five should be fine or subtle <note>original: "sukshma"</note> in those who are fortunate. Three features are said to be prominent in ancestors. The eyes, the arms, the nose, and the space between the breasts—these five should be long <note>original: "dirgha"</note>, which is considered excellent in men. Those who possess eight specific long features are said to become kings. As it is said: fourteen parts of the body should be large, while others should be firm and well-proportioned.

### The feet, the ankles, the buttocks, the sides, the testicles, the eyes, the breasts, the shanks, and the neck.

Fourteen parts are praised by the sages. Regarding the hair, white or dark hair is auspicious depending on the context. The fingers, the eyes, and the teeth—when these are well-formed, they bring constant sovereignty and happiness. The tongue, the palate, and the lips should be reddish like a lotus. The hands and feet are praised when they possess the glow of a lotus. The hands, feet, neck, testicles, and heart—these areas, along with the forehead, belly, and back, should be broad. When the pupils of the eyes are clear and the teeth are firm, it is auspicious. For men, it is said: five parts should be long, four should be small, five should be subtle, six should be raised, five should be reddish, three should be broad, and three should be deep. This is the praised standard. Specifically, the distance between the arms, the testicles, and the space between the breasts—these five long features are excellent. The neck, the male organ, the hands, the shanks, and the heart—these are also praised in men. Subtle features should include the joints of the fingers, the teeth, the hair, and the skin. The armpits, the chest, the face, and the waist—these six raised <note>original: "unnata"</note> parts are praised in all beings. He whose hands, feet, face, and eyes are reddish <note>original: "rakta"</note> is considered the best among men. The chest, face, and forehead are praised when they are broad.

## || Now the characteristics of the Shadow (Luster) are described ||

The bodily luster <note>original: "Chhaya"</note> reveals auspicious and inauspicious results, as taught by the lords of signs. It manifests the qualities of inner energy <note>original: "tejas"</note> outwardly, just like the light of a lamp placed inside a vessel made of crystal or precious gems. The "Kaya" or bodily radiance tells of good and bad fortunes through the physical marks of the body. This should be examined in humans, animals, and birds. This radiance, though residing within the body, brilliantly illuminates the qualities of energy even on the outside. How?
<margin>251</margin>
Like the light of a lamp situated inside a jar made of crystal. A crystal gem jar <note>original: "sphatika-ratna-ghata"</note> allows the light within to shine through perfectly.

# Page 52
## Header: V 52

Just as the light of a lamp illuminates the qualities of light even outwardly, so too does the internal "shadow" <note>original: "chāyā" - here meaning a subtle aura or luster reflecting one's inner state</note> illuminate one's qualities. In this regard, there are five shadows related to the five <term>Great Elements</term> <note>The Mahābhūtas: Earth, Water, Fire, Air, and Ether</note>. It was stated previously in the *Jātaka* <note>A text on horoscopy/birth signs</note>: "The shadows produced by the Great Elements are thus indicated by the state of one's voice and complexion. The internal and external residence of these qualities manifests in the limbs through the excellence of one's tone."

In that regard, he first describes the characteristics of the **Earthy Shadow** <term>Pārthiva-chāyā</term>:
"It is glossy, dense, and possesses a complexion like a dark jewel; it is fragrant and born of the earth's essence. It brings about the acquisition of wealth, gain, and prosperity, and it fosters a constant inclination toward righteousness."
When this shadow born of the earth manifests in a person, then their teeth, nails, body hair, and the hair on the head all become glossy and well-formed. Furthermore, that shadow makes the body's luster fragrant—meaning the person's body becomes pleasant-smelling. This shadow produces contentment of mind, the acquisition of wealth, and the successful rise of one's undertakings. Likewise, it creates a daily progression in one's taste for virtue. Some read this as "the beautiful growth of righteousness."

Now he describes the characteristics of the **Watery Shadow** <term>Āpya-chāyā</term> <note>The manuscript reads "Āsvyā," likely a scribal error for Āpyā</note>:
"Glossy, white, and pleasing to the eyes, it brings about good fortune, gentleness, and prosperity. Like a mother, this shadow is the producer of the fulfillment of all goals; it bestows auspicious fruits upon embodied beings."
The watery shadow is glossy, meaning it is endowed with a certain oiliness or purity. It is white in color—predominantly fair—and is described as being as pleasing to the eyes as a clear blue lotus. It generates satisfaction. It brings about "good fortune" (being beloved by all people), "gentleness" (patience and physical comfort), and "prosperity" in one's affairs. It is the "mother of the fulfillment of all goals," just as a mother acts for the benefit of all living beings. It bestows auspicious results upon those who possess it.

Now he describes the characteristics of the **Fiery Shadow** <term>Āgneya-chāyā</term>:
"Fierce, untouchable, possessing the color of a lotus or gold, and endowed with brilliance and valor; if this fiery shadow belongs to a living being, it bestows the attainment of their desired goals through power."
The fiery shadow is "fierce" (of a wrathful nature) and "untouchable" (not to be overcome by others). Its skin luster is the color of a lotus or gold, or like the color of a blazing fire. It is joined with "brilliance" (splendor), "valor" (prowess), and "power" (the heat of courage used to subdue enemies). Endowed with these qualities of brilliance and valor, this fiery shadow—so the sages say—leads to victory for embodied beings and the realization of their cherished desires.

...quickly indeed, these [signs] take hold. Now, he describes the characteristics of the shadows originating from the Wind and the Sky.

A shadow arising from the **Wind** is dirty, rough, black, and possesses a foul odor; it produces death, imprisonment, disease, misfortune, and the loss of wealth. 

A shadow arising from the **Sky** has a form like crystal, is endowed with radiance, and is most noble; like a treasury of all blessings, it possesses the color of pure skin.

<note>The text here distinguishes between the "Vayu" (Wind) aura, which is seen as a physical and moral omen of decay, and the "Nabhastya" (Sky/Ether) aura, which represents clarity and spiritual success.</note>

The Wind-born shadow is of a dirty color, marked by roughness, and possesses a "sinful smell" <note>original: "papagandha"</note>—meaning it is malodorous. It generates or produces death, captivity, various pains or diseases, "un-profit" (misfortune), and the destruction of accumulated wealth.

Now he describes the characteristics of the Sky-born shadow: **"Like crystal..."** The shadow born of the sky or space <note>original: "gaganauttha"</note> has a beauty and luster similar to a crystal gem; this means it is exceedingly pure. It is endowed with light, is "most noble"—meaning excessively generous—and is a treasury of all auspicious works; it is the giver of everything. Its color is that of clear skin.

### The Five and Twelve Shadows

Beyond these, he now speaks of the five shadows:

The shadows arising from Earth, Water, Fire, Wind, and Sky are described in sequence. Some [authorities] speak of twelve shadows according to their suitability: those belonging to the Sun, Vishnu, Indra, Yama, the Moon, and the Wind, according to their characteristics and results.

**Earth** (Prithivi), **Water** (Varuna), **Fire** (Agni/Hutashana), **Wind** (Anila/Vayu), and **Sky** (Ambara/Akasha)—the shadows produced from these follow a specific order. However, some teachers, through different applications, speak of twelve shadows. These are also described in a corresponding order as being related to:
*   **The Sun** (Surya)
*   **The Lotus-Navelled One** (Anjanabha/Vishnu)
*   **The One Invoked by Many** (Puruhuta/Indra)
*   **The Lord of the Ancestors** (Yama)
*   **The Lord of the Stars** (Udupa/The Moon)

These shadows, by their characteristics and fruits, are similar to those previously mentioned. Specifically: the Solar shadow is like the Fiery one; the Vishnu shadow is like the Ethereal one; the Indra shadow is like the Earthly one; the Yama shadow is like the Windy one; and the Lunar shadow is like the Watery one. Because of this similarity in results, a summary of these ten (or twelve) shadows is made, and only five primary shadows are established.

As the Sage **Garga** says:
*The five shadows are celebrated as being composed of Earth, Water, Fire, Wind, and Sky; others belong to the Sun, Moon, and the rest.*

<margin>252</margin>

### Characteristics of the Voice

Now he describes the characteristics of the **Voice** <term>Svara: the sound, tone, or resonance of a person's speech</term>:

Those whose voices resemble an **elephant**, a **bull**, the **clatter of a chariot**, a **kettledrum**, a **mridanga drum**, or a **lion** become kings. Those with voices like a **donkey** or a **broken pot** are joined with poverty.

**Elephant** (Kari), **Bull** (Vrisha), **Chariot-noise** (Rathaghosha), **Kettledrum** (Bheri), **Mridanga** (a percussion instrument known as a Mandala), **Lion** (Simha/Hari), or a **Cloud** (Megha)—those who have voices similar to these sounds become kings. A **Donkey** (Gardabha) or a **Broken/Jarring** (Jarjara) voice is harsh...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of physiognomy and bodily constitutions, likely following a section on vocal qualities and leading into the specific "essences" (saras) of the human body.</meta>

Those endowed with these ten lower-range vocal qualities are like the gods who have conquered all through their wealth. <note>The text compares a deep, resonant voice to divine success.</note> And so says Garga <note>An ancient sage and authority on astrology and omens</note>: "A voice like a deep drum, or the resonance of a great white conch shell," these are the five excellent qualities. One who understands these principles attains a vast ocean of results: long life, fame, knowledge, honor, mental happiness, and the arrival of wealth; children, objects of desire, a kingdom, and enjoyments. Conversely, sounds like those of a crow, a vulture, a camel, or a donkey are described as "bad dreams" or ill-omened. One who speaks with these despised tones becomes filled with greed, delusion, and darkness, exhibiting pride, harshness, and deceit. This is the nature of the bitter voice.

### Now, the Description of the Essences (Saras)

In this regard, there are the "essences": fat, marrow, skin, bone, semen, blood, and flesh. These sustain the life-force of the body. Here is the summary of their results. There are seven essences of men. They are as follows: **Fat** <term>Meda: adipose tissue</term>; **Marrow** <term>Majjā: the oily substance within the bones and skull</term>; **Skin** <term>Tvak</term>; **Bone** <term>Asthi</term>; **Semen** <term>Śukra: reproductive essence</term>; **Blood** <term>Rudhira</term>; and **Flesh** <term>Māṃsa</term>. These seven types of essences sustain the life of embodied beings. Their effects are now stated in brief. First, the characteristics of the "Skin Essence" are described:

The palate, lips, gums, tongue, eyes, anus, hands, and feet. If these are glossy and reddish, the person is endowed with many pleasures, wives, wealth, and sons. <margin>The palate is the roof of the mouth; the gums are the rows of flesh around the teeth; the tongue is the organ of taste; the eyes refer to the corners of the vision.</margin> Those whose skin is lustrous and reddish are fortunate; they are joined with many comforts, women, great riches, and many sons.

### Now, the Essences of Skin, Fat, and Marrow

Those with firm skin are wealthy; those with glossy skin are fortunate and wise; those with thin skin are learned. Those with the essence of marrow and fat have beautiful bodies and are endowed with sons and wealth. The skin is well-known. Those with skin that is firm and glossy at the edges are wealthy and thoughtful. Those with beautiful, oily skin possess good fortune. Those with thin skin are scholars. Those men who possess the essence of marrow and the essence of fat have well-formed bodies; they are characterized by being accompanied by sons and riches. <note>The text repeats these descriptions slightly to emphasize the link between physical luster and material prosperity.</note>

### Now, the Essences of Bone and Semen

One with the essence of bone has large and steady bones; he is powerful, learned, and reaches the end of knowledge. Those with the essence of semen are very handsome, fortunate, wise, and beautiful. One whose bones are thick and firm is a "bone-essence" person; he is strong, a master of sciences, and possesses a handsome appearance. Those who have an abundance of "heavy" or potent semen are "semen-essence" types; they are endowed with great charm and luck, are learned scholars, and are physically attractive.

# Now, the characteristics of the essence of flesh and the characteristics of bodily compactness.
### One whose body is well-developed is to be known as having the "essence of flesh" <note>māṃsasāra: a constitutional type where muscle and flesh are predominant and healthy</note>. Such a person is learned, wealthy, and free from disease. One whose joints are firmly knit and well-integrated is known as "compact" <note>saṃhati</note>; such people are enjoyers of happiness. || 1 ||

He whose body is well-developed—meaning a stout or robust frame—possesses the essence of flesh. He is a scholar <note>paṇḍita</note>, possesses wealth, and is characterized by the absence of disease. Now, regarding the characteristics of compactness: "compactness" refers to the well-joined nature of all the limbs' joints. Those whose joints are perfectly knit are "enjoyers of happiness," meaning they live a comfortable life.

### Now, he describes the characteristics of glossiness <note>sneha: refers to a healthy oily luster or "glow" of the body</note>.
### Glossiness should be examined in five places: the voice, the tongue, the teeth, the eyes, and the nails. Those possessing glossiness in these areas are endowed with children, wealth, and good fortune. Those who are dull or "dry" <note>rukṣa: the opposite of glossy/oily</note> in these areas are destitute.

The quality of glossiness is to be considered in five parts: in the voice (speech), the tongue, the teeth, the eyes, and the nails. If these—the voice and so on—are all glossy and possessed of a fine luster, men will be endowed with sons, wealth, and "good fortune" (meaning they are beloved by all people). By these very qualities, they are joined with success. Conversely, those who are "dry" or lackluster in these same areas are poor and destitute.

### Now, he describes the characteristics of complexion <note>varṇa</note>.
### A glossy complexion belongs to kings; a medium one to those with children and wealth; a dry/dull one to those without wealth. A "small" <note>kṣudra: here meaning subtle or refined</note> complexion is auspicious if it is not mottled.

Complexions may be fair, reddish, dark, and so on. He whose complexion is "glossy" possesses a certain radiance or brilliance; such a complexion belongs to kings (rulers of the earth). A "medium" complexion—one that is neither overly bright nor overly dull—belongs to those who possess sons and wealth. A "dry" complexion belongs to the wealthless and the poor. A "small" or subtle complexion is auspicious and praiseworthy only if it is glossy and not "mixed" <note>saṃkīrṇa</note>; a complexion that is glossy in some spots and dry in others is not auspicious.

### Now, he describes the characteristics of the face <note>vaktra</note>.
### Those whose faces resemble a bull, a tiger, a lion, or the celestial eagle <note>Garuda</note> possess irresistible valor, conquer their enemies, and are lords among men. Those with faces like a monkey, a buffalo, a boar, or a goat are enjoyers of learning, wealth, and happiness. Those with faces resembling a donkey or a young camel are destitute and devoid of happiness.

From the appearance of the face, one's destiny is to be determined. A "bull" refers to a disciplined ox; a "tiger" <note>śārdūla</note> and "lion" <note>siṃha</note> are specific powerful animals; "Garuda" is the son of Vinata. Those whose faces resemble these are of the highest wisdom. They possess "irresistible valor"—meaning a power that cannot be overcome—and they are "conquerors of enemies" and "lords of men" (kings).
A "monkey" is an ape; the "buffalo" is well-known; the "boar" is a pig; the "goat" is an aja. Men with faces resembling these are "enjoyers of learning, wealth, and happiness"—meaning they possess sacred knowledge, riches, and the comforts of life.
Finally, the "donkey" is a jackass and the "young camel" <note>karabha</note> is an uṣṭra. With faces resembling these, living beings become "destitute"—meaning they lack wealth and are "devoid of happiness." For them, the wisdom-reading is inauspicious.

### Now, he describes the characteristics of height and weight <note>unmāna</note>.

# Page 2

The total measurement is one hundred and eight; eighty-four for men of superior, middling, and inferior stature, according to the count of their own <term>fingers (angulas)</term> <note>An 'angula' is a traditional unit of measurement based on the width of a finger; 'own-finger' means the measurement is relative to the individual's own body proportions.</note>. For men, the measurement by the count of their own fingers is as follows: one hundred and eight fingers is the measure for superior men; ninety-six <note>The text clarifies the calculation for 'middling' here</note> is the measure for middling men; and eighty-four fingers is the measure for inferior men. Now, the height of a man is determined from the soles of the feet to the top of the head; it is established that he should be measured by these finger-units.

## On Weight (Unmana)

Now, he describes the characteristics of weight:

One whose body weighs half a <term>bhara</term> <note>A 'bhara' is a heavy unit of weight, defined here as 2,000 palas; half a bhara is roughly 40-50kg depending on the historical region</note> enjoys happiness; one who weighs less than that is of a miserable nature. One who weighs more than a bhara is exceedingly wealthy. One who weighs one and a half bharas—that is, three thousand <term>palas</term>—is a ruler of the whole earth, a universal king.

At what time should one measure this weight? He explains:

A woman at twenty years of age and a man at twenty-five years. One should certainly measure this weight and height in the first fourth of the lifespan <note>Based on a theoretical 100-year life expectancy</note>. A woman is fit for this measurement at twenty years, and a man at twenty-five, assuming a hundred-year life.

## On Natural Temperaments (Prakriti)

Now he describes the characteristics of innate nature:

A man is formed by the essence of Earth, Water, Fire, Air, Ether, Gods, Humans, Demons, Spirits, and Animals. He now speaks of their characteristics.

"Earth" is well-known; "Water" is clear; "Fire" is the flame; "Air" is the wind; "Ether" is the sky. "Gods" are the celestials; "Humans" are men; "Demons" are the Rakshasas; "Spirits" are the Pisachas <note>A class of flesh-eating spirits in mythology</note>; "Animals" are the non-human species. A man's nature is determined by the <term>Sattva</term> <note>The inner quality or essence</note> of these, and the following are their characteristics. Some read the sequence differently <note>original: "bhū-jala-śikhy-anilāmbara..."</note>, but the previous reading is more elegant.

### Characteristics of Earth and Water Temperaments

The Earth-natured man has the scent of auspicious flowers; he is fond of enjoyment, happy, and stable. The Water-natured man drinks much water, speaks kindly, and is a lover of flavors.

The Earth-natured man <margin>Nature of Earth</margin> possesses a scent like that of beautiful flowers such as Champaka or Ashoka. He is stable in character. The Water-natured man <margin>Nature of Water</margin> drinks a great deal of water, speaks in a pleasing and agreeable manner, and is a "vessel of flavors"—meaning he is a lover of sweet and savory tastes. Some read "fond of eating" instead of "fond of enjoyment."

### Characteristics of Fire and Wind Temperaments

One of a Fire-nature is restless, has a very sharp appetite, is cruel, and... <note>The text breaks off here</note>

<summary>This page details the ideal physical proportions and weights for different classes of men and introduces the 'Prakriti' (elemental temperaments), describing how Earth and Water influences manifest in personality and scent.</summary>
<keywords>physiognomy, Samudrika Shastra, body measurements, angula, bhara, weight, temperaments, five elements, Earth nature, Water nature</keywords>

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of human temperaments (Sattvas) and physical constitutions (Prakritis) based on elemental and supernatural archetypes, likely following a section on water or earth-based types.</meta>

One who eats poor food and is of a windy <note>original: "vayu"; referring to the Vata dosha in Ayurvedic medicine</note> nature is solitary; the five-fold group <note>the senses or elements</note> comes under his control. A man of a fiery nature <note>original: "agni"; Pitta dosha</note> is restless, as if unsettled, extremely sharp, wicked, fierce, cruel, prone to anger, and a loud lamenter. He eats much and is gluttonous. A man of a windy nature is restless, of unstable mind, lean, weak, and falls under the power of anger and negligence; he becomes easily led.

### Now he describes the characteristics of the Ethereal temperament.
One of an ethereal nature <note>original: "akashasattva"; the element of space or ether</note> is clever, fond of solitude, skilled in the nuances of sound, has well-proportioned limbs, is generous, and possesses a mild temper. He is devoted to affection and lives comfortably. A man of ethereal nature is clever, always very skillful in various arts, and has a pleasant outlook. He is skilled in "sound-paths," meaning he understands the movement of refined Sanskrit words; he is compassionate and capable. Alternatively, he is skilled in the movement of the scriptures. Some say he is skilled in the movement of time <note>kalpa</note>, or very clever in the movement of music and song. He has well-formed limbs and a divine nature. Such a man is a giver, has little anger, and is full of love.

### Now he describes the characteristics of the Human temperament.
A man of human temperament <note>original: "purushasattva"</note> is fond of music, garlands, and perfumes, and is habitually generous. Such a person is always truly human. A man endowed with the human temperament loves songs and ornaments—ornaments meaning jewelry and decorations. He practices "sharing," meaning he is a benefactor to his kinsmen. He is virtuous in conduct and remains a true human being at all times.

### Now he describes the characteristics of Demons and Goblins.
Those with the temperament of Night-stalkers <note>original: "nishachara"; demons or Rakshasas</note> are sharp, easily provoked, and devoted to sin. One with a Goblin temperament <note>original: "pishacha"</note> is restless, dirty, talks excessively, and has a stout body. In the temperament of Rakshasas, a man is sharp in anger and delights in sin. A man of Goblin nature is restless with an unstable mind, has a body covered in impurities, prattles about many things, and has thick or heavy limbs.

### Now he describes the characteristics of the Animal temperament.
A man who is timid, irritable, gluttonous, and restless should be known as having the temperament of animals <note>original: "tiryaksattva"</note>. Thus, those who know the science of signs <note>physiognomy</note> declare the temperaments of men. A man who is timid, afflicted by fear, irritable, a loud lamenter, and a gluttonous eater is to be known by the temperament of lower animals. In this manner, the natures of men are described by those who know human characteristics. And so says the sage Garga: "Wealth and grain depend on color; the voice is more important than color; but the inner temperament is higher than the voice, for everything is established in the temperament." Nature and temperament are one.

### Now he describes the characteristics of Gait.
Those whose gait is like that of a tiger, a swan, an elephant in rut, or a bull become kings of the earth. Even if their own behavior is the cause of their progress, those who walk with a hurried or faltering gait remain poor.

<summary>This page categorizes human personalities and physical traits based on elemental natures (Fire, Wind, Ether), supernatural archetypes (Humans, Demons, Goblins), and animal temperaments, concluding with how a person's walk predicts their status.</summary>

--- Page 121 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary by Bhattotpala on Varahamihira's Brihat Samhita. It transitions from the general physical characteristics of men (Purushalakshana) to the specific astrological theory of the Five Great Men (Pancha Mahapurusha).</meta>

The tiger, the lion, and the swan <note>original: "hamsa"</note> are birds. Some read this as "tiger-lion." The tiger is a specific type of creature, famous in the world for its royal majesty; the lion is the "Hari," intoxicated with pride. "Intoxicated like a great elephant" is the meaning. The "lord of cows" refers to the bull, and the "crested one" refers to the peacock. By the gaits <note>the manner of walking</note> of these animals, one should judge: men who possess similar gaits become kings of the earth. Those whose walking is silent, steady, and slight are lords and wealthy. Those who are quick and leaping are poor; "quick and leaping" means jumping like a frog, which indicates those who are destitute and without wealth. Now, he states the established principle:

> For one who is weary, a vehicle; for one who is hungry, food; for one who is overcome by thirst, drink; and protection for one who is afraid. When these things come to a man at the proper time, those who know the signs of men call him truly blessed.

For a man who is weary or exhausted, a vehicle—such as a horse or similar—is provided. For one who is hungry, there is a meal. For one overcome by thirst, there is water to drink. For one in fear, there is protection and safety. When these are utilized exactly when needed, that man is fortunate and possesses auspicious signs; so say the experts in human characteristics <note>term: Purushavidah (those who understand the nature of men)</note>. || 6 ||

### Thus ends the sixty-fourth chapter, titled "The Characteristics of Men," in the Commentary on the Samhita composed by the venerable Bhattotpala. || 64 ||

### Now, the Classification of the Five Types of Men is explained.

At the very beginning, he speaks to demonstrate the purpose of starting this section.

<margin>I shall describe those men</margin>
<insert>When the planetary strengths are present, and planets are in their own signs or exaltation in the four corners (quadrants), the five types of excellent men are born; I shall describe those men.</insert>

The "star-planets" <note>term: Taragraha (the non-luminary planets)</note> are Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, and Saturn. When these star-planets are endowed with strength—possessing strength of position, direction, movement, and time—and are situated in their own signs or in their signs of exaltation, and are located in the first, fourth, seventh, or tenth houses <note>the "Kendras" or pillars of an astrological chart</note>, then the five types of excellent men are born. I shall now describe and explain them. Now, he gives the classification of their characteristics:

<insert>By Jupiter, the Swan (Hamsa) is formed; by Saturn, the Rabbit (Shasha); by Mars, the Radiant (Ruchaka); and by a strong Mercury, the Auspicious (Bhadra); and by the Priest of the Demons (Venus), the Garland-Bearer (Malavya).</insert>

By a strong Jupiter <note>original: "Jiva"</note>, the man named **Hamsa** is produced. Similarly, by a strong Saturn <note>original: "Sauri"</note>, the **Shasha** type is produced. By a strong Mars <note>original: "Kuja"</note>, the yoga <note>astrological combination</note> named **Ruchaka** occurs. By a strong Mercury, the **Bhadra** type occurs. By a strong Venus, the "Priest of the Demons," the **Malavya** type occurs. And so it is said in the *Taravali*:

> Whether in their own signs or in their signs of exaltation, one should observe the strong planets: Venus, Mars, Saturn, Jupiter, and Mercury. Respectively, these are said to produce the Malavya, Ruchaka, Shasha, Hamsa, and Bhadra types. The sages declare their characteristics, which lead to great wealth.

He explains the specific distinctions of these types based on the strength of the Sun and Moon in two verses. The purity of essence and lack of weariness of the mind depends on the Sun, while the physical body and the state of the mind depend on the strength of the Moon and the specific zodiac signs they occupy...

<summary>This page concludes the chapter on general physical omens (comparing human gaits to noble animals) and introduces the "Pancha Mahapurusha" system, where five specific planets create five types of extraordinary men when positioned strongly in a birth chart.</summary>
<keywords>Bhattotpala, Brihat Samhita, Physiognomy, Astrology, Pancha Mahapurusha, Hamsa Yoga, Shasha Yoga, Ruchaka Yoga, Bhadra Yoga, Malavya Yoga, Planetary Strength</keywords>

--- Page 122 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of human physiognomy and temperament as determined by planetary influences, specifically focusing on the "Sattva" (inner essence) and the "Mahabhuta" (great elements) associated with the planets.</meta>

<margin>255</margin>
The man is characterized by these traits. It is said that the nature of the Great Elements <note>Mahabhuta: Earth, Water, Fire, Air, and Ether</note> and their forms are determined by those planets endowed with pure strength; through them, a man of mixed characteristics is formed. Regarding the phrase "Sattva is not diminished in all": this means that the quality of *Sattva* <note>The quality of purity, light, and harmony</note> remains intact. For whomever the skin and other parts are influenced by the Sun, his body, qualities, weight, luster, and mind become significant according to the strength of the Sun and Moon. How is this so? It is said: through the divisions of the signs—such as the ninth part (Navamsha), the twelfth part (Dwadasamsha), or the thirtieth part (Trishamsha)—whichever planet like Mars and others is connected to that specific division of the sign, the lord of that division determines the characteristics. The man possesses those traits. These sign divisions are explained in the chapter on "Sign Divisions" in the birth manual (*Jataka*).

Whatever is said regarding the nature of the Great Elements in those sign divisions, the man is known by those characteristics. In that context, the bodily constituent (*Dhatu*) belongs to him. For example, the constituents are assigned as follows: the skin, blood, flesh, fat, bone, marrow, and semen are ruled by the Moon, Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, Saturn, and the Sun <note>The text lists the traditional Ayurvedic tissues/Dhatus and their planetary rulers</note>. The Great Elements—Earth and the others—are said to be caused by Mars and the other planets. As it is said: "The lords of the qualities of Earth, Water, Fire, Air, and Ether are the sons of Earth (Mars) and the others in sequence."

Thus, he becomes endowed with the nature of that Great Element. As it was stated at the beginning: "A high-minded disposition, the scent of auspicious flowers," and so on. Luster refers to the complexion; for instance, the Sun is described as "reddish-dark." The portion of the *Sattva* quality provides the authority for his characteristics, because when prosperity arrives, <insert>well-featured</insert> men are like kings. Regarding the phrase "having eyes honey-colored like a hornet," and so on: by including "and so on," it refers to natural skill. Through these various strengths and powers, or by being joined with the Sun and others lacking strength, men of mixed or unmixed characteristics are produced. This is what is meant: whichever of the Moon or Sun is stronger in the specific sign division, the man takes on those characteristics. If both are equally strong, then he possesses a mixture of characteristics from where the Sun and Moon are situated.

Now, why does one have such characteristics? This is explained in the following pair of verses:
"From Mars comes the *Sattva* (inner strength); from Mercury comes the weight and the voice; from Venus comes the oiliness (suppleness) and complexion; from Saturn comes the constituent parts. Through their virtues or defects, goodness or badness arises."
For these men, *Sattva* arises from Mars. By the word "weight," it implies the quality of gravity or dignity from Mercury. From Jupiter comes the voice or sound. From Venus comes luster and oiliness. From Saturn comes the complexion. Does this happen always? No, it says: "Goodness or badness arises through virtues or defects." The excellence of *Sattva* and other qualities occurs when the planets are strong; through defects or weak strength, they are not good, and one becomes devoid of *Sattva* and falls into sin.
And so it is said in the *Saravali* <note>A classic 8th-century Sanskrit text on astrology by Kalyanavarman</note>: "From the son of Earth (Mars) comes noble *Sattva*; from the son of the Moon (Mercury) comes weight; from Jupiter comes the voice; from Venus comes oiliness..."

<summary>This page discusses how a person's physical and psychological traits are determined by the strength of planets within specific zodiacal divisions, referencing the Saravali. It links the five Great Elements and the seven bodily tissues to planetary rulers to explain human temperament.</summary>

Dull-witted, highly intelligent, perverse, striking, a copper-colored poet, red-eyed, generous, prone to transformation, fond of wandering; he is also known by the low name <note>original: "jaghanyanāmā"</note> or another reading is "the foot."
A follower who always, at all times, moves like a cloud. He is marked on his chest, feet, and hands with a sword, a noose, and a serpent <note>original: "pāśaparisrapāṅkaiḥ"</note>. On the chest (the breast), the feet, and the hands (the palms), there is a sword—a specific weapon of Indra—a noose, a serpent, or perhaps the word "noose" itself, or a club-like weapon. He is marked or characterized by these.

Now, he describes the **Characteristics of the Cruel Type** <note>original: "krūralakṣaṇa"</note> in two verses.
(A pair of verses):
The one named Cruel is pure in his lower parts, slightly bent in the forepart of his body, a follower of the "Swan" type, an atheist, endowed with wealth, learned, and fleeting. He is a slanderer, yet generous and grateful.
He is an expert in the essence of the arts, fond of quarreling, has a pleasant livelihood, and is conquered by women. In the world of names, he suddenly abandons his path; he is possessed by anger and is constantly striving.
"The one named Cruel" means: his lower part is pure and full. In the forepart of his body, specifically the upper part, he is slightly thin and somewhat bent. A "follower of the Swan" means he is an attendant to the Swan type <note>The "Swan" or Haṃsa is often considered the highest category of man in Indian physiognomy</note>. An "atheist" means he is devoid of ritual actions, mostly following the Materialist <note>original: "lokāyatika"</note> school. He is "endowed with wealth" and is a "learned scholar." He is fond of battle. A "slanderer" means he is a backbiter. He is "generous" and "grateful," meaning he acknowledges favors.
"Expert in the arts" means he is skilled in the arts of singing, dancing, and instrumental music; he knows them thoroughly. He is "fond of quarreling" and has a "pleasant livelihood," meaning he has many means of living. "Conquered by women" means he is dominated by females. Having honored or served people, he suddenly abandons them like a lightning bolt. This "Cruel" type is passionate, "constantly striving," meaning he is enthusiastic at all times. Another reading for this is "the remaining principal part." (The pair of verses ends).

Now, he describes the **Characteristics of the Mandaka Type** <note>original: "maṇḍakalakṣaṇa"</note> in two verses.
The characteristics of the Mandaka: he is a follower of the "Ruchaka" type, an expert in magic spells <term>Abhicāra: incantations used for malevolent purposes or controlling others</term>, and is devoted to the rituals of the Kritya and the Vetala <note>original: "vetāla"; spirits or vampires inhabiting corpses</note> and the various sciences.
He has the appearance of an old man, possesses excellent eyelashes, and is skilled in destroying enemies. His intellect is attached to the worship of the Twice-born <note>original: "dvija"; Brahmins</note>, the Gods, and sacrifices, as well as to Yoga. He is conquered by women and is possessed of pride.
"The characteristics of the Mandaka": after the "Cruel" type comes the "Mandaka" type. What is he like? He is a "follower of the Ruchaka," meaning he lives by serving the Ruchaka type. "Expert in magic" means he knows the practices of driving others away to foreign lands, or subjugation <note>original: "vaśīkaraṇa"</note>. He is "skilled" or capable in the rituals of the Vetala and others—the word "others" refers to actions mentioned in the scriptures—and is devoted to those scriptures. "Appearance of an old man" means his body looks like that of an elderly person. "Excellent eyelashes" means he has thick, firm lashes or hair born of the sun. He is "expert in destroying enemies," meaning he is capable and powerful in battle. His "intellect is attached" to the Twice-born (Brahmins), the Gods (deities), sacrifices (offerings), and to "Yoga," meaning the systems of philosophy such as Samkhya. Another reading for this is "always joined with the sound of the lute."

<summary>This page details the physical and behavioral traits of three human archetypes: the follower of the cloud, the "Cruel" (Krūra) type, and the "Mandaka" type, including their skills in arts, magic, and social standing.</summary>

A man conquered by women <note>original: "streejitah"</note> may still be wise and intelligent. Now, the characteristics of the **Hare-type** <note>Sasha: one of the five types of "Great Men" in Indian physiognomy</note> man are described:

He who walks with a slight slant, whose body may not be conventionally handsome, but who follows the teachings of the scriptures <note>original: "shashanugami" interpreted here as following the Shastras</note> and acts as a faithful attendant—this is a stylistic ornament of the sentence—though he may be unpopular among common folk, he is a giver by nature. He undertakes great tasks and sees them through to completion. In these qualities, he is equal to the Hare-type man described previously.

### On the Efficacy of the Science of Man

Regarding the characteristics of men, I have stated this by observing the opinions of the ancient Sages <note>Rishis</note> in a summarized form. By studying this, a man becomes favored by kings and beloved by all people. Since the ancient teachers promised that in the science of stars <term>Jyotishastra</term> <note>the traditional Indian system of astronomy and astrology</note>, it is not proper for us to rely solely on our own doubts when there is a conflict in the traditional texts; rather, one should speak the consensus of many. Having reflected thus, I have stated the characteristics of men based on all traditional knowledge, as I have seen and considered it.

Thus ends the sixty-ninth chapter, titled "The Characteristics of the Attendants of the Five Great Men," in the *Commentary on the Compendium* <note>referring to the Brihat Samhita</note> composed by **Bhattotpala**.

### The Chapter on the Characteristics of Maidens

Now begins the chapter on the characteristics of maidens <note>Kanya-lakshana</note>. First, the characteristics of the feet:

If a young maiden’s feet are well-arched like the back of a tortoise, with copper-colored nails, and are even, fleshy, and beautiful; if the ankles are hidden <note>not bony or protruding</note> and the toes are closely set together; and if the soles possess the luster of a lotus—such a woman, if she marries a lord of the earth, will elevate him to great heights.

Furthermore, the characteristics of the feet, shanks, knees, and thighs are described:

Feet marked with signs like the fish, the elephant goad, the lotus, or barley corn are praised, especially if they do not sweat and have no visible veins. The shanks should be hairless, without prominent veins, and well-rounded. The two knees should be smooth, without protruding joints at the point of union.

<summary>This page concludes the discussion on male physiognomy (specifically the Hare-type man) and begins the section on "Kanya-lakshana," detailing the auspicious physical traits of women, starting with the feet and legs.</summary>
<keywords>physiognomy, Bhattotpala, Brihat Samhita, Sasha-purusha, women's characteristics, auspicious signs, Indian astrology, Kanya-lakshana</keywords>

--- Page 125 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of physiognomy (the study of physical features to determine character or fortune), specifically focusing on the lower body and torso of women. It follows the traditional Sanskrit "Samudrika Shastra" (the science of body marks).</meta>

<warning>Water staining and minor ink bleeding along the top and left edges.</warning>

The thighs and hands should resemble the trunk of an elephant; the hair should be fine like the leaf of a sacred fig tree <note>original: *ashvattha*; Ficus religiosa</note>; and the ankles should be broad. A waist and forehead that are elevated like the shell of a tortoise, and are hairless and broad, bestow great fortune. Regarding the marks of the fish and the elephant-goad: the fish is a well-known symbol, as are the goad, the wheel <note>Chakra</note>, the lotus, the barley grain, the thunderbolt weapon <note>Vajra</note>, the plow, and the sword. Feet marked with these signs, which are free from sweat and have soft soles, are considered excellent. As stated in the *Samudra* <note>The Samudrika Shastra, a foundational text on physiognomy</note>:

> The nails of women should be glossy and copper-colored; the tops of the feet should be well-reddened, the ankles hidden (fleshy), and the toes thick and closely set. Feet marked with the fish, goad, barley, lotus, plow, thunderbolt, and sword, which are pleasant to the touch and hairless, bring wealth and are auspicious.

The opposite of these traits leads to sorrow and poverty. A maiden whose feet are bowed or overly raised is not ideal. The shanks <note>lower legs</note> should be hairless and radiant; they should be without visible veins <note>original: *vishire*</note> and perfectly rounded. The pair of knees should be equal, neither too high nor too low, and well-fleshed so the bone is not prominent. The thighs should be continuous and resemble the trunk of an elephant <note>term: *karikara-pratibhau* — resembling the graceful, tapering strength of an elephant's trunk</note>, hairless, and broad like the leaf of the sacred fig. The forehead should be wide and elevated like the back of a tortoise. Such a woman bestows great prosperity. As stated in the *Samudra*:

> Shanks that are hairless, without visible veins, and well-rounded; knees that are not bony and are free of hair; thighs that resemble the trunk of an elephant, are pleasant to the touch, continuous, and even; a pubic mound <note>original: *jaghana*</note> that is broad and shaped like a tortoise shell—these are auspicious. When the waist is well-formed and the buttocks are broad and lovely, and the navel region is deep, that woman shall possess great wealth.

### On the Characteristics of the Hips and Navel

A woman whose hips are broad, well-fleshed, and heavy enough to support a girdle <note>term: *rashanakalapa* — an ornamental multi-stringed belt or zone</note> is praised. The navel should be deep, wide, and spiraling to the right <note>clockwise</note>. The "hips" refer to the waist region; it should be expansive, muscular, and possess a dignified weightiness to carry the expanse of the jeweled girdle. As it is said:

> She whose pelvic region is broad, soft to the touch, hairless, and has the luster of gold, shall be worthy of a kingdom.

Regarding the navel: for women, a navel that is deep, wide, and turns in a rightward spiral is considered excellent. As it is said:

> A deep, wide navel that follows a rightward spiral is auspicious; the opposite—especially a leftward spiral—is not desired.

### On the Characteristics of the Waist, Breasts, Belly, and Neck

<meta>This page continues the physiognomic analysis of women's physical traits, following the tradition of Samudrika Shastra (the science of body marks). It focuses on the torso, neck, and facial features.</meta>

# Page 261

<warning>Significant water damage/staining across the center and right side of the page; ink is blurred or faded in several areas. Edges are frayed.</warning>

A navel with three folds, hairless, and a circular chest; breasts that are firm, even, and hard. A chest free of hair on beautiful women, and a neck marked like a conch shell, bestows great happiness.

Regarding women: in the middle part of the body, the navel should possess three folds <note>original: "trivali"; these are considered auspicious beauty marks in classical Indian aesthetics</note>, meaning it is situated within three distinct creases and is hairless. As it is said:

> She whose middle is marked by three folds, is pleasant to the touch, and devoid of hair, is undoubtedly a royal queen <note>original: "Rajamahishi"</note>.

Regarding the breasts: they should be very firm, circular, dense, continuous, even, and hard. As it is said:

> Breasts that are well-rounded, heavy, continuous, firm, hairless, and not drooping, bestow the happiness of sons and wealth.

Regarding the hairless chest: the chest <note>original: "uras"</note> of women should be hairless, soft to the touch, and auspicious. As it is said:

> A chest without hair, soft to the touch, and broad, gives happiness to the husband. One that is veined, uneven, or hairy brings sorrow and barrenness.

Regarding the neck: a neck <note>original: "griva"</note> marked with three lines like a conch shell <note>original: "kambu-nibhita"; a 'conch-neck' is a classical sign of beauty</note> bestows happiness. As the sage Garga says:

> A neck that is firm, marked with auspicious lines, well-fleshed, well-rounded, not too long, measuring four fingers in width, and elegant, brings great prosperity.

### Now, the characteristics of the lower lip and teeth are described:

A lower lip like the Bandhujiva flower, fleshy, and resembling the bright Bimba fruit; teeth like jasmine buds, even and white, give a woman's husband immeasurable happiness. The lower lip <note>original: "adhara"</note> should resemble the Bandhujiva flower <note>a bright red flower, Pentapetes phoenicea</note>, meaning it is very red; it should be fleshy and possess the luster of the Bimba fruit <note>a bright red gourd often used to describe ideal lips</note>. As it is said:

> She whose lower lip resembles the Bimba fruit, is fleshy and soft, is undoubtedly a royal queen, even if she is currently a maiden.

Regarding the teeth: the teeth <note>original: "dvija", literally 'twice-born'</note> should resemble jasmine buds <note>original: "kunda-kudmala"</note>. Jasmine is a specific flower, and its buds are its unblossomed state. Women whose teeth are even and like these buds possess immeasurable happiness with their husbands and great wealth. As Garga says:

> Teeth that are sharp, rounded, very even, firm, and have the color of a bright lotus stalk or the moon, and are closely set, are like silver in their brilliance.

And as Samudra <note>the mythical founder of this science</note> says:

> She who has thirty-two teeth, all as white as cow's milk and pointed like peaks, shall become the wife of a king.

### Now, the characteristics of the voice, nose, and eyes are described:

Speech that is polite, honest, sweet like a cuckoo, and gentle brings great happiness. A nose that is straight, with even nostrils, is beautiful and praised; eyes that have the luster of a blue lotus petal. The speech <note>original: "prabhashitam"</note> should be endowed with politeness, favorable, and free from deceit <note>original: "ashatham"</note>.

It cites authorities like Garga and Samudra to validate these physiognomic standards.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of feminine physical traits (Samudrika Shastra), moving from the quality of the voice and facial features to the characteristics of the hair, head, and the auspicious marks found on the hands and feet.</meta>

If a woman’s voice is like that of a <term>Kokila</term><note>The Indian Cuckoo, prized for its melodic song</note>, or like the pleasant sound of a <term>Valgu</term><note>A species of bird known for a sweet warble</note>, and if it is naturally resonant and not strained, it brings great happiness and immense wealth. 

**As the Sage Garga says:**
"Those women whose voices resemble the swan, the trumpet, the deep resonance of a drum, the thunder of a cloud, the sound of a conch shell, or the sweet tone of the lute and the cuckoo—these women are praised. Even those with voices like the <term>Chakra</term><note>The Ruddy Shelduck</note> or the tinkling of small bells are auspicious. Such women possess wealth and sons, are devoted to virtue, and are self-controlled."

Regarding the nose: a nose that is straight, symmetrical, and well-rounded is a giver of good fortune. Regarding the eyes: a gaze that has the luster of a blue lotus is highly praised. The eyebrows should not be too thick or bushy; they are best when shaped like the young crescent moon. 

The forehead should be shaped like a half-moon, hairless, and neither too sunken nor too protruding. The eyebrows should not meet in the middle. If the eyebrows are too thick or hang low, one should predict a difficult life for that woman. 

**As it is said:**
"A forehead that is neither too high nor too low, free of visible veins and hair, and shaped like the half-moon is considered excellent by the wise."

## On the Characteristics of the Ears and Hair

The ears should be fleshy and well-formed, soft, symmetrical, and firmly attached. 

**As it is said:**
"Ears that are not too long, resemble a pair, are well-attached and fleshy, belong to the wife of a king; of this, there is no doubt."

The hair (<term>Murdhaja</term>) should be glossy, pure black, soft, and curling. Each hair should ideally grow from a single pore; such hair brings happiness. 

**As it is said:**
"Glossy, blue-black, soft, and curling hair is always praised in women, as it bestows wealth and sons."

The head should be level—neither sunken nor too high—to bring happiness.

## On the Characteristics of the Hands and Feet

If the following signs are found on the soles of the feet or the palms of the hands, the woman attains the highest status:
A <term>Bhringara</term><note>A ceremonial water pitcher</note>, a throne, a horse, an elephant, a chariot, a <term>Shrivatsa</term><note>An auspicious curl or mark of Vishnu</note>, a sacrificial pillar, an arrow, a garland, earrings, a fly-whisk, a goad, a grain of barley, a mountain, a flag, a lance, a fish, a <term>Swastika</term><note>An ancient symbol of well-being</note>, an altar, a parasol, or a lotus. 

The <term>Bhringara</term> is a well-known water vessel; the <term>Asana</term> is a seat or throne; the <term>Vaji</term> is a horse; the <term>Kunjara</term> is an elephant; the <term>Shrivatsa</term> is the Bilva tree mark; the <term>Yupa</term> is the sacrificial post; and the <term>Ishu</term> is an arrow. Along with these marks, the garland, earrings, and the <term>Chamara</term><note>A fly-whisk used in royal ceremonies</note>...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Samudrika Shastra (physiognomy), specifically focusing on the auspicious marks found on the hands and feet of women that indicate royal status and prosperity.</meta>

The **Ankusha** <note>original: "Ankusha"; an elephant goad</note> is well-known; the **Barley-grain** <note>original: "Yava"</note> is shaped like a grain of barley. By these marks, as well as the **Mountain** <note>original: "Shaila"</note>, the **Banner** <note>original: "Dhvaja"</note> in the form of a flag, and the **Archway** <note>original: "Torana"</note> made of many cloths—which is also well-known—and likewise the **Fish** <note>original: "Matsya"</note>, the **Swastika** <note>original: "Svastika"</note> (a specific auspicious symbol), and the **Altar** <note>original: "Vedika"</note> which is square in the middle. Furthermore, the **Fan** <note>original: "Vyajanaka"</note> made of palm leaves, and the **Conch** <note>original: "Shankha"</note> which is famous. The **Parasol** <note>original: "Atapatra"</note> or umbrella, and the **Lotus** <note>original: "Ambuja" or "Padma"</note>. By these beautiful signs, if they are located on the soles of the feet or the palms of the hands, women attain a steady and high position, like a row of female elephants <note>interpretive: this implies a majestic and graceful gait or status</note>.

And so says **Garga** <note>An ancient sage and authority on astrology and omens</note>:
"The Man, the Ocean, the Earth, Wealth, the Bull, the Archway of the people, the Water-pot, the Lotus, the Elephant-goad, the Lion, the Barley-grain, the Swastika, or even a Pond. The Tortoise, the Banner, the Sea-monster <note>original: "Makara"</note>, the Male figure, the Staff, and the River of pleasant water. If these marks are found on the hands, whether one, two, or many, they indicate extreme happiness and many sons for women; such are the characteristics described."

Whosoever has on her palm a **Parasol**, a **Tortoise**, or a **Banner**; or the **Shrivatsa** <note>An auspicious curl of hair or mark associated with Vishnu</note>, a **Lotus**, a **Conch**, a **Mind-symbol**, or a **Chowrie** <note>original: "Chamara"; a fly-whisk used as a royal insignia</note>. If an **Elephant-goad** and a **Garland** are seen on her hand, she will give birth to a single son who will be a King, a lord of the earth. She on whose palm is seen a **Storehouse** <note>original: "Kosthagara"</note> with **Archways**, even if she is born into a family of servants, she shall become a **Queen** <note>original: "Rajamahishi"</note>.

**Now, the characteristics of the hand are described:**
Hands with hidden wrist-joints, resembling the interior of a young lotus, belong to the wives of kings; they have thin fingers with well-joined joints. A palm that is neither too sunken nor too raised, and is marked with beautiful lines, ensures a woman will never be a widow, but will long enjoy sons, happiness, and wealth.

"Hands" refers to the two hands. "Hidden wrist-joints" <note>original: "Nigudha-manibandhau"</note> means the wrists or forearms are well-fleshed and not bony, where the root of the hand begins. "Resembling the interior of a young lotus" means they have the inner glow and color of a fresh, newly opened lotus flower. "Thin fingers with well-joined joints" means the fingers are slender and the segments <note>original: "Parva"</note> are long and smooth, not knotted. Such hands belong to the wives of kings. The palm should be neither too low (sunken) nor too high (bulging), and endowed with "Beautiful lines" <note>original: "Surekha"</note>, meaning auspicious and clear markings. This brings about a state of being "not a widow" (joined with her husband for a long time) and one who enjoys the pleasure of children and wealth.

As it is said:
"With hidden wrist-joints, glowing like a young lotus, with smooth finger-joints—such are the hands of a king's wife. Neither high nor low, even and well-marked with beautiful lines; the woman who possesses such hands is remembered as a Queen."

Furthermore:
If a line rises from the wrist and goes to the middle finger on a woman's palm, or if marks like the Barley-grain are situated upward on the palm, it leads to royal happiness for men or women alike.

### On the Signs of Women and Men

In the case of women, if a line rises from the <term>Manibandha</term><note>The wrist-base or "bracelet" lines</note> on the palm of the hand and goes upward until it reaches the middle finger; or likewise, if such a line exists on the sole of the left foot—and for a man, on the right foot—such a person shall enjoy the pleasures of sovereignty. This means it brings the joy of ruling a kingdom. A maiden who possesses a line rising from the wrist and resting upon the middle finger is destined to share in royal power.

#### On the Line of Longevity

Now, regarding the gift of life: a line that originates at the base of the <term>Kanishthika</term><note>The little finger</note> and passes through the interval between the <term>Pradeshini</term><note>The forefinger or index finger</note> and the <term>Madhyama</term><note>The middle finger</note> indicates the maximum lifespan. 

That line which arises from the root of the little finger and reaches the space between the finger near the thumb (the index) and the middle finger, grants the measure of a "supreme life." This means the person lives for one hundred and twenty years. If the line is broken or raised, the life is measured accordingly; one must use their own intelligence to infer the result based on the quality of the lines. As it is said: *“She whose line starts at the base of the little finger and ends between the index and middle fingers is long-lived.”*

#### On the Knowledge of Progeny

Now he speaks of the knowledge of offspring:
At the base of the thumb (<term>Angusthamula</term>) are the lines of procreation. The thick lines indicate sons, while the thin ones indicate women (daughters). Those that are unbroken in the middle signify long life. <gloss>Heart-line <unclear>...</unclear> remaining</gloss> Those of small proportion signify short life.

At the base of the thumb, there are as many lines of procreation as there will be births. Among them, the lines that are well-formed and thick are sons; those that are slender and fine are daughters. Those lines which are unbroken in the middle—meaning they are clear and continuous—should be known as "increased life," signifying they will be long-lived. Some say that those which are "cut" or broken in the middle, or are of very small measure, should be known as having a very short lifespan. 

As it is said: *“The thick lines at the base of the thumb are considered sons; the thin ones are daughters. Those that are disconnected among them are short-lived.”* A wise man should examine these signs on the **left hand** for women and on the **right hand** for men, according to the words of the sages.

#### On Inauspicious Signs

Having thus spoken of the auspicious signs (<term>Shubha-lakshana</term>), he now says: "I shall speak of the inauspicious signs (<term>Ashubha-lakshana</term>)."

Thus, the auspicious signs for women have been described. That which is the opposite of this is called "undesirable." I shall now proclaim in summary those signs which are especially fruitless or produce bad results. 

First, he describes the inauspicious signs of the feet:
If a woman's little finger, or the one next to it, does not touch the ground while she walks, or if it overrides the thumb... <note>The text cuts off here, describing a gait or toe-alignment thought to be an unlucky omen in traditional physiognomy.</note>
